. This gives

\displaystyle\sup_{t,v\in[0,1]^{2}}\Big(\nu_{t,v}\big((R_{1,1}-\rho_{n,d})^{2}\big)^{1/2}+\nu_{t,v}\big((R_{1,2}-q_{n,d})^{2}\big)^{1/2}\Big)\leq K(D,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\kappa,\lambda)\,.

We thus can bound

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}v}\varphi(v)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq K(D,\lambda)\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}+K(D,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\kappa,\lambda)\Big(\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}+\frac{1}{d}\Big)\,,

which as remarked earlier is sufficient to conclude Proposition
A.4
, since
d\geq\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{\prime\prime 2}}
.
∎

A.2
Simplifying the expression from cavity in
n

Again let
\alpha=n/d
, and let
\psi_{\alpha},\bar{\psi}_{\alpha}
be defined in terms of
\alpha
as per (
A.20
), (
A.21
).
Our goal here in Appendix
A.2
is to show that the expression from Proposition
A.4
, specifically

\displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\log\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\exp u\big(xs_{n}+\sqrt{q_{n,d}}Z+\sqrt{\rho_{n,d}-q_{n,d}}W\big)\Big]\,,

(A.29)

is very close to
\textsc{RSG}_{I}
. The difference between (
A.29
) and
\textsc{RSG}_{I}
is that
\textsc{RSG}_{I}
instead depends on
\rho_{0},q_{0}
, and additionally for the interpolators, the mollifier
u(\cdot)
in (
A.29
) is replaced by the hard step function
\mathbf{1}\{x\geq\kappa\}
in
\textsc{RSG}_{I}
.

Here we will do the work in this argument that is common to the proof for the interpolators and posterior, specifically Propositions
A.9
,
A.10
,
A.11
.
The additional work for the interpolators that is needed to replace the mollifier
u(\cdot)
in (
A.29
) by the hard step function
\mathbf{1}\{x\geq\kappa\}
in
\textsc{RSG}_{I}
is done in Propositions

A.14
and
A.13
, presented later in Appendix
A.3
.

Beginning with the argument, consider the following system of four variables and four equations:

\displaystyle r=\psi_{\alpha}(q,\rho)\,,\,\bar{r}=\bar{\psi}_{\alpha}(q,\rho)\,,\,\rho=\frac{1}{2\beta+r-\bar{r}}+\frac{r}{(2\beta+r-\bar{r})^{2}}\,,\,q=\frac{r}{(2\beta+r-\bar{r})^{2}}\,.

(A.30)

We will establish that
\rho_{n,d},q_{n,d}
approximately satisfy (
A.30
). This is where we use the careful choice of
r,\bar{r}
in terms of
\psi_{\alpha},\bar{\psi}_{\alpha}
from (
A.22
), (
A.23
), which lets us complete the cavity in
n
argument. In particular, in Appendix
D.2
, we will complete the cavity in
n
and also execute the cavity in
d
to establish the following.

Lemma A.8
.

For all
t\in[0,1]
, we have

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\nu_{t}\big(\theta_{d}^{1}\theta_{d}^{2}\big)\Big|\,,\,\Big|\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\nu_{t}\big((\theta_{d}^{1})^{2}\big)\Big|\leq K\Big(\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\Big)\,,

(A.31)

where
K
depends on
D,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\lambda
.

With Lemma
A.8
in hand, we now establish:

Proposition A.9
.

The following system of equations is satisfied by
\rho_{n,d},q_{n,d}
:

\displaystyle r=\psi_{\alpha}(q,\rho)\,,\,\bar{r}=\bar{\psi}_{\alpha}(q,\rho)\,,\,\rho=\frac{1}{2\beta+r-\bar{r}}+\frac{r}{(2\beta+r-\bar{r})^{2}}+\delta_{1}\,,\,q=\frac{r}{(2\beta+r-\bar{r})^{2}}+\delta_{2}\,,

(A.32)

where

\displaystyle\big|\delta_{1}\big|\,,\,\big|\delta_{2}\big|\leq K_{\textup{\ref{prop:approxrealRSequations}}}\Big(\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\Big)\,,

(A.33)

for
K_{\textup{\ref{prop:approxrealRSequations}}}
depending on
D,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\lambda
. Here the errors
|\delta_{1}|,|\delta_{2}|
are upper bounded by a quantity independent of
\alpha=\frac{n}{d}
.
1
1

1

The upper bound only depends on
\alpha_{0}
and is monotonically increasing, so we can apply the bound
\alpha_{0}\leq 2
.

Moreover, as noted earlier, we have
r\geq\bar{r}
.

Proof of Proposition
A.9
.

Consider
\nu_{1}(R_{1,1}),\nu_{1}(R_{1,2})
. By symmetry between coordinates,

\nu_{1}(R_{1,1})=\nu_{1}\big((\theta_{d}^{1})^{2}\big)\,,\,\nu_{1}(R_{1,1})=\nu_{1}\big(\theta_{d}^{1}\theta_{d}^{2}\big)\,.

Next observe that for the measure
\nu_{0}
, the
d
-th coordinate completely decouples from the others. Moreover, the restriction
\mathbf{1}\{\theta_{1}/\sqrt{d}\in I\}
only concerns the first coordinate
\theta_{1}
. Thus, letting
Z_{t}
denote the relevant normalizing constant, we have

\displaystyle\nu_{0}\big((\theta_{d}^{1})^{2}\big)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\frac{\int\theta_{d}^{2}\mathbf{1}\{\theta_{1}/\sqrt{d}\in I\}\exp\Big(\sum_{i\leq n}u\big(S_{i,0}(\theta)\big)-\beta\|\theta\|^{2}+\theta_{d}\sqrt{r}Y-\frac{r-\bar{r}}{2}\theta_{d}^{2}\Big)\mathrm{d}\theta}{Z_{t}}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\frac{\int\theta_{d}^{2}\exp\Big(\theta_{d}\sqrt{r}Y-\frac{2\beta+r-\bar{r}}{2}\theta_{d}^{2}\Big)\mathrm{d}\theta_{d}}{\int\exp\Big(\theta_{d}\sqrt{r}Y-\frac{2\beta+r-\bar{r}}{2}\theta_{d}^{2}\Big)\mathrm{d}\theta_{d}}\,.

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\nu_{0}\big(\theta_{d}^{1}\theta_{d}^{2}\big)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\frac{\int\theta^{1}_{d}\theta^{2}_{d}\exp\Big((\theta^{1}_{d}+\theta^{2}_{d})\sqrt{r}Y-\frac{2\beta+r-\bar{r}}{2}\Big(\big(\theta^{1}_{d}\big)^{2}+\big(\theta^{2}_{d}\big)^{2}\Big)\Big)\mathrm{d}\theta^{1}_{d}\theta^{2}_{d}}{\int\exp\Big((\theta^{1}_{d}+\theta^{2}_{d})\sqrt{r}Y-\frac{2\beta+r-\bar{r}}{2}\Big(\big(\theta^{1}_{d}\big)^{2}+\big(\theta^{2}_{d}\big)^{2}\Big)\Big)\mathrm{d}\theta^{1}_{d}\theta^{2}_{d}}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Bigg(\frac{\int\theta_{d}\exp\Big(\theta_{d}\sqrt{r}Y-\frac{2\beta+r-\bar{r}}{2}\theta_{d}^{2}\Big)\mathrm{d}\theta_{d}}{\int\exp\Big(\theta_{d}\sqrt{r}Y-\frac{2\beta+r-\bar{r}}{2}\theta_{d}^{2}\Big)\mathrm{d}\theta_{d}}\Bigg)^{2}\,.

The above are univariate Gaussian integrals w.r.t.
\theta_{d}
. As detailed on p. 219 of
[
Tal10
]
, letting
Z
be a centered univariate Gaussian and
s\in\mathbb{R}
, we obtain from Gaussian Integration by Parts that

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[Ze^{sZ}]=s\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[Z^{2}\big]\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[e^{sZ}\big]\,,\,\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[Z^{2}e^{sZ}]=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[Z^{2}\big]\Big(\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[e^{sZ}\big]+s^{2}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[Z^{2}\big]\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[e^{sZ}\big]\Big)\,,

where expectation is w.r.t. the law of
Z
. We obtain

\displaystyle\Bigg(\frac{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[Ze^{sZ}]}{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[e^{sZ}\big]}\Bigg)^{2}=s^{2}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[Z^{2}\big]^{2}\,,\,\frac{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[Z^{2}e^{sZ}]}{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[e^{sZ}\big]}=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[Z^{2}]+s^{2}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[Z^{2}\big]^{2}\,.

We apply the above display with
s=\sqrt{r}Y
,
Z\sim N\big(0,\frac{1}{2\beta+r-\bar{r}}\big)
to compute the quantity inside the expectation in the integrals equaling
\nu_{0}\big(\theta_{d}^{1}\theta_{d}^{2}\big)
,
\nu_{0}\big((\theta_{d}^{1})^{2}\big)
. This yields

\displaystyle\nu_{0}\big(\theta_{d}^{1}\theta_{d}^{2}\big)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Bigg(\frac{\int\theta_{d}\exp\Big(\theta_{d}\sqrt{r}Y-\frac{2\beta+r-\bar{r}}{2}\theta_{d}^{2}\Big)\mathrm{d}\theta_{d}}{\int\exp\Big(\theta_{d}\sqrt{r}Y-\frac{2\beta+r-\bar{r}}{2}\theta_{d}^{2}\Big)\mathrm{d}\theta_{d}}\Bigg)^{2}=\frac{rY^{2}}{(2\beta+r-\bar{r})^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle\nu_{0}\big((\theta_{d}^{1})^{2}\big)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\frac{\int\theta_{d}^{2}\exp\Big(\theta_{d}\sqrt{r}Y-\frac{2\beta+r-\bar{r}}{2}\theta_{d}^{2}\Big)\mathrm{d}\theta_{d}}{\int\exp\Big(\theta_{d}\sqrt{r}Y-\frac{2\beta+r-\bar{r}}{2}\theta_{d}^{2}\Big)\mathrm{d}\theta_{d}}=\frac{1}{2\beta+r-\bar{r}}+\frac{rY^{2}}{(2\beta+r-\bar{r})^{2}}\,.

Finally taking expectation over
Y\sim N(0,1)
, we establish that

\displaystyle\rho

\displaystyle=\nu_{1}\big((\theta_{d}^{1})^{2}\big)=\frac{1}{2\beta+r-\bar{r}}+\frac{r}{(2\beta+r-\bar{r})^{2}}+\Big(\nu_{1}\big((\theta_{d}^{1})^{2}\big)-\nu_{0}\big((\theta_{d}^{1})^{2}\big)\Big)\,,

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle=\nu_{1}\big(\theta_{d}^{1}\theta_{d}^{2}\big)=\frac{r}{(2\beta+r-\bar{r})^{2}}+\Big(\nu_{1}\big(\theta_{d}^{1}\theta_{d}^{2}\big)-\nu_{0}\big(\theta_{d}^{1}\theta_{d}^{2}\big)\Big)\,.

The result follows by applying Lemma
A.8
to control
\nu_{1}\big((\theta_{d}^{1})^{2}\big)-\nu_{0}\big((\theta_{d}^{1})^{2}\big)
,
\nu_{1}\big(\theta_{d}^{1}\theta_{d}^{2}\big)-\nu_{0}\big(\theta_{d}^{1}\theta_{d}^{2}\big)
.
∎

In Appendix
E.1
, we will establish:

Proposition A.10
.

For all
(\alpha,\beta,x)\in[0,\alpha_{0}]\times[\beta_{0},\beta_{1}]\times[-1,1]
, any solution
(q,\rho,r,\bar{r})
of
(
A.30
)
must satisfy
(q,\rho)\in(0,C_{\textsc{sol}})\times\big(\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}\big)
and
\frac{1}{\rho-q}<C_{\textsc{sol}}
.

We will use Proposition
A.9
and
A.10
to establish the following.

Proposition A.11
.

Consider any
(\alpha,\beta,x)\in[0,\alpha_{0}]\times[\beta_{0},\beta_{1}]\times[-1,1]
. Then there is a
0<\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}_{\textup{\ref{prop:boundedapproxRSeq}}}\leq\varepsilon^{\prime}
depending on
u,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\lambda
satisfying the following. For all
\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}\leq\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}_{\textup{\ref{prop:boundedapproxRSeq}}}
and all
d\geq d_{\textup{\ref{prop:boundedapproxRSeq}}}\geq\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{\prime\prime 2}}
, for any
n\geq 1
such that
\frac{n}{d}=\alpha\in[0,\alpha_{0}]
, we have

\displaystyle(q_{n,d},\rho_{n,d})\in\big[0,C_{\textsc{sol}}\big]\times\Big[\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}\Big]\quad\text{ and }\quad\frac{1}{\rho_{n,d}-q_{n,d}}\leq C_{\textsc{sol}}\,,

Furthermore, in the case of the interpolators, supposing the mollifier
u
satisfies the conditions of Theorem
A.1
in terms of some parameter
\varepsilon^{\prime}
, we have
|\delta_{1}|,|\delta_{2}|\leq\varepsilon^{\prime}
where
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}
are the errors from Proposition
A.9
for
\rho_{n,d},q_{n,d}
.

Proof.

By definition of (
A.32
), Proposition
A.9
, and as
\alpha\leq 2
, for suitable
\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}_{\textup{\ref{prop:boundedapproxRSeq}}}
depending on
u,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\lambda
and
\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}\leq\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}_{\textup{\ref{prop:boundedapproxRSeq}}}
and
d\geq d_{\textup{\ref{prop:boundedapproxRSeq}}}\geq\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{\prime\prime 2}}
, we have the bounds
\big|\rho_{n,d}\big|,\big|q_{n,d}\big|\leq K_{\textsc{bound}}(D,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\lambda)
for some
K_{\textsc{bound}}(D,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\lambda)>0
(dependence on
D
comes through
u
). Without loss of generality, suppose
K_{\textsc{bound}}(D,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\lambda)>C_{\textsc{sol}}
. Consider

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{u}

\displaystyle:=\Big[-K_{\textsc{bound}}(D,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\lambda),K_{\textsc{bound}}(D,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\lambda)\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\Big[-K_{\textsc{bound}}(D,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\lambda),K_{\textsc{bound}}(D,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\lambda)\Big]\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}

\displaystyle:=\Big\{(q,\rho)\in\big(0,C_{\textsc{sol}}\big)\times\Big(\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}\Big),\frac{1}{\rho-q}<C_{\textsc{sol}}\Big\}\,.

Let

\displaystyle R(q,\rho,\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle:=\Bigg(\rho-\frac{1}{2\beta+\psi_{\alpha}(q,\rho)-\bar{\psi}_{\alpha}(q,\rho)}-\frac{\psi_{\alpha}(q,\rho)}{\big(2\beta+\psi_{\alpha}(q,\rho)-\bar{\psi}_{\alpha}(q,\rho)\big)^{2}},

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad q-\frac{\psi_{\alpha}(q,\rho)}{\big(2\beta+\psi_{\alpha}(q,\rho)-\bar{\psi}_{\alpha}(q,\rho)\big)^{2}}\Bigg)\,,

\displaystyle\underline{c}

\displaystyle:=\inf_{(q,\rho)\in\mathcal{D}_{u}-\mathcal{D},\alpha\in[0,2],\beta\in[\beta_{0},\beta_{1}]}\big\|R(q,\rho,\alpha,\beta)\big\|\leq\inf_{(q,\rho)\in\mathcal{D}_{u}-\mathcal{D},\alpha\in[0,\alpha_{0}],\beta\in[\beta_{0},\beta_{1}]}\big\|R(q,\rho,\alpha,\beta)\big\|\,.

Note
R(q,\rho,\alpha,\beta)
is continuous in
q,\rho,\alpha,\beta
. Moreover in
\mathcal{D}_{u}-\mathcal{D}
, by Proposition
A.10
, we have that
R(q,\rho,\alpha,\beta)>0
pointwise; else we obtain a solution to (
A.30
) outside
\mathcal{D}
, contradicting Proposition
A.10
. Thus as
\big\{(q,\rho,\alpha,\beta):(q,\rho)\in\mathcal{D}_{u}-\mathcal{D},\alpha\in[0,2],\beta\in[\beta_{0},\beta_{1}]\big\}
is compact,
\underline{c}>0
.

Hence in the case of interpolators, we may take
\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}_{\textup{\ref{prop:boundedapproxRSeq}}}=\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}_{\textup{\ref{prop:boundedapproxRSeq}}}(u,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\lambda)
small enough, and
d_{\textup{\ref{prop:boundedapproxRSeq}}}(\varepsilon^{\prime\prime})\geq\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{\prime\prime 2}}
large enough in terms of
K_{\textup{\ref{prop:approxrealRSequations}}}(D,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\lambda)
and
\varepsilon^{\prime}
(recall that
D
depends solely on
u
) so that
|\delta_{1}|,|\delta_{2}|
from (
A.33
) are each strictly less than
\min\big\{\frac{\underline{c}}{2},\frac{\varepsilon^{\prime}}{2}\big\}
.
In the case of the posterior, we ignore dependence in
\varepsilon^{\prime}
in the above definitions of
\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}_{\textup{\ref{prop:boundedapproxRSeq}}}=\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}_{\textup{\ref{prop:boundedapproxRSeq}}}(u,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\lambda)
and
d_{\textup{\ref{prop:boundedapproxRSeq}}}(\varepsilon^{\prime\prime})
, now guaranteeing
|\delta_{1}|,|\delta_{2}|<\underline{c}/2
.

By Proposition
A.9
, it follows that
\|R(q_{n,d},\rho_{n,d},\alpha,\beta)\|<\underline{c}
.
Since
(q_{n,d},\rho_{n,d})\in\mathcal{D}_{u}
, by definition of
\underline{c}
, it follows that
(q_{n,d},\rho_{n,d})\in\mathcal{D}
. The conclusion of this Proposition follows.
∎

Note this argument is where we use that
|\delta_{1}|,|\delta_{2}|
from (
A.33
) are upper bounded independently of
\alpha
.
In particular this lets us take
\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}_{\textup{\ref{prop:boundedapproxRSeq}}}=\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}_{\textup{\ref{prop:boundedapproxRSeq}}}(u,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\lambda)
independently of
\alpha
, which is crucial as we will use the same
\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}
across
n=1,2,\ldots,d
when computing the expected free energy.
The compactness supplied from the above Proposition is crucial in the arguments to follow in Propositions

A.14
and
A.13
.

The above work lets us interpolate one datapoint at a time and compute the difference in free energy, as we do in Appendix
A.3
.
The last step we need is to compute
\phi_{\beta,0,d}(I)
, which is the base case arising when all
n
datapoints have been accounted for.

Lemma A.12
.

For any
\varepsilon>0
, for some
\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}_{\textup{\ref{lem:interpolationlastcalc}}},d_{\textup{\ref{lem:interpolationlastcalc}}}>0
depending on
\varepsilon
, we have for all
\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}\leq\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}_{\textup{\ref{lem:interpolationlastcalc}}}
and
d\geq d_{\textup{\ref{lem:interpolationlastcalc}}}
that

\displaystyle\Big|\phi_{\beta,0,d}(I)-\Big(\textsc{RS}_{0,I}(0)+\frac{1}{2}\log(2\pi e)\Big)\Big|\leq\varepsilon\,.

Proof.

A direct computation gives that

\displaystyle\phi_{\beta,0,d}(I)=\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{\pi}{\beta}-\beta r(x)+O\Big(\frac{\log d}{d}+\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}\Big)\,,

where we recall the definition of
r(x)
from (
A.10
). Here
O(\cdot)
only hides universal constants.
Now note when
\alpha=0
,
\frac{\partial F}{\partial q}=0
implies
q=0
, hence
q_{0}(0)=0
. Also, considering
\frac{\partial F}{\partial\rho}=0
when
\alpha=0
implies that
\rho_{0}(0)=\frac{1}{2\beta}
.
Therefore

\displaystyle\textsc{RS}_{0,I}(0)+\frac{1}{2}\log(2\pi e)=\frac{1}{2}\log\big(2\pi e\rho_{0}(0)\big)-\beta\big(\rho_{0}(0)+r(x)\big)=\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{\pi}{\beta}-\beta r(x)\,.

For suitable
\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}\leq\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}_{\textup{\ref{lem:interpolationlastcalc}}}(\varepsilon)
and
d\geq d_{\textup{\ref{lem:interpolationlastcalc}}}(\varepsilon)
, this implies the Lemma.
∎

A.3
Proof of Theorem
A.1
for interpolators

We now complete the proof of Theorem
A.1
by summing Proposition
A.4
and the results of Appendix
A.2
across all datapoints
i
,
1\leq i\leq n
.
We first handle the necessary steps to replace the mollifier
u(\cdot)
in (
A.29
) by the hard step function
\mathbf{1}\{x\geq\kappa\}
in
\textsc{RSG}_{I}
.
Specifically, this is done in the following Propositions
A.13
and
A.14
, which establish that the impact of the mollifier
u(\cdot)
is insignificant. At a high level, this is because the conditions of Theorem
A.1
imply
u(\cdot)
is an accurate approximation to
\mathbf{1}\{x\geq\kappa\}
.

Proposition A.13
.

Consider any
\delta>0
and let
\kappa^{+}=\max\{\kappa,0\}
. Then there exists
\varepsilon^{\prime}_{\textup{\ref{prop:mollifiererror}}}>0
depending on
\delta,C_{\textsc{sol}},\kappa^{+},\lambda
such that the following holds.
Suppose
u\leq 0
satisfies for some
\varepsilon^{\prime}\leq\varepsilon^{\prime}_{\textup{\ref{prop:mollifiererror}}}
that:

1.

u(x)=0
for
x\geq\kappa
,

2.

\exp u(x)\leq\varepsilon^{\prime}
for all
x\leq\kappa-\varepsilon^{\prime}
.

Then for any
(q,\rho)
such that
(q,\rho)\in[0,C_{\textsc{sol}}]\times[\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}]
and
\frac{1}{\rho-q}\leq C_{\textsc{sol}}
, and any
S\sim\mathcal{D}
, we have

\displaystyle\Big|\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\log\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\exp u\big(xS+\sqrt{q}Z+\sqrt{\rho-q}W\big)\Big]-\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\log\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{W}\big(xS+\sqrt{q}Z+\sqrt{\rho-q}W\geq\kappa\big)\Big]\Big|\leq\delta\,.

Proof.

Note
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\exp u\big(xS+\sqrt{q}Z+\sqrt{\rho-q}W\big)\geq\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{W}\big(xS+\sqrt{q}Z+\sqrt{\rho-q}W\geq\kappa\big)
.
Breaking into cases on the value of
xS+\sqrt{q}Z+\sqrt{\rho-q}W
and using that
\exp u(x)\leq\varepsilon^{\prime}
for all
x\leq\kappa-\varepsilon^{\prime}
and
u\leq 0
, we thus may rewrite

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\log\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\exp u\big(xS+\sqrt{q}Z+\sqrt{\rho-q}W\big)\Big]-\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\log\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{W}\big(xS+\sqrt{q}Z+\sqrt{\rho-q}W\geq\kappa\big)\Big]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Bigg[\log\frac{\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{W}\big(xS+\sqrt{q}Z+\sqrt{\rho-q}W\geq\kappa\big)+\varepsilon^{\prime}+\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{W}\big(xS+\sqrt{q}Z+\sqrt{\rho-q}W\in(\kappa-\varepsilon^{\prime},\kappa)\big)}{\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{W}\big(xS+\sqrt{q}Z+\sqrt{\rho-q}W\geq\kappa\big)}\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Bigg[\log\Bigg(1+\frac{\varepsilon^{\prime}+\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{W}\big(xS+\sqrt{q}Z+\sqrt{\rho-q}W\in(\kappa-\varepsilon^{\prime},\kappa)\big)}{\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{W}\big(xS+\sqrt{q}Z+\sqrt{\rho-q}W\geq\kappa\big)}\Bigg)\Bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Bigg[\frac{\varepsilon^{\prime}+\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{W}\big(xS+\sqrt{q}Z+\sqrt{\rho-q}W\in(\kappa-\varepsilon^{\prime},\kappa)\big)}{\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{W}\big(xS+\sqrt{q}Z+\sqrt{\rho-q}W\geq\kappa^{+}\big)}\Bigg]\,,

where we used the inequality
\log(1+x)\leq x
and replaced
\kappa
by
\kappa^{+}
in the denominator by monotonicity. We next bound

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{W}\Big(xS+\sqrt{q}Z+\sqrt{\rho-q}W\in(\kappa-\varepsilon^{\prime},\kappa)\Big)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{W}\Big(W\in\Big[\frac{\kappa-\varepsilon^{\prime}-sx-z\sqrt{q}}{\sqrt{\rho-q}},\frac{\kappa-sx-z\sqrt{q}}{\sqrt{\rho-q}}\Big]\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\varepsilon^{\prime}}{\sqrt{\rho-q}}\cdot\sup_{w\in\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp(-w^{2}/2)

\displaystyle\leq C_{\textsc{sol}}^{1/2}\varepsilon^{\prime}\,.

Also note that as
\rho-q\geq\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}}
,
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\frac{1}{\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{W}\big(xS+\sqrt{q}Z+\sqrt{\rho-q}W\geq\kappa^{+}\big)}\Big]
is a continuous function of
q,\rho
and only depends on
q,\rho,\kappa^{+},\lambda
. Hence we may upper bound its value in the compact region
(q,\rho)\in[0,C_{\textsc{sol}}]\times[\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}],\frac{1}{\rho-q}\leq C_{\textsc{sol}}
as follows:

\displaystyle\sup_{(q,\rho)\in[0,C_{\textsc{sol}}]\times[\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}],\frac{1}{\rho-q}\leq C_{\textsc{sol}}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Bigg[\frac{1}{\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{W}\big(xS+\sqrt{q}Z+\sqrt{\rho-q}W\geq\kappa^{+}\big)}\Bigg]\leq K(C_{\textsc{sol}},\kappa^{+},\lambda)\,.

It follows that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\log\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\exp u\big(xS+\sqrt{q}Z+\sqrt{\rho-q}W\big)\Big]-\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\log\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{W}\big(xS+\sqrt{q}Z+\sqrt{\rho-q}W\geq\kappa\big)\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon^{\prime}(C_{\textsc{sol}}^{1/2}+1)\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Bigg[\frac{1}{\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{W}\big(xS+\sqrt{q}Z+\sqrt{\rho-q}W\geq\kappa^{+}\big)}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\leq K(C_{\textsc{sol}},\kappa^{+},\lambda)\big(C_{\textsc{sol}}^{1/2}+1\big)\varepsilon^{\prime}\,.

Thus we may take
\varepsilon^{\prime}_{\textup{\ref{prop:mollifiererror}}}=\varepsilon^{\prime}_{\textup{\ref{prop:mollifiererror}}}(\delta,C_{\textsc{sol}},\kappa^{+},\lambda)\leq\frac{\delta}{K(C_{\textsc{sol}},\kappa^{+},\lambda)(C_{\textsc{sol}}^{1/2}+1)}
.
∎

We next use the uniqueness of the solution
(q_{0},\rho_{0})
to (
A.11
), given by Proposition
A.3
, to control the difference of
(q_{n,d},\rho_{n,d})
vs.
(q_{0},\rho_{0})
.

Proposition A.14
.

Consider any
(\beta,x)\in[\beta_{0},\beta_{1}]\times[-1,1]
. Given any
\delta>0
, there exists
\varepsilon^{\prime}_{\textup{\ref{prop:diffvsuniquesol}}}>0
depending on
\delta
such that the following holds.
Consider any
u\leq 0
satisfies the conditions of Theorem
A.1
with
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\varepsilon^{\prime}_{\textup{\ref{prop:diffvsuniquesol}}}
.
Then for all
\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}\leq\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}_{\textup{\ref{prop:boundedapproxRSeq}}}
and all
d\geq d_{\textup{\ref{prop:boundedapproxRSeq}}}
, and for any
n\geq 1
such that
\frac{n}{d}=\alpha\in[0,\alpha_{0}]
, the following holds.
Defining
\big(q_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x),\rho_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)\big)
as per Proposition
A.3
,

\displaystyle\big|q_{n,d}-q_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)\big|\quad\text{and}\quad\big|\rho_{n,d}-\rho_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)\big|\leq\delta\,.

Proof.

Suppose for the sake of contradiction that there exists a
\delta>0
and a sequence
\varepsilon^{\prime}_{k}\rightarrow 0
, a sequence of functions
u_{k}
such that
u_{k}(x)=0
for
x\geq\kappa
and
\exp u_{k}(x)\leq\varepsilon^{\prime}_{k}
for all
x\leq\kappa-\varepsilon^{\prime}_{k}
,
\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}_{k}\leq\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}_{\textup{\ref{prop:boundedapproxRSeq}}}(u_{k},\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\lambda)
,
d_{k}\geq d_{\textup{\ref{prop:boundedapproxRSeq}}}(\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}_{k})
,
n_{k}
with
\frac{n_{k}}{d_{k}}=\alpha_{k}\in[0,\alpha_{0}]
, such that

\displaystyle\max\Big\{\big|q_{n_{k},d_{k}}-q_{0}(\alpha_{k},\beta,x)\big|\,,\,\big|\rho_{n_{k},d_{k}}-\rho_{0}(\alpha_{k},\beta,x)\big|\Big\}\geq\delta\,.

(A.34)

By Proposition
A.11
, since
\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}_{k}\leq\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}_{\textup{\ref{prop:boundedapproxRSeq}}}(u_{k},\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\lambda)
,
d_{k}\geq d_{\textup{\ref{prop:boundedapproxRSeq}}}(\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}_{k})
, we have

\displaystyle(q_{n_{k},d_{k}},\rho_{n_{k},d_{k}})\in\big[0,C_{\textsc{sol}}\big]\times\Big[\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}\Big]\,,\,\frac{1}{\rho_{n_{k},d_{k}}-q_{n_{k},d_{k}}}\leq C_{\textsc{sol}}\,.

(A.35)

Also, letting
\delta_{1,k},\delta_{2,k}
be the corresponding
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}
from the system (
A.32
) for
u_{k},\alpha_{k},q_{n_{k},d_{k}},\rho_{n_{k},d_{k}}
, we have by the second part of Proposition
A.11
that
|\delta_{1,k}|,|\delta_{2,k}|\leq\varepsilon^{\prime}_{k}
.

Thus
(q_{n_{k},d_{k}},\rho_{n_{k},d_{k}})
lie in a set bounded independently of
k
, so we may extract a bounded subsequence
(\alpha_{k},q_{n_{k},d_{k}},\rho_{n_{k},d_{k}})
converging to some limit
(\alpha^{\star},q^{\star},\rho^{\star})
.
Upon reindexing, as
\varepsilon^{\prime}_{k}\rightarrow 0
, we may suppose that

\displaystyle(\varepsilon^{\prime}_{k},\alpha_{k},q_{n_{k},d_{k}},\rho_{n_{k},d_{k}},\delta_{1,k},\delta_{2,k})\rightarrow(0,\alpha^{\star},q^{\star},\rho^{\star},0,0)\,.

(A.36)

Thus, we have

\displaystyle(q^{\star},\rho^{\star})\in\big[0,C_{\textsc{sol}}\big]\times\Big[\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}\Big]\,,\,\frac{1}{\rho^{\star}-q^{\star}}\leq C_{\textsc{sol}}\,.

(A.37)

By (
A.34
), and as
\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}q_{0}(\alpha_{k},\beta,x)=q_{0}(\alpha^{\star},\beta,x)
,
\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\rho_{0}(\alpha_{k},\beta,x)=\rho_{0}(\alpha^{\star},\beta,x)
by Proposition
A.3
, it follows that

\displaystyle\max\Big\{\big|q^{\star}-q_{0}(\alpha^{\star},\beta,x)\big|,\big|\rho^{\star}-\rho_{0}(\alpha^{\star},\beta,x)\big|\Big\}\geq\delta\,.

(A.38)

By (
A.35
), (
A.36
), (
A.37
), in particular as
\rho_{n_{k},d_{k}}-q_{n_{k},d_{k}},\rho^{\star}-q^{\star}\geq\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}}
, we also have the existence of the following limit and the following equality:

\displaystyle\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\Big[f(W)\mathbf{1}\big\{xS+\sqrt{q_{n_{k},d_{k}}}Z+\sqrt{\rho_{n_{k},d_{k}}-q_{n_{k},d_{k}}}W\geq\kappa\big\}\Big]

(A.39)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\Big[f(W)\mathbf{1}\big\{xS+\sqrt{q^{\star}}Z+\sqrt{\rho^{\star}-q^{\star}}W\geq\kappa\big\}\Big]\,.

Next, letting
f(W)\in\big\{1,W,W^{2}-1\big\}
and using our conditions on
u_{k}
,

\displaystyle\Big|\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\Big[f(W)\exp u_{k}\big(xS+\sqrt{q_{n_{k},d_{k}}}Z+\sqrt{\rho_{n_{k},d_{k}}-q_{n_{k},d_{k}}}W\big)\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad-\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\Big[f(W)\mathbf{1}\big\{xS+\sqrt{q_{n_{k},d_{k}}}Z+\sqrt{\rho_{n_{k},d_{k}}-q_{n_{k},d_{k}}}W\geq\kappa\big\}\Big]\Big|

\displaystyle\quad\leq\varepsilon^{\prime}_{k}\Big|\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[f(W)\big]\Big|+\Big|\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\Big[f(W)\mathbf{1}\big\{xS+\sqrt{q_{n_{k},d_{k}}}Z+\sqrt{\rho_{n_{k},d_{k}}-q_{n_{k},d_{k}}}W\in\big(\kappa-\varepsilon^{\prime}_{k},\kappa\big)\big\}\Big]\Big|

\displaystyle\quad=\varepsilon^{\prime}_{k}\Big|\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[f(W)\big]\Big|+\int_{\kappa_{1}}^{\kappa_{2}}|f(W)|\varphi_{0}(W)\,\mathrm{d}W

\displaystyle\quad\leq\varepsilon^{\prime}_{k}\Big|\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[f(W)\big]\Big|+\frac{K\varepsilon^{\prime}_{k}}{\sqrt{\rho_{n_{k},d_{k}}-q_{n_{k},d_{k}}}}

\displaystyle\quad\leq KC_{\textsc{sol}}^{1/2}\varepsilon^{\prime}_{k}\,,

(A.40)

where we let
\kappa_{1}=\frac{\kappa-\varepsilon^{\prime}_{k}-xS-\sqrt{q_{n_{k},d_{k}}}Z}{\sqrt{\rho_{n_{k},d_{k}}-q_{n_{k},d_{k}}}}
,
\kappa_{2}=\frac{\kappa-sX-\sqrt{q_{n_{k},d_{k}}}Z}{\sqrt{\rho_{n_{k},d_{k}}-q_{n_{k},d_{k}}}}=\kappa_{1}+\frac{\varepsilon^{\prime}_{k}}{\sqrt{\rho_{n_{k},d_{k}}-q_{n_{k},d_{k}}}}
.
Here in the last step we used that
|f(y)|\varphi_{0}(y)\leq K
uniformly for all
y\in\mathbb{R}
.

In light of (
A.39
), (
A.40
), since
C_{\textsc{sol}}
is independent of the mollifier and as
\varepsilon^{\prime}_{k}\rightarrow 0
, it follows that the following limit exists and can be computed as:

\displaystyle\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\Big[f(W)\exp u_{k}\big(xS+\sqrt{q_{n_{k},d_{k}}}Z+\sqrt{\rho_{n_{k},d_{k}}-q_{n_{k},d_{k}}}W\big)\Big]

(A.41)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\Big[f(W)\mathbf{1}\big\{xS+\sqrt{q^{\star}}Z+\sqrt{\rho^{\star}-q^{\star}}W\geq\kappa\big\}\Big]\,.

(Note this argument does not assume anything a-priori about the limit of the
u_{k}
’s; the existence of
q^{\star},\rho^{\star}
and (
A.40
) shows that the limit exists.)

Now consider the system (
A.32
), which we know each
(u_{k},\alpha_{k},q_{n_{k},d_{k}},\rho_{n_{k},d_{k}},\delta_{1,k},\delta_{2,k})
solves by Proposition
A.9
.
Since
\rho^{\star}-q^{\star}>\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}}
, we then may rewrite
\psi_{\alpha},\bar{\psi}_{\alpha}
and hence
r,\bar{r}
as per (
A.22
), (
A.23
).

Since
\delta_{1,k},\delta_{2,k}\rightarrow 0
, we may now take limits
k\rightarrow\infty
on both sides of (
A.32
) (where now
r,\bar{r}
are given as per (
A.22
), (
A.23
)), since the denominators of (
A.32
) are bounded away from
0
independently of
k
as
r\geq\bar{r}
by Lemma
E.3
and as
\beta\leq\beta_{1}
.
Using (
A.41
) to compute the limit of the expressions involving the
u_{k}
’s and letting
\kappa^{\prime}=\frac{\kappa-xS-\sqrt{q}Z}{\sqrt{\rho-q}}
, it follows that
(q^{\star},\rho^{\star})
solves the system

\displaystyle r=\frac{\alpha^{\star}}{\rho-q}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\Big(\frac{\varphi_{0}(\kappa^{\prime})}{\mathcal{N}(\kappa^{\prime})}\Big)^{2}\Big]\quad\,

\displaystyle,\quad\bar{r}=\frac{\alpha^{\star}}{\rho-q}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\frac{\kappa^{\prime}\varphi_{0}(\kappa^{\prime})}{\mathcal{N}(\kappa^{\prime})}\Big]\,,

\displaystyle\rho=\frac{1}{2\beta+r-\bar{r}}+\frac{r}{(2\beta+r-\bar{r})^{2}}\quad\,

\displaystyle,\quad q=\frac{r}{(2\beta+r-\bar{r})^{2}}\,.

Manipulating the above system and using the identity
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(x)=\mathcal{A}(x)^{2}-x\mathcal{A}(x)
where
\mathcal{A}(x)=\frac{\varphi_{0}(x)}{\mathcal{N}(x)}
denotes the Inverse Mills’ Ratio (see Lemma
E.5
), it follows that
(q^{\star},\rho^{\star})
solves (
A.11
).

Since
(q^{\star},\rho^{\star})\in[0,C_{\textsc{sol}}]\times\big[\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}\big]
and
\frac{1}{\rho^{\star}-q^{\star}}\leq C_{\textsc{sol}}
, and as
(q^{\star},\rho^{\star})
solves (
A.11
), uniqueness given by Proposition
A.3
now implies that
\rho^{\star}=\rho_{0}(\alpha^{\star},\beta,x)
and
q^{\star}=q_{0}(\alpha^{\star},\beta,x)
.
This contradicts (
A.38
).
∎

We now complete the proof of Theorem
A.1
.
As
C_{\textsc{sol}}
only depends on
\beta_{0},\beta_{1}
, we may let
H_{1}=H_{1}(\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\kappa,\lambda)
denote an upper bound on the norm of the gradient of the
C^{\infty}
function
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[\log\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{W}\big(xS+\sqrt{q}Z+\sqrt{\rho-q}W\geq\kappa\big)\big]
over the compact set
\big\{(x,\rho,q):-1\leq x\leq 1,0\leq q\leq C_{\textsc{sol}},\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}}\leq\rho\leq C_{\textsc{sol}},\frac{1}{\rho-q}\leq C_{\textsc{sol}}\big\}
. Here the function is
C^{\infty}
in this compact domain as
\rho-q\geq\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}}
. Dependence on
\lambda
arises as the law of
S
depends on
\lambda
. Without loss of generality suppose
H_{1}\geq 1
.

By Proposition
A.3
, we may also let
H_{2}=H_{2}(\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\kappa,\lambda)
be an upper bound on the gradient of the
C^{\infty}
function
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[\log\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{W}\big(xS+\sqrt{q_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)}Z+\sqrt{\rho_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)-q_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)}W\geq\kappa\big)\big]
on
(\alpha,\beta,x)\in[0,\alpha_{0}]\times[\beta_{0},\beta_{1}]\times[-1,1]
. Here the function is
C^{\infty}
in this compact domain as
\rho_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)-q_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)\geq\frac{1}{2C_{\textsc{sol}}}
by Proposition
A.3
.

Now, we let

\displaystyle\delta=\delta(\varepsilon,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\kappa,\lambda)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\varepsilon}{12H_{1}}\,,

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{\prime}=\varepsilon^{\prime}(\varepsilon,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\kappa,\lambda)

\displaystyle:=\min\Big\{\varepsilon^{\prime}_{\textup{\ref{prop:mollifiererror}}}(\delta,C_{\textsc{sol}},\kappa^{+},\lambda),\varepsilon^{\prime}_{\textup{\ref{prop:diffvsuniquesol}}}(\delta)\Big\}\,,

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\textsc{RS}_{0}}=\varepsilon_{\textsc{RS}_{0}}(\varepsilon,u,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\kappa,\lambda)

\displaystyle:=\min\Big\{\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}_{\textup{\ref{prop:boundedapproxRSeq}}}(u,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\lambda),\frac{\varepsilon}{12K_{\textup{\ref{prop:interpolationperconstraint}}}(D,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\kappa,\lambda)},\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}_{\textup{\ref{lem:interpolationlastcalc}}}(\varepsilon/4)\Big\}\,.

Consider any
0<\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}\leq\varepsilon_{\textsc{RS}_{0}}
as in the statement of this Theorem. (Note dependence on
\varepsilon^{\prime}
in
\varepsilon_{\textsc{RS}_{0}}
comes implicitly through
u
.) Finally we let

\displaystyle d(\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime\prime},\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\kappa,\lambda)

\displaystyle:=\max\Big\{d_{\textup{\ref{prop:boundedapproxRSeq}}}(\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}),\frac{12H_{2}}{\varepsilon},d_{\textup{\ref{lem:interpolationlastcalc}}}(\varepsilon/4),d_{\textup{\ref{prop:interpolationperconstraint}}}(\varepsilon^{\prime\prime})\Big\}\,.

Consider any
d\geq d(\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime\prime},\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\kappa,\lambda)
as in the statement of this Theorem. We now consider any
n
such that
\frac{n}{d}\in[0,\alpha_{0}]
.

Next, we consider any
1\leq i\leq n
and let
\alpha=\frac{i}{d}
.
In what follows, we apply the results from Appendices
A.1
,
A.2
with
i
in place of
n
.
By Proposition
A.4
and as
d\geq d_{\textup{\ref{prop:interpolationperconstraint}}}(\varepsilon^{\prime\prime})
and
\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{12K_{\textup{\ref{prop:interpolationperconstraint}}}(D,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\kappa,\lambda)}
, we have

\displaystyle\Big|\phi_{\beta,i,d}(I)-\phi_{\beta,i-1,d}(I)-\frac{1}{d}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\log\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\exp u\big(xs_{i}+\sqrt{q_{i,d}}Z+\sqrt{\rho_{i,d}-q_{i,d}}W\big)\Big]\Big|

(A.42)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{K_{\textup{\ref{prop:interpolationperconstraint}}}(D,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\kappa,\lambda)}{d}\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{12d}\,.

By Proposition
A.11
, as
\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}\leq\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}_{\textup{\ref{prop:boundedapproxRSeq}}}(u,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\lambda)
and
d\geq d_{\textup{\ref{prop:boundedapproxRSeq}}}(\varepsilon^{\prime\prime})
, we have

\displaystyle\big(q_{i,d},\rho_{i,d}\big)\in[0,C_{\textsc{sol}}]\times\Big[\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}\Big]\,,\,\frac{1}{\rho_{i,d}-q_{i,d}}\leq C_{\textsc{sol}}\,.

Consequently by Proposition
A.13
, as
\varepsilon^{\prime}\leq\varepsilon^{\prime}_{\textup{\ref{prop:mollifiererror}}}(\delta,C_{\textsc{sol}},\kappa^{+},\lambda)
and
s_{i}\sim\mathcal{D}
,

\displaystyle\Big|\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\log\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\exp u\big(xs_{i}+\sqrt{q_{i,d}}Z+\sqrt{\rho_{i,d}-q_{i,d}}W\big)\Big]

(A.43)

\displaystyle-\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\log\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{W}\big(xs_{i}+\sqrt{q_{i,d}}Z+\sqrt{\rho_{i,d}-q_{i,d}}W\geq\kappa\big)\Big]\Big|\leq\delta\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{12}\,.

Next, by Proposition
A.14
, we have

\displaystyle\big|q_{i,d}-q_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)\big|\,,\,\big|\rho_{i,d}-\rho_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)\big|\leq\delta=\frac{\varepsilon}{12H_{1}}\,.

Consequently

\displaystyle\Big|\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\log\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{W}\big(xs_{i}+\sqrt{q_{i,d}}Z+\sqrt{\rho_{i,d}-q_{i,d}}W\geq\kappa\big)\Big]

(A.44)

\displaystyle-\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\log\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{W}\big(xs_{i}+\sqrt{q_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)}Z+\sqrt{\rho_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)-q_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)}W\geq\kappa\big)\Big]\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\varepsilon\sqrt{2}}{12H_{1}}\cdot H_{1}<\frac{\varepsilon}{8}\,.

Finally, observe that for any
\alpha^{\prime}\in\big[\frac{i-1}{d},\frac{i}{d}\big]
,
\big|\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}\big|\leq\frac{1}{d}
, so as
d\geq 12H_{2}/\varepsilon
we have

\displaystyle\Big|\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\log\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{W}\big(xs_{i}+\sqrt{q_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)}Z+\sqrt{\rho_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)-q_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)}W\geq\kappa\big)\Big]

(A.45)

\displaystyle-\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\log\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{W}\big(xs_{i}+\sqrt{q_{0}(\alpha^{\prime},\beta,x)}Z+\sqrt{\rho_{0}(\alpha^{\prime},\beta,x)-q_{0}(\alpha^{\prime},\beta,x)}W\geq\kappa\big)\Big]\Big|

\displaystyle\leq H_{2}\cdot\frac{1}{d}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{12}\,.

By Proposition
A.3
, as
\beta,x,I
are fixed, we may consider
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\alpha}\textsc{RS}_{0,I}(\alpha)
. Since
\big(q_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x),\rho_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)\big)
solves
\frac{\partial F_{I}}{\partial q}=\frac{\partial F_{I}}{\partial\rho}=0
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\alpha}\textsc{RS}_{0,I}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial F_{I}}{\partial\alpha}\big(x,q_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x),\rho_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{\partial F_{I}}{\partial q}\big(x,q_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x),\rho_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)\big)\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial\alpha}q_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{\partial F_{I}}{\partial\rho}\big(x,q_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x),\rho_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)\big)\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial\alpha}\rho_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\log\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{W}\big(xs_{i}+\sqrt{q_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)}Z+\sqrt{\rho_{0}(\alpha)-q_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)}W\geq\kappa\big)\Big]\,,

as
s_{i}\sim\mathcal{D}
and as independent of
Z,W
. The Mean Value Theorem now implies that for some
\alpha^{\prime}\in\big[\frac{i-1}{d},\frac{i}{d}\big]
,

\displaystyle\textsc{RS}_{0}\Big(\frac{i}{d}\Big)-\textsc{RS}_{0,I}\Big(\frac{i-1}{d}\Big)

(A.46)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\log\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{W}\big(xs_{i}+\sqrt{q_{0}(\alpha^{\prime},\beta,x)}Z+\sqrt{\rho_{0}(\alpha^{\prime})-q_{0}(\alpha^{\prime},\beta,x)}W\geq\kappa\big)\Big]\,.

Combining (
A.42
), (
A.43
), (
A.44
), (
A.45
), (
A.46
) implies for all
1\leq i\leq n
,

\displaystyle\Big|\phi_{\beta,i,d}(I)-\phi_{\beta,i-1,d}(I)-\Big(\textsc{RS}_{0,I}\Big(\frac{i}{d}\Big)-\textsc{RS}_{0,I}\Big(\frac{i-1}{d}\Big)\Big)\Big|<\frac{3\varepsilon}{8d}\,.

Summing across all such
i
and noting there are at most
n\leq\alpha_{0}d\leq 2d
such
i
yields

\displaystyle\Big|\phi_{\beta,n,d}(I)-\phi_{\beta,0,d}(I)-\Big(\textsc{RS}_{0,I}\Big(\frac{n}{d}\Big)-\textsc{RS}_{0,I}(0)\Big)\Big|<\frac{3\varepsilon}{4}\,.

Last, we combine with Lemma
A.12
, yielding

\displaystyle\Big|\phi_{\beta,n,d}(I)-\Big(\textsc{RS}_{0,I}\Big(\frac{n}{d}\Big)+\frac{1}{2}\log(2\pi e)\Big)\Big|<\varepsilon\,.

This completes the proof of Theorem
A.1
.

A.4
Proof of Theorem
A.2
for posterior

Here we prove Theorem
A.2
with the same argument as the proof of Theorem
A.1
.
Note
u(x)\geq-D(\lambda)(1+|x|)
for
u(x)
from (
3.17
), (
3.18
).
Next, by the same argument as the proof of Proposition
A.14
, we have the following for any
(\beta,x)\in[\beta_{0},\beta_{1}]\times[-1,1]
. For all
\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}\leq\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}_{\textup{\ref{prop:boundedapproxRSeq}}}
, all
d\geq d_{\textup{\ref{prop:boundedapproxRSeq}}}
, and all
n\geq 1
with
n/d=\alpha
,

\displaystyle\big|q_{n,d}-q_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)\big|\,,\,\big|\rho_{n,d}-\rho_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)\big|\leq\delta\,.

(A.47)

Here the proof follows by the same argument as the proof of Proposition
A.14
, using Propositions
A.3
,
A.9
, and
A.11
, but now we do not consider the
u_{k}
or replace
u_{k}
by
\mathbf{1}\{x\geq\kappa\}
as
k\rightarrow\infty
. Here we use that a solution to (
A.30
) solves (
A.11
) where
u(\cdot)
is identical in both systems; this can be seen via Gaussian integration by parts.

Now to prove Theorem
A.1
, we let
H_{1}=H_{1}(\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\lambda)
be an upper bound on the gradient of the continuously differentiable function
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[\log\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\exp u\big(xS+\sqrt{q}Z+\sqrt{\rho-q}W\big)\big]
over the compact set
\big\{(x,\rho,q):-1\leq x\leq 1,0\leq q\leq C_{\textsc{sol}},\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}}\leq\rho\leq C_{\textsc{sol}},\frac{1}{\rho-q}\leq C_{\textsc{sol}}\big\}
. Here we can verify that the function is continuously differentiable as
\rho-q\geq\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}}
, as
u
is twice differentiable, and as
u(x)\geq-D(\lambda)(1+|x|)
. Without loss of generality suppose
H_{1}\geq 1
.

Similarly by Proposition
A.3
, we may let
H_{2}=H_{2}(\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\lambda)
be an upper bound on the gradient of the continuously differentiable
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[\log\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\exp u\big(xS+\sqrt{q_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)}Z+\sqrt{\rho_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)-q_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)}W\big)\big]
on
(\alpha,\beta,x)\in[0,\alpha_{0}]\times[\beta_{0},\beta_{1}]\times[-1,1]
. Here we use that
\rho_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)-q_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)\geq\frac{1}{2C_{\textsc{sol}}}
by Proposition
A.3
.
Now, we let

\displaystyle\delta=\delta(\varepsilon,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\lambda)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\varepsilon}{12H_{1}}\,,

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\textsc{RS}_{0}}=\varepsilon_{\textsc{RS}_{0}}(u,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\lambda)

\displaystyle:=\min\Big\{\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}_{\textup{\ref{prop:boundedapproxRSeq}}}(u,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\lambda),\frac{\varepsilon}{12K_{\textup{\ref{prop:interpolationperconstraint}}}(D,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\lambda)},\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}_{\textup{\ref{lem:interpolationlastcalc}}}(\varepsilon/4)\Big\}\,.

Considering any
0<\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}\leq\varepsilon_{\textsc{RS}_{0}}
as in the statement of this Theorem, we let

\displaystyle d(\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime\prime},\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\lambda)

\displaystyle:=\max\Big\{d_{\textup{\ref{prop:boundedapproxRSeq}}}(\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}),\frac{12H_{2}}{\varepsilon},d_{\textup{\ref{lem:interpolationlastcalc}}}(\varepsilon/4),d_{\textup{\ref{prop:interpolationperconstraint}}}(\varepsilon^{\prime\prime})\Big\}\,.

Consider any
d\geq d(\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime\prime},\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\lambda)
as in the statement of this Theorem. We now consider any
n
such that
\frac{n}{d}\in[0,\alpha_{0}]
.
For any
1\leq i\leq n
, letting
\alpha=\frac{i}{d}
, we obtain by Proposition
A.4
that

\displaystyle\Big|\phi_{\beta,i,d}(I)-\phi_{\beta,i-1,d}(I)-\frac{1}{d}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\log\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\exp u\big(xs_{i}+\sqrt{q_{i,d}}Z+\sqrt{\rho_{i,d}-q_{i,d}}W\big)\Big]\Big|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{12d}\,.

By Proposition
A.11
, we have

\displaystyle\big(q_{i,d},\rho_{i,d}\big)\in[0,C_{\textsc{sol}}]\times\Big[\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}\Big]\,,\,\frac{1}{\rho_{i,d}-q_{i,d}}\leq C_{\textsc{sol}}\,.

Next by (
A.47
), we have

\displaystyle\big|q_{i,d}-q_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)\big|\,,\,\big|\rho_{i,d}-\rho_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)\big|\leq\delta=\frac{\varepsilon}{12H_{1}}\,.

Consequently

\displaystyle\Big|\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\log\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\exp u\big(xs_{i}+\sqrt{q_{i,d}}Z+\sqrt{\rho_{i,d}-q_{i,d}}W\big)\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\log\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\exp u\big(xs_{i}+\sqrt{q_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)}Z+\sqrt{\rho_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)-q_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)}W\big)\Big]\Big|<\frac{\varepsilon}{8}\,.

Finally, we observe that for any
\alpha^{\prime}\in\big[\frac{i-1}{d},\frac{i}{d}\big]
, we have
\big|\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}\big|\leq\frac{1}{d}
, so as
d\geq 12H_{2}/\varepsilon
,

\displaystyle\Big|\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\log\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\exp u\big(xs_{i}+\sqrt{q_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)}Z+\sqrt{\rho_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)-q_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)}W\big)\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\log\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\exp u\big(xs_{i}+\sqrt{q_{0}(\alpha^{\prime},\beta,x)}Z+\sqrt{\rho_{0}(\alpha^{\prime},\beta,x)-q_{0}(\alpha^{\prime},\beta,x)}W\big)\Big]\Big|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{12}\,.

With the above bounds in hand, we finish identically to the proof of Theorem
A.1
, using Proposition
A.3
and Lemma
A.12
. This proves Theorem
A.2
.

Appendix B
Concentration with respect to Gaussian Measure

The goal of this section is to show that the log-partition function concentrates with respect to the Gaussian measure, as stated in Theorem
B.1
for interpolators and Theorem
B.2
for the posterior below.
Recall that in Theorems
A.1
,
A.2
, we computed the value of the free energy, that is the expected normalized log-partition function, corresponding to the interpolators and the posterior respectively. Here, we upgrade these statements to exponentially high-probability concentration of the normalized log-partition function about this same value.

Theorem
B.1
for interpolators:

Recall the definition of the set
U_{i}
interpolating the
i
-th datapoint
i=1,\ldots,n
(equivalently, satisfying each constraint) defined in (
5.5
), and consider
C
, the intersection of all the
U_{i}
. Recalling the definition of
S_{i}
in (
A.1
), these sets are defined by

\displaystyle U_{i}

\displaystyle:=\bigl\{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,:\,S_{i}\geq\kappa\bigr\}\quad\text{and}\quad C:=\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}\,.

(B.1)

In Theorem
A.1
, we showed
\phi_{\beta,n,d}(I)
from (
A.4
) equals
\textsc{RSG}_{I}(n/d,\beta,x)
, where
\phi_{\beta,n,d}(I)
is the normalized logarithm of (
A.3
).
We now show after replacing the mollifier
\sum_{1\leq i\leq n}u\big(\big(s_{i}\theta_{1}+\langle g_{i},\bar{\theta}\rangle\big)/\sqrt{d}\big)
in (
A.3
) by the indicator
\mathbf{1}\{\bigcap_{1\leq i\leq n}U_{i}\}
, the corresponding normalized log-partition-function concentrates exponentially around the same value. Note this hard indicator arises as we want to study the set of interpolators.
We now are in a position to state Theorem
B.1
:

Theorem B.1

(For interpolators)
.

Consider
n
such that
1\leq n\leq\alpha_{0}d
. Then for any
\varepsilon>0
, there is
\varepsilon_{\textsc{RSG}}>0
depending on
\varepsilon,\alpha_{0},\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\kappa,\lambda
such that the following holds. For any
\beta\in[\beta_{0},\beta_{1}]
, recalling the definition of
C
in
(
B.1
)
, define for any interval
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle Z_{\beta}(I)

\displaystyle:=\int\mathbf{1}\big\{\{\theta_{1}/\sqrt{d}\in I\}\cap C\big\}\,\exp\big(-\beta\|\theta\|^{2}\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\theta\,,

(B.2)

Then for any
x\in[-1,1]
and
0<\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}\leq\varepsilon_{\textsc{RSG}}
, letting
I=[x-\varepsilon^{\prime\prime},x+\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}]
, we have for
d
large enough in terms of
\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime\prime},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\kappa,\lambda
that

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}\Bigg(\Big|\frac{1}{d}\log Z_{\beta}(I)-\textsc{RSG}_{I}(n/d,\beta,x)\Big|\geq\varepsilon\Bigg)\leq\exp\big(-d/K\big)\,,

(B.3)

where
\textsc{RSG}_{I}
is defined as in
(
A.13
)
with
\exp u(x)=\mathbf{1}\{x\geq\kappa\}
, and where
K
is large enough in terms of
\varepsilon,\alpha_{0},\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\kappa,\lambda
.

We prove Theorem
B.1
in Appendix
B.1
.
Specifically, we must handle the non-Gaussian signal component to establish several key technical results on stochastic processes and concentration of measure, which establishes the key ‘add one constraint’ style estimate Theorem
B.9
. This result upper bounds the probability that a Gibbs measure places small mass on one side of a given constraint. This is done in Appendix
B.1.1
. To handle the non-Gaussianity, it turns out we need the width
\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}
of
I
to be small enough in terms of
\lambda
but independent of
d
. See the discussion at the start of Appendix
B.1.1
for more details.

Theorem
B.2
for posterior:

We have the following similar result for the posterior, which establishes exponential concentration of the log-partition function about
\textsc{RSG}_{I}
.
Note this Theorem does not involve hard indicators as we are interested in studying the posterior
p_{X,y}
in (
2.7
), which is defined in terms of
u(x)
from (
3.17
), (
3.18
) in the GMM and logistic cases respectively.

Theorem B.2

(For posterior)
.

Consider
n
such that
1\leq n\leq\alpha_{0}d
. Consider
u(x)
from
(
3.17
)
,
(
3.18
)
in the GMM and logistic cases respectively. Then for any
\varepsilon>0
, there is
\varepsilon_{\textsc{RSG}}>0
depending on
\varepsilon,\alpha_{0},\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\lambda
such that the following holds. For any
\beta\in[\beta_{0},\beta_{1}]
, define for any interval
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle Z_{\beta}(I)

\displaystyle:=\int\mathbf{1}\big\{\theta_{1}/\sqrt{d}\in I\big\}\exp\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}u(S_{i})-\beta\|\theta\|^{2}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}\theta\,,

(B.4)

where
S_{i}
is defined as in
(
A.1
)
. Then for any
x\in[-1,1]
and
0<\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}\leq\varepsilon_{\textsc{RSG}}
, letting
I=[x-\varepsilon^{\prime\prime},x+\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}]
, we have for
d
large enough in terms of
\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime\prime},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\lambda
that

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}\Bigg(\Big|\frac{1}{d}\log Z_{\beta}(I)-\textsc{RSG}_{I}(n/d,\beta,x)\Big|\geq\varepsilon\Bigg)\leq\exp\big(-d/K\big)\,,

(B.5)

where
\textsc{RSG}_{I}
is defined as in
(
A.13
)
with this
\exp u(x)
, and where
K
is large enough in terms of
\varepsilon,\alpha_{0},\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\lambda
.

We prove Theorem
B.2
in Appendix
B.2
. Unlike Theorem
B.1
, the proof of Theorem
B.2
is relatively direct as a consequence of concentration of Lipschitz functions of log-concave measures, as stated below. Note that in contrast, such tools are not available to prove Theorem
B.1
due to the hard indicators in the Hamiltonian therein.

Concentration of Lipschitz functions log-concave measures.

We next introduce concentration of Lipschitz functions of strongly log-concave measures in the form given in Theorem
B.3
below, originally credited to B. Maurey. See also the text of Bakry, Gentil, and Ledoux
[
BGL13
]
, Chapter 5. This fact allows us to establish the high-probability concentration bounds of both Theorem
B.1
and Theorem
B.2
. We will also use this fact crucially in proving Proposition
A.6
in Appendix
D.3
.

Note that this Theorem holds when
\psi
can take on the value
-\infty
, that is when
\psi
is supported on a convex set that is a strict subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, as noted in (3.21) of
[
Tal10
]
. Technically, this result is written in
[
Tal10
]
when
f
is
H
-Lipschitz on all of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
; the result writte in
[
Tal10
]
immediately implies the following by the McShane-Whitney Extension Theorem
[
McS34
,
Whi34
]
.

Theorem B.3

(Theorem 3.1.4,
[
Tal10
]
)
.

Consider a measure
\mu(\theta)\propto\exp\Big(\psi(\theta)\Big)
defined over a convex set
C
such that for some
\beta>0
, we have

\frac{1}{2}\Big(\psi(\theta_{1})+\psi(\theta_{2})\Big)-\psi\Big(\frac{\theta_{1}+\theta_{2}}{2}\Big)\leq-\beta\Big\|\frac{\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}}{2}\Big\|^{2}~.

(Note
\psi
can take on the value
-\infty
in the above.)
Then for any set
C\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\int\exp\Big(\frac{\beta}{2}d^{2}(x,C)\Big)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\leq\frac{1}{\mu(C)}\,,

where
d(x,C)=\inf\{d(x,y):y\in C\}
is the distance from
x
to
C
. Moreover, if
f
is a function on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with Lipschitz constant
H
on the support of
\mu
, i.e., for all
x,y\in\text{supp}(\mu)
we have
\|f(x)-f(y)\|\leq H\|x-y\|
, then

\int\exp\Big(\frac{\beta}{8H^{2}}\big(f(x)-\int f\,\mathrm{d}\mu\big)^{2}\Big)~\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\leq 4~,

and

\text{ for all }k\geq 1\,,\,\quad\int\left(f(x)-\int f\mathrm{d}\mu\right)^{2k}\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\leq 4\left(\frac{8kH^{2}}{\beta}\right)^{k}\,.

B.1
Proof of Theorem
B.1
for interpolators

Here throughout Appendix
B.1
, we let
Z_{\beta}(I)
be as per (
B.2
). The goal of this section is to prove Theorem
B.1
, and to this end there are two main steps.

1.

First, we establish high-probability concentration of the normalized logarithm of (
A.3
) around its expectation
\textsc{RSG}_{I}(n/d,\beta,x)
, and more generally, concentration of the normalized free energy around its expectation. In particular, this also applies for (
B.2
).
This is done via a technically involved application of Bernstein’s Inequality in Lemma
B.12
.

2.

Second, we show that the mollifier
u(\cdot)
in (
A.3
) can be eliminated without significantly changing the expectation of its logarithm, as discussed in the proof ideas in Section
5
. This is a delicate step done in Lemma
B.14
.

With both of these steps in hand, we then prove Theorem
B.1
. All these steps are done in Appendix
B.1.2
. Preliminary steps on stochastic processes and concentration of measure necessary to prove these Lemmas – specifically, the key ‘add one constraint’ style estimate, Theorem
B.9
– are presented in Appendix
B.1.1
. These initial steps are important but highly technical.

B.1.1
Preliminaries

Central to the subsequent proof in Appendix
B.1.2
will be the combination of the following Theorem
B.9
and Lemma
B.10
. These are important results on concentration of measure that let us control hard-to-bound quantities which involve the addition of the
i
-th datapoint or constraint, which behave at an exponential scale. In turn, these results hinge on the key Lemma
B.7
on stochastic processes, which is proved by several comparison inequalities.

Although similar in spirit, the statements and proofs differ from those in Section 8.2 of
[
Tal11
]
, as handling the non-Gaussian ‘signal’ component
s_{i}\sim\mathcal{D}
of the disorder requires more technical ingredients. Notably, these results hinge on being able to choose the width
\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}
of
I
small enough in terms of
\lambda
, but still independently of
d
, so that for
d\geq d(\varepsilon^{\prime\prime})
, various partition functions are lower bounded by
\exp(-O(d))
where
O(\cdot)
is independent of
\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}
. In particular, see the assumption
Z\geq\exp(-ad)
in Theorem
B.9
; this assumption applies in the proof of Theorem
B.1
with exponentially high probability by Lemma
B.12
.

Recall as per Appendix
A
that
K_{\textsc{signal}},\beta_{\textsc{signal}}
depend on
\lambda
. However the following bounds depend delicately on these parameters, so here in Appendix
B.1.1
and only here, we will make the dependence on
K_{\textsc{signal}},\beta_{\textsc{signal}}
fully explicit.

We begin with several preliminary results on stochastic processes.

Lemma B.4
.

Let
\xi_{1}
and
(X^{\prime}_{\ell})_{1\leq\ell\leq L}
be a collection of real valued random variables where
\xi_{1}
has zero mean, and
\xi_{1}
and the collection
(X^{\prime}_{\ell})_{1\leq\ell\leq L}
are independent (but the elements of
(X^{\prime}_{\ell})_{1\leq\ell\leq L}
are not necessarily independent of each other).
Let
u_{1},\ldots,u_{L}\in\mathbb{R}
be fixed such that
|u_{\ell}-x|\leq\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}
for all
1\leq\ell\leq L
for some
x\in\mathbb{R}
.
Define
X=(X_{\ell})_{\ell\leq L}
by
X_{\ell}:=u_{\ell}\xi_{1}+X^{\prime}_{\ell}
for all
1\leq\ell\leq L
.
Then for all
t\geq 0
,

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\log\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\exp(tX_{\ell})\Big]\geq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\log\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\exp(tX^{\prime}_{\ell})\Big]\,.

Proof.

Fix
s\in\mathbb{R}
. Since the function
F(x):=\log\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\exp(tx_{\ell})
for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{L}
is convex, we have

F(X)\geq F(X^{\prime})+\langle\nabla F(X^{\prime}),X-X^{\prime}\rangle\,.

Let
u=(u_{\ell})_{\ell=1}^{L}
. The expectation of the second term on the right-hand side is
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[\xi_{1}\langle\nabla F(X^{\prime}),u\rangle\big]=0
as
\xi_{1}
is zero mean and independent of
X^{\prime}
, proving the Lemma.
∎

Lemma B.5

(Slepian’s Inequality, in the form of Proposition 8.2.2,
[
Tal11
]
)
.

Consider two jointly Gaussian families
(U_{\ell})_{\ell\leq L}
,
(V_{\ell})_{\ell\leq L}
, and suppose that for all
1\leq\ell\leq L
we have
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[U_{\ell}^{2}]\geq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[V_{\ell}^{2}]
, and for all
1\leq\ell_{1}\neq\ell_{2}\leq L
we have
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[U_{\ell_{1}}U_{\ell_{2}}]\leq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[V_{\ell_{1}}V_{\ell_{2}}]
. Then

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\log\sum_{1\leq\ell\leq L}\exp(tU_{\ell})\Big]\geq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\log\sum_{1\leq\ell\leq L}\exp(tV_{\ell})\Big]\,.

Lemma B.6

(Proposition 8.2.3,
[
Tal11
]
)
.

There exists a
K_{\textup{\ref{lem:lowerboundsoftmaxindependent}}}>0
with the following property. Letting
(W_{\ell})_{\ell\leq L}\sim N(0,1)
be i.i.d., for
K_{\textup{\ref{lem:lowerboundsoftmaxindependent}}}\leq t\leq\sqrt{\log L}/K_{\textup{\ref{lem:lowerboundsoftmaxindependent}}}
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\log\sum_{\ell\leq L}\exp(tW_{\ell})\Big]\geq\log L+\frac{t^{2}}{5}\,.

We will now prove the crucial Lemma
B.7
that combines the above results. Its aim is to upper bound the probability of the following event: that for
L
replicas, the number of
\ell,1\leq\ell\leq L
such that
\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\big(\theta_{1}^{\ell}s+\langle\bar{\theta}^{\ell},\bar{g}\rangle\big)\geq\kappa
is small. The proof is involved and spans the next several pages. We will then leverage Lemma
B.7
to prove the important Theorem
B.9
.

Lemma B.7
.

Consider any
x\in[-1,1]
and
0\leq c_{1}<c_{2}<c_{3}
with

\displaystyle c_{3}\geq\frac{1}{c_{2}-c_{1}}\quad\text{and}\quad c_{1}\geq x^{2}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[s^{2}]\,.

(B.6)

Then there exists
K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}\geq 1
depending on
c_{3},K_{\textsc{signal}},\beta_{\textsc{signal}}
and
K^{\prime}_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}\geq\max\big\{12c_{3}K_{\textsc{signal}},1\big\}
depending on
c_{3},K_{\textsc{signal}}
satisfying the following.
Consider
0<\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}\leq\frac{1}{K^{\prime}_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}}
and

\displaystyle\theta^{1},\ldots,\theta^{L}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\quad\text{such that}\quad\theta_{1}^{\ell}/\sqrt{d}\in[x-\varepsilon^{\prime\prime},x+\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}]\,\forall\,1\leq\ell\leq L\,.

(B.7)

Consider independent
s\sim\mathcal{D}
,
\bar{g}\sim N(0,I_{d-1})
, and a family of random variables
(X_{\ell})_{\ell\leq L}
defined by

\displaystyle X_{\ell}=\frac{\theta_{1}^{\ell}s+\langle\bar{\theta}^{\ell},\bar{g}\rangle}{\sqrt{d}}\,,

such that

\displaystyle c_{2}\leq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[X^{2}_{\ell}\big]\leq c_{3}\text{ for all }1\leq\ell\leq L\quad\text{ and }\quad\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[X_{\ell_{1}}X_{\ell_{2}}\big]\leq c_{1}\text{ for all }1\leq\ell_{1}\neq\ell_{2}\leq L\,.

(B.8)

Then for
K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}\leq t\leq\sqrt{\log L}/K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\#\Big\{\ell\leq L\,:\,X_{\ell}\geq\frac{t}{K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}}\Big\}\leq L\exp\big(-K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}t^{2}\big)\Big)\leq K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}\exp\Big(-\frac{t^{2}}{K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}}\Big)\,.

(B.9)

Consequently, for all
L^{-1/K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}}\leq t^{\prime}\leq\exp\big(-K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}\max\{1,\kappa\}^{2}\big)
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\#\Big\{\ell\leq L\,:\,X_{\ell}\geq\kappa\Big\}\leq Lt^{\prime}\Big)\leq K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}\,t^{\prime 1/K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}^{2}}\,.

(B.10)

Proof.

We will focus on proving (
B.9
). The proof of (
B.10
) then directly follows from (
B.9
).

Proof of (
B.9
).

The idea behind proving (
B.9
) is to apply the second moment method to
\exp(tX_{\ell})
. Specifically, let us consider
t
such that
K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}\leq t\leq\sqrt{\log L}/K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}
and define the following event
E
:

\displaystyle E

\displaystyle:=\Big\{\sum_{1\leq\ell\leq L}\exp(tX_{\ell})\geq L\exp\Big(\frac{t^{2}}{60c_{3}}\Big)\Big\}

(B.11)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\cap\Big\{\sum_{1\leq\ell\leq L}\exp(2tX_{\ell})\leq L\exp\big((1+K(c_{3},K_{\textsc{signal}}))t^{2}\big)\,\Big\}\,.

(B.12)

The main claim is that

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(E)\geq 1-2\exp\Big(-\frac{t^{2}}{K(c_{3},\beta_{\textsc{signal}})}\Big)-\exp\big(-t^{2}\big)\,.

(B.13)

We will prove (
B.13
) at the end of the proof of this Lemma. Assuming (
B.13
) for now, let us prove (
B.9
).
Let
\mathcal{U}[L]
denote the uniform measure on
[L]
.
Consider any
(X_{1},\ldots,X_{L})\in E
.
By definition of
E
, we have from the Paley-Zygmund Inequality

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\ell\sim\mathcal{U}[L]}\Big(\exp(tX_{\ell})\geq\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\sim\mathcal{U}[L]}\Big[\exp(tX_{\ell})\Big]\Big)\geq\frac{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\sim\mathcal{U}[L]}\Big[\exp(tX_{\ell})\Big]^{2}}{4\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\sim\mathcal{U}[L]}\Big[\exp(2tX_{\ell})\Big]}\,.

(B.14)

Now, we note
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\sim\mathcal{U}[L]}\Big[\exp(tX_{\ell})\Big]=\frac{1}{L}\sum_{1\leq\ell\leq L}\exp(tX_{\ell})
for any
\in\mathbb{R}
.
By definition of
E
, specifically (
B.11
), we have for
t\geq K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}
that

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\sim\mathcal{U}[L]}\Big[\exp(tX_{\ell})\Big]\geq\exp\Big(\frac{t^{2}}{60c_{3}}\Big)\geq 2\exp\Big(\frac{t^{2}}{80c_{3}}\Big)\geq 4\,.

Consequently,
(X_{1},\ldots,X_{L})\in E
implies the following by (
B.14
) and (
B.12
):

\displaystyle\frac{1}{L}\#\Big\{\ell\leq L\,:\,X_{\ell}\geq\frac{t}{80c_{3}}\Big\}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\ell\sim\mathcal{U}[L]}\Big(\exp(tX_{\ell})\geq\exp\Big(\frac{t^{2}}{80c_{3}}\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle\geq\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{\ell\sim\mathcal{U}[L]}\Big(\exp(tX_{\ell})\geq\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\sim\mathcal{U}[L]}\Big[\exp(tX_{\ell})\Big]\Big)

\displaystyle\geq\exp\big(-(1+K(c_{3},K_{\textsc{signal}}))t^{2}\big)\,.

Combining the above display with (
B.13
), we obtain

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\#\Big\{\ell\leq L\,:\,X_{\ell}\geq\frac{t}{80c_{3}}\Big\}\geq L\exp\big(-(1+K(c_{3},K_{\textsc{signal}}))t^{2}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle\quad\geq\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(E)

\displaystyle\quad\geq 1-2\exp\Big(-\frac{t^{2}}{K(c_{3},\beta_{\textsc{signal}})}\Big)-\exp\big(-t^{2}\big)

\displaystyle\quad\geq 1-3\exp\Big(-\frac{t^{2}}{K(c_{3},\beta_{\textsc{signal}})}\Big)\,.

Thus for
K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}=K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}(c_{3},K_{\textsc{signal}},\beta_{\textsc{signal}})
large enough, we obtain from the above that

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\#\Big\{\ell\leq L\,:\,X_{\ell}\geq\frac{t}{K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}}\Big\}\leq L\exp\big(-K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}t^{2}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle\quad\leq\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\#\Big\{\ell\leq L\,:\,X_{\ell}\geq\frac{t}{80c_{3}}\Big\}\leq L\exp\big(-K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}t^{2}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle\quad\leq\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\#\Big\{\ell\leq L\,:\,X_{\ell}\geq\frac{t}{80c_{3}}\Big\}\leq L\exp\big(-(1+K(c_{3},K_{\textsc{signal}}))t^{2}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle\quad\leq 3\exp\Big(-\frac{t^{2}}{K(c_{3},\beta_{\textsc{signal}})}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad\leq K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}\exp\Big(-\frac{t^{2}}{K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}}\Big)\,,

proving (
B.9
).

Proof of (
B.10
).

Note by monotonicity in
\kappa
, it suffices to prove the result for
\kappa\geq 0
.
First suppose
\kappa\geq 1
. Consider any
t
such that
K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}\kappa\leq t\leq\sqrt{\log L}/K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}
. As
\kappa\geq 1
, we may apply (
B.9
), which yields

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\#\Big\{\ell\leq L\,:\,X_{\ell}\geq\kappa\Big\}\leq L\exp\big(-K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}t^{2}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\#\Big\{\ell\leq L\,:\,X_{\ell}\geq\frac{t}{K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}}\Big\}\leq L\exp\big(-K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}t^{2}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle\leq K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}\exp\Big(-\frac{t^{2}}{K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}}\Big)\,.

Letting
t^{\prime}=\exp\big(-K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}t^{2}\big)
, we obtain for
L^{-1/K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}}\leq t^{\prime}\leq\exp\big(-K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}^{3}\kappa^{2}\big)\leq\exp\big(-K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}\kappa^{2}\big)
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\#\Big\{\ell\leq L\,:\,X_{\ell}\geq\kappa\Big\}\leq Lt^{\prime}\Big)\leq K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}t^{\prime 1/K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}^{2}}

Now suppose
\kappa\in[0,1]
. Consider any
t
such that
K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}\leq t\leq\sqrt{\log L}/K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}
. Since
\kappa\leq 1\leq\frac{t}{K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}}
, we obtain from (
B.9
) that

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\#\Big\{\ell\leq L\,:\,X_{\ell}\geq\kappa\Big\}\leq L\exp\big(-K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}t^{2}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\#\Big\{\ell\leq L\,:\,X_{\ell}\geq 1\Big\}\leq L\exp\big(-K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}t^{2}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\#\Big\{\ell\leq L\,:\,X_{\ell}\geq\frac{t}{K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}}\Big\}\leq L\exp\big(-K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}t^{2}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle\leq K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}\exp\Big(-\frac{t^{2}}{K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}}\Big)\,.

The result now follows from setting
t^{\prime}=\exp\big(-K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}t^{2}\big)
as in the
\kappa\geq 1
case above. As remarked earlier, the result for
\kappa<0
follows from the
\kappa=0
case. This proves (
B.25
).

Proof of (
B.13
).

We will lower bound the probabilities of each of the events (
B.11
), (
B.12
) defining
E
separately and then take a Union Bound. For brevity, consider the vector
X=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{L})\in\mathbb{R}^{L}
and let

\displaystyle F(X):=\log\sum_{1\leq\ell\leq L}\exp(tX_{\ell})=\log\sum_{1\leq\ell\leq L}\exp\Big(t\cdot\frac{\theta_{1}^{\ell}s+\langle\bar{\theta}^{\ell},\bar{g}\rangle}{\sqrt{d}}\Big)\,.

First we lower bound the probability of (
B.11
). Note
F(X)
can be viewed a function of
g^{\prime}=(s,\bar{g})^{T}
, which has
\min\{1,\beta_{\textsc{signal}}\}
strongly log-concave law.
Letting
w_{\ell}(g^{\prime})\propto\exp\big(t\langle\theta^{\ell},g^{\prime}\rangle/\sqrt{d}\big)
be weights summing to 1 for
\ell,1\leq\ell\leq L
, a direct calculation shows that the gradient
\nabla_{g^{\prime}}F
of
F
w.r.t.
g^{\prime}
is
t\sum_{\ell\leq L}w_{\ell}(g^{\prime})\theta^{\ell}/\sqrt{d}
. Also note as
\bar{g}\sim N(0,I_{d-1})
independent of
s
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[X_{\ell}^{2}]=\frac{(\theta_{1}^{\ell})^{2}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[s^{2}]+\|\bar{\theta}^{\ell}\|^{2}}{d}=\frac{\|\theta^{\ell}\|^{2}+(\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[s^{2}]-1)(\theta_{1}^{\ell})^{2}}{d}\,.

Consequently since
\theta_{1}^{\ell}/\sqrt{d}\in I
and
I\subseteq[-2,2]
,

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{g^{\prime}}F\|^{2}\leq t^{2}\max_{\ell}\|\theta^{\ell}\|^{2}/d=t^{2}\max_{\ell}\Big(\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[X_{\ell}^{2}]+(1-\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[s^{2}])\cdot\frac{(\theta_{1}^{\ell})^{2}}{d}\Big)\leq t^{2}(c_{3}+4)\,.

Applying Theorem
B.3
on the concentration of Lipschitz functions of strongly log-concave measures now gives for all
t^{\prime}>0
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\big|F(X)-\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[F(X)\big]\big|\geq t^{\prime}\Big)\leq 2\exp\Big(-\frac{t^{\prime 2}}{K(c_{3},\beta_{\textsc{signal}})t^{2}}\Big)\,.

(B.15)

We now aim to lower bound
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[F(X)\big]
. Define

\displaystyle u_{\ell}:=\frac{\theta_{1}^{\ell}}{\sqrt{d}}\,,\,\xi_{1}:=s-\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[s]\,,\,X^{\prime}_{\ell}:=\frac{\theta_{1}^{\ell}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[s]}{\sqrt{d}}+\frac{\langle\bar{\theta}^{\ell},\bar{g}\rangle}{\sqrt{d}}\,.

Note that
X_{\ell}=u_{\ell}\xi_{1}+X^{\prime}_{\ell}
, that
\xi_{1}
has mean 0, and that
\xi_{1},X^{\prime}_{\ell}
are independent as the
\theta^{\ell}
here are fixed and as
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[s],s
are independent. Lemma
B.4
now gives

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[F(X)\big]

\displaystyle\geq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\log\sum_{1\leq\ell\leq L}\exp(tX^{\prime}_{\ell})\Big]\,.

(B.16)

Recall
\big|\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[s]\big|\leq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[s^{2}]^{1/2}\leq K_{\textsc{signal}}^{1/2}
, and note
\big|\theta_{1}^{\ell}/\sqrt{d}\big|\leq 2
for all
\ell
as
I\subseteq[-2,2]
. Thus

\displaystyle X^{\prime}_{\ell}\geq\bar{X}_{\ell}-\big|\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[s]\big|\cdot\max_{1\leq\ell\leq L}\big|\theta_{1}^{\ell}/\sqrt{d}\big|\geq\bar{X}_{\ell}-2K_{\textsc{signal}}^{1/2}\quad\text{where}\quad\bar{X}_{\ell}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\langle\bar{\theta}^{\ell},\bar{g}\rangle\,.

Note
\bar{X}_{\ell}
is a centered Gaussian family. Combining the above display with (
B.16
) gives

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[F(X)\big]

\displaystyle\geq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\log\sum_{1\leq\ell\leq L}\exp(t\bar{X}_{\ell})\Big]-2tK_{\textsc{signal}}^{1/2}\,.

(B.17)

We now observe that as
\bar{g}\sim N(0,I_{d-1})
independent of
s
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[X_{\ell}^{2}]

\displaystyle=\frac{(\theta_{1}^{\ell})^{2}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[s^{2}]+\|\bar{\theta}^{\ell}\|^{2}}{d}=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\bar{X}_{\ell}^{2}]+\frac{(\theta_{1}^{\ell})^{2}}{d}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[s^{2}]\,,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[X_{\ell_{1}}X_{\ell_{2}}]

\displaystyle=\frac{\theta_{1}^{\ell_{1}}\theta_{1}^{\ell_{2}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[s^{2}]+\langle\bar{\theta}^{\ell_{1}},\bar{\theta}^{\ell_{2}}\rangle}{d}=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\bar{X}_{\ell_{1}}\bar{X}_{\ell_{2}}]+\frac{\theta_{1}^{\ell_{1}}\theta_{1}^{\ell_{2}}}{d}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[s^{2}]\,.

As
\theta_{1}^{\ell}/\sqrt{d}\in[x-\varepsilon^{\prime\prime},x+\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}]
, it follows that
\big|\frac{(\theta_{1}^{\ell})^{2}}{d}-x^{2}\big|,\big|\frac{\theta_{1}^{\ell_{1}}\theta_{1}^{\ell_{2}}}{d}-x^{2}\big|\leq 3\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}
and therefore

\displaystyle\big|\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\bar{X}_{\ell}^{2}]-\big(\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[X_{\ell}^{2}]-x^{2}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[s^{2}])\big|\,,\,\big|\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\bar{X}_{\ell_{1}}\bar{X}_{\ell_{2}}]-\big(\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[X_{\ell_{1}}X_{\ell_{2}}]-x^{2}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[s^{2}]\big)\big|\leq 3\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}K_{\textsc{signal}}\,.

(B.18)

Let

\displaystyle\bar{c}_{1}:=c_{1}-x^{2}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[s^{2}]+3\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}K_{\textsc{signal}}\,,\,\bar{c}_{2}:=c_{2}-x^{2}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[s^{2}]-3\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}K_{\textsc{signal}}\,.

It follows by (
B.18
) that for
\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}\leq\frac{1}{K^{\prime}_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}(c_{3},K_{\textsc{signal}})}\leq\frac{1}{12K_{\textsc{signal}}c_{3}}
, because
c_{2}-c_{1}\geq\frac{1}{c_{3}}
, we have

\displaystyle\bar{c}_{2}-\bar{c}_{1}

\displaystyle\geq(c_{2}-x^{2}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[s^{2}])-(c_{1}-x^{2}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[s^{2}])-6\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}K_{\textsc{signal}}\geq c_{2}-c_{1}-\frac{1}{2c_{3}}\geq\frac{1}{2c_{3}}\,,

(B.19)

\displaystyle\bar{c}_{1}

\displaystyle>c_{1}-x^{2}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[s^{2}]\geq 0\,,

(B.20)

where the last step uses the condition (
B.6
). Additionally by (
B.18
), we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\bar{X}_{\ell_{1}}\bar{X}_{\ell_{2}}]

\displaystyle\leq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[X_{\ell_{1}}X_{\ell_{2}}]-x^{2}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[s^{2}]+3\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}K_{\textsc{signal}}=\bar{c}_{1}\,,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\bar{X}_{\ell}^{2}]

\displaystyle\geq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[X_{\ell}^{2}]-x^{2}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[s^{2}]-3\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}K_{\textsc{signal}}=\bar{c}_{2}\,.

We now consider i.i.d.
Z,(W_{\ell})_{\ell\leq L}\sim N(0,1)
. By (
B.19
), (
B.20
), we may define the centered Gaussian family
(V_{\ell})_{\ell=1}^{L}
by

\displaystyle V_{\ell}:=Z\sqrt{\bar{c}_{1}}+W_{\ell}\sqrt{\bar{c}_{2}-\bar{c}_{1}}\,.

Thus,
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[V_{\ell}^{2}]=\bar{c}_{2}\leq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\bar{X}_{\ell}^{2}]
and
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[V_{\ell_{1}}V_{\ell_{2}}]=\bar{c}_{1}\geq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\bar{X}_{\ell_{1}}\bar{X}_{\ell_{2}}]
for
\ell_{1}\neq\ell_{2}
.
Consequently as
\bar{X}_{\ell}
is a centered Gaussian family, the conditions of Lemma
B.5
apply. Combining it with (
B.17
) gives

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[F(X)\big]

\displaystyle\geq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\log\sum_{1\leq\ell\leq L}\exp(t\bar{X}_{\ell})\Big]-2tK_{\textsc{signal}}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\geq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\log\sum_{1\leq\ell\leq L}\exp(tV_{\ell})\Big]-2tK_{\textsc{signal}}^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\log\sum_{1\leq\ell\leq L}\exp(t\sqrt{\bar{c}_{2}-\bar{c}_{1}}W_{\ell})\Big]-2tK_{\textsc{signal}}^{1/2}\,.

Take
K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}=K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}(c_{3},K_{\textsc{signal}},\beta_{\textsc{signal}})\geq K_{\textup{\ref{lem:lowerboundsoftmaxindependent}}}\sqrt{2c_{3}}\geq\frac{K_{\textup{\ref{lem:lowerboundsoftmaxindependent}}}}{\sqrt{\bar{c}_{2}-\bar{c}_{1}}}
, where the last inequality follows from (
B.19
).
Thus we may apply Lemma
B.6
with
t\sqrt{\bar{c}_{2}-\bar{c}_{1}}
in place of
t
, which gives that for
t\geq K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}=K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}(c_{3},K_{\textsc{signal}},\beta_{\textsc{signal}})
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[F(X)\big]

\displaystyle\geq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\log\sum_{1\leq\ell\leq L}\exp(t\sqrt{\bar{c}_{2}-\bar{c}_{1}}W_{\ell})\Big]-2tK_{\textsc{signal}}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\geq\log L+\frac{(\bar{c}_{2}-\bar{c}_{1})t^{2}}{5}-2tK_{\textsc{signal}}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\geq\log L+\frac{t^{2}}{20c_{3}}-2tK_{\textsc{signal}}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\geq\log L+\frac{t^{2}}{30c_{3}}\,,

where we use (
B.19
) to lower bound
\bar{c}_{2}-\bar{c}_{1}
.
Combining with (
B.15
) gives

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\sum_{1\leq\ell\leq L}\exp(tX_{\ell})\geq L\exp\Big(\frac{t^{2}}{60c_{3}}\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle\geq 1-\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}\Big(F(X)\leq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[F(X)\big]-\frac{t^{2}}{60c_{3}}\Big)

(B.21)

\displaystyle\geq 1-2\exp\Big(-\frac{t^{2}}{K(c_{3},\beta_{\textsc{signal}})}\Big)\,.

This lower bounds the probability of (
B.11
).

Next, we lower bound the probability of (
B.12
) as follows. As each
X_{\ell}=\frac{\theta^{\ell}_{1}s+\langle\bar{\theta}^{\ell},\bar{g}\rangle}{\sqrt{d}}
has identical law and as
s,\bar{g}
are independent,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\sum_{1\leq\ell\leq L}\exp(2tX_{\ell})\Big]=L\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[\exp(2tX_{1})\big]=L\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\exp\Big(\frac{2t\theta^{1}_{1}}{\sqrt{d}s}\Big)\Big]\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\exp\Big(\frac{2t\langle\bar{\theta}^{1},\bar{g}\rangle}{\sqrt{d}}\Big)\Big]\,.

(B.22)

Standard calculations on the MGF of a Gaussian yield

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\exp\big(\frac{2t\langle\bar{\theta}^{1},\bar{g}\rangle}{\sqrt{d}}\big)\Big]=\exp\Big(\frac{2t^{2}\|\bar{\theta}^{1}\|^{2}}{d}\Big)\,.

(B.23)

Next we have
\frac{2t\theta^{1}_{1}}{\sqrt{d}}s\leq\frac{t^{2}(\theta^{1}_{1})^{2}K_{\textsc{signal}}}{d}+\frac{s^{2}}{K_{\textsc{signal}}}
, therefore as
s^{2}
is
K_{\textsc{signal}}
-sub-Exponential,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\exp\Big(\frac{2t\theta^{1}_{1}s}{\sqrt{d}}\Big)\Big]\leq\exp\Big(\frac{t^{2}(\theta^{1}_{1})^{2}K_{\textsc{signal}}}{d}\Big)\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\exp\Big(\frac{s^{2}}{K_{\textsc{signal}}}\Big)\Big]\leq 2\exp\Big(\frac{t^{2}(\theta^{1}_{1})^{2}K_{\textsc{signal}}}{d}\Big)\,.

(B.24)

Since
c_{3}\geq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[X_{\ell}^{2}\big]=\frac{(\theta^{\ell}_{1})^{2}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[s^{2}]+\|\bar{\theta}^{\ell}\|^{2}}{d}
and as
\theta_{1}^{1}/\sqrt{d}\in I\subseteq[-2,2]
, it follows that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\|\theta^{1}\|^{2}\leq c_{3}+\frac{(\theta_{1}^{1})^{2}}{d}\leq c_{3}+4\,.

Combining the above bound with (
B.22
), (
B.23
), (
B.24
) gives

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\sum_{1\leq\ell\leq L}\exp(2tX_{\ell})\Big]\leq 2L\exp\Big(\frac{t^{2}K(K_{\textsc{signal}})\|\theta^{1}\|^{2}}{d}\Big)\leq L\exp\big(K(c_{3},K_{\textsc{signal}})t^{2}\big)\,,

where we used that
t\geq K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}=K_{\textup{\ref{lem:keyconcentrationgaussianconversion}}}(c_{3},K_{\textsc{signal}},\beta_{\textsc{signal}})
.
Thus by Markov’s Inequality,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\sum_{1\leq\ell\leq L}\exp(2tX_{\ell})\leq L\exp\big((1+K(c_{3},K_{\textsc{signal}}))t^{2}\big)\Big)\geq 1-\exp(-t^{2})\,.

(B.25)

This lower bounds the probability of (
B.12
). Combining (
B.21
), (
B.25
) proves (
B.13
), thus completing the proof of Lemma
B.7
.
∎

We also cite the following fact from
[
Tal11
]
. Technically, in
[
Tal11
]
, Theorem
B.8
is stated with
\theta
in place of
\bar{\theta}
in the conclusion (
B.26
), (
B.27
), however the proof is identical.

Theorem B.8

(Corollary of the proof of Theorem 8.2.7,
[
Tal11
]
)
.

Consider a concave
U\leq 0
defined on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and a convex set
C\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For
\beta\in[\beta_{0},\beta_{1}]
, define a measure
G_{C}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
as follows, where
Z^{\prime}
denotes the appropriate normalization constant:

\text{ for all }B\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}\,,\,G_{C}(B)=\frac{1}{Z^{\prime}}\int\mathbf{1}\{B\cap C\}\exp\Big(U(\theta)-\beta\|\theta\|^{2}\Big)\mathrm{d}\theta\,.

Assume that for some
a>0
we have

Z^{\prime}\geq\exp(-ad)\,.

Then for some
0\leq c^{\prime}_{1}<c^{\prime}_{2}<c^{\prime}_{3}
with
c^{\prime}_{3}\geq\frac{1}{c^{\prime}_{2}-c^{\prime}_{1}}
, where
c^{\prime}_{3}
only depends on
a,\beta_{0},\beta_{1}
, we have that
G_{C}
satisfies:

\displaystyle G_{C}\Bigl(\big\{\theta:c^{\prime}_{2}d\leq\|\bar{\theta}\|^{2}\leq c^{\prime}_{3}d\big\}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\geq 1-\exp\Bigl(-\frac{d}{c^{\prime}_{3}}\Bigr)\,,

(B.26)

\displaystyle G_{C}^{\otimes 2}\Bigl(\big\{(\theta^{1},\theta^{2}):|\langle\bar{\theta}^{1},\bar{\theta}^{2}\rangle|\leq c^{\prime}_{1}d\big\}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\geq 1-\exp\Bigl(-\frac{d}{c^{\prime}_{3}}\Bigr)\,.

(B.27)

Proof.

This follows from the exact same proof of Theorem 8.2.7,
[
Tal11
]
, except we now consider
R_{1,1}=\frac{1}{d}\|\bar{\theta}\|^{2},R_{1,2}=\frac{1}{d}\langle\bar{\theta}_{1},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle
as we do throughout this paper, as per (
A.16
). Following the same proof as therein, it is shown that (
B.26
), (
B.27
) hold where we can take
c^{\prime}_{3}=\frac{4}{d^{\prime 2}}
where
d^{\prime}=\exp\big(-2(a+a^{2}/4\beta+3)\big)
. Note now rather than following the proof of (3.26) and Theorem 3.1.11 in
[
Tal10
]
, we follow the proof of Lemma
B.19
and Proposition
D.5
, directly using the bound
Z^{\prime}\geq\exp(-ad)
rather than Lemma
D.9
. (There is no dependence on
D
here as dependence on
D
in the proof of Lemma
B.19
, Proposition
D.5
only came due to Lemma
D.9
.) The rest of the proof is identical to the proof of Theorem 8.2.7 in
[
Tal11
]
. Note that
c^{\prime}_{1}\geq 0
as
\langle R_{1,2}\rangle\geq 0
.
∎

Now we prove the following very useful result that upper bounds the probability that a Gibbs measure places small mass on one side of a random hyperplane in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where the hyperplane has law identical to the constraints (the probability is over the hyperplane). We will use this result repeatedly next in Appendix
B.1.2
.

Theorem B.9
.

Consider a concave
U\leq 0
defined on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and a convex set
C\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Consider any
x\in[-1,1]
and let
I=[x-\varepsilon^{\prime\prime},x+\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}]
for some
\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}>0
. For
\beta\in[\beta_{0},\beta_{1}]
, define a measure
G_{C}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
