\displaystyle=\int_{C}(f-m)^{2k}\mathrm{d}\gamma=\gamma(C)\int_{C}(f-m)^{2k}\mathrm{d}\gamma^{\prime}\leq\Big(\frac{K(D,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\kappa,\lambda)k}{d}\Big)^{k}\,.

We now combine Lemma
D.9
and Lemma
B.19
together as in the proof of (
D.18
), and then take expectations and applying Lemma
D.8
. Using that
f=R_{1,1}
or
R_{1,2}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[f^{4k}\big]\leq K(D,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\kappa,\lambda)^{k}\,.

Next we upper bound
m
. By Lemma
D.9
and Lemma
B.19
, we have
|f|\leq\frac{K(D,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\kappa,\lambda)B^{\star}}{d}
. Hence by definition of
C^{\star}
,

\displaystyle|m|=\Big|\int f\mathrm{d}\gamma^{\prime}\Big|\leq K(D,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\kappa,\lambda)\,.

It follows from the above two displays that
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[(f-m)^{4k}\big]\leq K(D,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\kappa,\lambda)^{k}
. Thus

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[(f-m)^{2k}\big]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[(f-m)^{2k}\mathbf{1}\{C\}\big]+\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[(f-m)^{2k}\mathbf{1}\{C^{c}\}\big]

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\frac{K(D,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\kappa,\lambda)k}{d}\Big)^{k}+\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[(f-m)^{4k}\big]\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(C^{c})

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\frac{K(D,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\kappa,\lambda)k}{d}\Big)^{k}+K(D,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\kappa,\lambda)^{k}\cdot K(\lambda)\exp(-d)

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\frac{K(D,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\kappa,\lambda)k}{d}\Big)^{k}\,,

where the last inequality uses that
\exp(-d)\leq(k/d)^{k}
, valid for all
k,d>0
. The result now follows from symmetrization, Lemma
D.7
.
∎

As noted before, combining Proposition
D.5
and Proposition
D.6
proves the first part of Proposition
A.6
.

D.3.2
Second part of Proposition
A.6

Recall that we have (
D.19
) by Lemma
D.9
and Lemma
B.19
.
Now by Hölder’s Inequality and (
D.15
), taking
K(D,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\kappa,\lambda)
appropriately and combining with (
D.19
), we obtain

\displaystyle\log\nu_{t,v}\Big(\exp\Big(\frac{\|\bar{\theta}\|^{2}}{K(D,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\kappa,\lambda}\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle=\log\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big\langle\exp\Big(\frac{\|\bar{\theta}\|^{2}}{K(D,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\kappa,\lambda)}\Big)\Big\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\log\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big\langle\exp\Big(\frac{\beta\|\bar{\theta}\|^{2}}{2}\Big)\Big\rangle^{\frac{1}{1+K_{\textup{\ref{lem:logconcaveboundmomentslemma}}}(K_{\textup{\ref{lem:lem316}}},D,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\kappa,\lambda)}}

\displaystyle\leq\log\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\exp(B^{\star})

\displaystyle\leq K(\lambda)\,d\,.

It remains to prove that

\displaystyle\log\nu_{t,v}\Big(\exp\Big(\frac{\theta_{d}^{2}}{K(D,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\kappa,\lambda)}\Big)\Big)\leq K(D,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\kappa,\lambda)\,.

(D.31)

To this end, we cite the following Lemma from
[
Tal10
]
. This function is stated for concave
T\leq 0
and
\beta^{\prime}>0
in
[
Tal10
]
, however the proof therein applies verbatim under the conditions stated below.

Lemma D.16

(Lemma 3.2.5,
[
Tal10
]
)
.

Consider a concave function
T(\theta)
defined on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that the following integrals are well-defined. Consider any
(a_{j})_{j\leq d}
,
\beta>0,\beta^{\prime}\geq 0
and any convex set
C\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Define the measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
by defining for all
B\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\mu(B)\propto\int_{B\cap C}\exp\Big(T(\theta)-\beta\|\theta\|^{2}-\beta^{\prime}\theta_{d}^{2}+\sum_{j\leq d}a_{j}\theta_{j}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}\theta\,.

Let

\displaystyle C^{\prime}:=\big\{\theta_{d}\in\mathbb{R}\,:\,\exists\theta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\text{ s.t. }(\theta^{\prime},\theta_{d})\in C\}\,.

Then the following function
f
on
C^{\prime}
is concave, where
f
is defined by

\displaystyle f(\theta_{d}):=\log\int_{\theta=(\theta^{\prime},\theta_{d})\in C}\exp\Big(T(\theta)-\beta\sum_{j\leq d-1}\theta_{j}^{2}+\sum_{j\leq d-1}a_{j}\theta_{j}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}\theta^{\prime}\,.

Moreover, letting

\displaystyle w(\theta):=f(\theta)-(\beta+\beta^{\prime})\theta^{2}+a_{d}\theta\,,

the law of
\theta_{d}
under
\mu
is the measure on
C^{\prime}
with density proportional to
\exp w(\theta)
.

We will also need the following corollary of the Prékopa-Leindler Inequality:

Lemma D.17
.

Consider a log-concave distribution
\theta
and and a random variable
W
with a log-concave density. Then for any function
T(\theta,W)
that is jointly concave in
\theta
and
W
, the function
\theta\rightarrow\log\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[\exp T(\theta,W)\big]
is concave in
\theta
.

Proof.

Letting
\varphi(W)
denote the density of
W
, it follows that
S(\theta,W)=\exp T(\theta,W)\varphi(W)
is log-concave. Thus it remains to prove that
\theta\rightarrow\int S(\theta,W)\mathrm{d}W
is log-concave. Considering any
\lambda\in[0,1]
and any
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}
, we have by log-concavity of
S(\theta,W)
that for any
W
,

\displaystyle S(\lambda\theta_{1}+(1-\lambda)\theta_{2},W)\geq S(\theta_{1},W)^{\lambda}S(\theta_{2},W)^{1-\lambda}\,.

It follows from the Prékopa-Leindler Inequality (see
[
Pré73
]
) that

\displaystyle\int S(\lambda\theta_{1}+(1-\lambda)\theta_{2},W)\mathrm{d}W\geq\Big(\int S(\theta_{1},W)\mathrm{d}W\Big)^{\lambda}\Big(\int S(\theta_{2},W)\mathrm{d}W\Big)^{1-\lambda}\,.

This proves that
\int S(\theta,W)\mathrm{d}W
is log-concave, hence the conclusion.
∎

We now complete the proof of the second part of Proposition
A.6
. By Theorem
B.3
applied with the 1-Lipschitz function
\theta\rightarrow\theta_{d}
, we have

\displaystyle\Big\langle\exp\Big(\frac{(\theta_{d}-\langle\theta_{d}\rangle)^{2}}{K(\beta_{0})}\Big)\Big\rangle\leq K\,.

(D.32)

Next, note by convexity of the function
\exp x^{2}
that for
f_{1},f_{2}
satisfying
\nu_{t,v}\big(\exp(f_{1}^{2}/K)\big)\leq K
,
\nu_{t,v}\big(\exp(f_{2}^{2}/K)\big)\leq K
, we have the upper bound
\nu_{t,v}\big(\exp\big((f_{1}+f_{2})^{2}/K\big)\big)\leq K
(here
K
can differ). Thus taking expectations of (
D.32
) and applying this observation, to establish (
D.31
), it thus remains to show that

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\exp\Big(\frac{\langle\theta_{d}\rangle^{2}}{K(D,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\kappa,\lambda)}\Big)\Big]\leq K(D,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\kappa,\lambda)\,.

(D.33)

To this end, we apply Lemma
D.16
. Define the convex set
C=\mathbb{R}^{d}\cap\mathbf{1}\{\theta_{1}/\sqrt{d}\in I\}
, and let

\displaystyle T_{0}(\theta,W):=\sum_{1\leq i\leq n}u(S_{i,t})+u(S_{n,t,v})\,.

Since
S_{i,t}
is affine in
\theta
and as
S_{n,t,v}
is jointly affine in
\theta
and
W
, since
u
is concave, it follows that
T_{0}(\theta,W)
is concave jointly in
\theta,W
.
Thus letting
T(\theta):=\log\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[\exp T_{0}(\theta)\big]
,
T
is concave by Lemma
D.17
.

Next, let
\beta^{\prime}=\frac{(1-t)(r-\bar{r})}{2}\geq 0
, where the inequality follows as
r\geq\bar{r}
. Define
a_{j}
for
1\leq j\leq d
by letting
a_{j}=0
for all
j\leq d-1
and
a_{d}=\sqrt{(1-t)r}Y
.
Defining
\mu
from Lemma
D.16
with these choices, as dependence on
W
in
\nu_{t,v}
is only via
S_{n,t,v}(\theta)
, as
T(\theta)
is defined with expectation w.r.t.
W
, and as the number of replicas
L=1
here, it follows that
\mu
is exactly the Gibbs average
\langle\cdot\rangle
.

Note
C^{\prime}=\big\{\theta\in\mathbb{R}\,:\,\exists\theta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\text{ s.t. }(\theta^{\prime},\theta)\in C\}=\mathbb{R}
. Hence by Lemma
D.16
, the following function is concave on all of
\mathbb{R}
:

\displaystyle f(\theta_{d})

\displaystyle:=\log\int_{\theta=(\theta^{\prime},\theta_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\cap\mathbf{1}\{\theta_{1}/\sqrt{d}\in I\}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\Big[\exp\Big(T_{0}(\theta,W)-\beta\sum_{j\leq d-1}\theta_{j}^{2}\Big)\Big]\,\mathrm{d}\theta^{\prime}\,.

Moreover, by Lemma
D.16
, we know that letting

\displaystyle w(\theta):=f(\theta)-\Big(\beta+\frac{(1-t)(r-\bar{r})}{2}\Big)\theta^{2}+\sqrt{(1-t)r}Y\theta\,,

the law of
\theta_{d}
under
\mu
is the measure on
\mathbb{R}
with density proportional to
\exp w(\theta)
.

As
r\geq\bar{r}
, we know
\beta+\frac{(1-t)(r-\bar{r})}{2}>0
, and thus
w(\theta)
is
\beta
strongly concave and attains a unique maximum
\theta^{\star}
on
\mathbb{R}
. By Lemma
F.1
, it follows that
\big\langle(\theta_{d}-\theta^{\star})^{2}\big\rangle\leq K(\beta_{0})
. By Jensen and Triangle Inequality, we thus have
\langle\theta_{d}\rangle\leq K(\beta_{0})+|\theta^{\star}|
(for a different
K
).

Note that
w^{\prime}(\theta^{\star})=0
. Since
w
is
\beta
strongly concave,
|w^{\prime}(0)|=|w^{\prime}(\theta^{\star})-w^{\prime}(0)|\geq 2\beta|\theta^{\star}|
. Note
w^{\prime}(0)=f^{\prime}(0)+\sqrt{(1-t)r}Y
. Combining the above steps yields

\displaystyle\big\langle\theta_{d}\big\rangle\leq K(\beta_{0})\Big(1+|f^{\prime}(0)|+\sqrt{(1-t)r}|Y|\Big)\,.

(D.34)

Note since
Y
is a standard Gaussian, we have
\exp(Y^{2})\leq K
.
By (
D.34
), since
r\leq K(D)
, it suffices to prove the following to establish (
D.33
) and hence complete the proof:

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\exp\Big(\frac{f^{\prime}(0)^{2}}{K(D,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\kappa,\lambda)}\Big)\Big]\leq K(D,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\kappa,\lambda)\,.

(D.35)

Let
\langle\cdot\rangle_{f}
denote the Gibbs average defined as follows: for any test function
h
,

\displaystyle\langle h\rangle_{f}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\bar{Z}}\int_{\theta=(\theta^{\prime},\theta_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\cap\mathbf{1}\{\theta_{1}/\sqrt{d}\in I\}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\Big[h\exp\Big(T_{0}(\theta,W)-\beta\sum_{j\leq d-1}\theta_{j}^{2}+\sqrt{(1-t)r}Y\theta_{d}\Big)\Big]\,\mathrm{d}\theta\,,

where
\bar{Z}
denotes the corresponding normalizing constant.
Explicitly calculating yields

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(\theta_{d})

\displaystyle=\Bigg\langle\sum_{1\leq i\leq n-1}u^{\prime}(S_{i,t})\sqrt{\frac{t}{d}}g_{i,d}+u^{\prime}(S_{n,t,v})\sqrt{\frac{tv}{d}}g_{n,d}\Bigg\rangle_{f}\,.

(D.36)

Notice that when
\theta_{d}=0
,
S_{i,t}
,
S_{n,t,v}
,
\sqrt{(1-t)r}Y\theta_{d}=0
, and therefore
T_{0}(\theta,W)
do not depend on
(g_{i,d})_{1\leq i\leq n},Y
. Hence when
\theta_{d}=0
,
\bar{Z}
also does not depend on
(g_{i,d})_{1\leq i\leq n},Y
.
(
D.36
) thus implies that
f^{\prime}(0)
can be written as a linear combination of
(g_{i,d})_{1\leq i\leq n}
with coefficients independent of
(g_{i,d})_{1\leq i\leq n}
. Specifically, these coefficients are Gibbs averages of
u^{\prime}(S_{i,t})\sqrt{\frac{t}{d}}
,
u^{\prime}(S_{n,t,v})\sqrt{\frac{tv}{d}}
(where we consider
S_{i,t},S_{n,t,v}
with
\theta_{d}=0
) w.r.t.
\langle\cdot\rangle_{f}
.

Since the
(g_{i,d})_{1\leq i\leq n}\sim N(0,1)
, it follows that
f^{\prime}(0)
is a Gaussian. Let
\bar{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}}
denote expectation in the
g_{i,d}
. As
|u^{\prime}|\leq D
, the corresponding coefficients on the
g_{i,d}
are most
D/\sqrt{d}
in magnitude. By the independence of the
g_{i,d}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\bar{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}}\big[f^{\prime}(0)^{2}\big]\leq\frac{n}{d}\cdot D^{2}\leq K(D)\,,

where we use that
n/d\leq\alpha_{0}\leq 2
. Since
f^{\prime}(0)
is Gaussian, it follows from standard upper bounds for the MGF of a Gaussian with a given variance (see (A.11) in
[
Tal10
]
) that for suitable
K(D,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\kappa,\lambda)
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\exp\Big(\frac{f^{\prime}(0)^{2}}{K(D,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\kappa,\lambda)}\Big)\Big]=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\bar{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}}\Big[\exp\Big(\frac{f^{\prime}(0)^{2}}{K(D,\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\kappa,\lambda)}\Big)\Big]\leq 2\,.

This establishes (
D.35
) and hence establishes Proposition
A.6
.

Appendix E
Properties of the RS equations

Here, we establish several important properties of the RS equations.
Specifically, we prove Proposition
A.10
in Appendix
E.1
, Proposition
A.3
in Appendix
E.2
, and Lemma
C.3
in Appendix
E.3
.
Throughout this section, recall that
\varphi_{0}
denotes the PDF of a standard univariate normal
N(0,1)
and
\varphi_{1}
denotes the PDF of
S\sim\mathcal{D}
.

All three of these results are critical, and are proved through similar ideas.
Discussing the proof of Proposition
A.3
for the interpolators for concreteness, we must establish that the RS equations exhibit a particular convex-concave structure.
Talagrand’s proof of this convex-concave structure in Section 3.3,
[
Tal10
]
proceeds by showing each part of the expression for the relevant second derivatives is of the correct sign.
This relies crucially on the fact that
\kappa\geq 0
and that there is no signal in the data.

Here we instead proceed by showing the part of the expression for the relevant second derivatives whose signs are not correct are small in magnitude, and therefore do not eliminate the desired convex-concave structure.
We must also bound similar terms in proving Proposition
A.10
.
The magnitude of these terms all can be upper bounded by
\alpha\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[f\big(\frac{\kappa-xS-\sqrt{q}Z}{\sqrt{\rho-q}}\big)\Big]
for a function
f(x)\leq O(|x|^{2}+1)
that depends on the Inverse Mills’ Ratio from (
3.12
), where expectation is over
Z,S
.

The magnitude of the expression for each such part can be directly shown to be of order
O\Big(\alpha\cdot\frac{\kappa^{2}+q+1}{\rho-q}\Big)
for all
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}
.
However, to establish our result for all
\alpha\leq\alpha_{0}
for all
\kappa<0
, such a bound is not sufficient.
The idea is instead as follows.
When
\frac{\kappa-xS-\sqrt{q}Z}{\sqrt{\rho-q}}\leq 0
, the relevant quantities are simple to control by properties of the Inverse Mills’ Ratio.
Else, we let
\kappa^{\prime}:=\kappa-xS
be the ‘effective margin’ and analyze the sign of
\kappa^{\prime}
.

•

If
|S|<|\kappa|
, as
|x|\leq 1
, we must have
\kappa^{\prime}<0
. In this case, observe that the probability over
Z
that
\frac{\kappa-xS-\sqrt{q}Z}{\sqrt{\rho-q}}=\frac{\kappa^{\prime}-\sqrt{q}Z}{\sqrt{\rho-q}}>0
is exponentially small in
|\kappa^{\prime}|^{2}/q
.

•

Else, the probability over
S
that
|S|\geq|\kappa|
is exponentially small in
|\kappa|^{2}
as the law of
S^{2}
is sub-Exponential.

Combining both these bounds enables us to upper bound
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[f\big(\frac{\kappa-xS-\sqrt{q}Z}{\sqrt{\rho-q}}\big)\Big]
independently of
\kappa
for
\kappa<0
, which is sufficient for our purposes.

E.1
Proof of Proposition
A.10

We consider an arbitrary solution
(q,\rho,r,\bar{r})
of (
A.30
) and show that
(q,\rho)\in[0,C_{\textsc{sol}})\times[0,C_{\textsc{sol}})
and
\frac{1}{\rho-q}<C_{\textsc{sol}}
.
This is sufficient to establish Proposition
A.10
, up to showing
q>0
. To this end, note
q=0
implies
r=0
. Now as
\lambda>0
, we have
u^{\prime}>0
on a set of positive Lebesgue measure. It follows that for every
Z,S
, there exists a set of
W
with positive Lebesgue measure such that
u^{\prime}(\eta)\exp u(\eta)>0
, where
\eta
is given in terms of
W,Z,S
by (
A.19
). Since
u^{\prime}\exp u\geq 0
pointwise, it follows that
r>0
by definition of (
A.30
). Hence
q=0
yields contradiction, so as
q\geq 0
, we must have
q>0
.

Proof for posterior.

The proof of Proposition
A.10
for the posterior is direct. Recall that
|u^{l}|\leq D=D(\lambda)
for
l=1,2,3,4
. Thus by definition of
\psi_{\alpha}
,
\bar{\psi}_{\alpha}
in (
A.22
), (
A.23
), we have
|r|,|\bar{r}|\leq K(D)
. Also note
r\geq 0
by definition of
\psi_{\alpha}
. Since
r\geq\bar{r}
, the definition of the system (
A.30
) implies
0\leq q,\rho\leq K(D)
.
Next, we note
\rho,\rho-q\geq\frac{1}{2\beta+r-\bar{r}}\geq\frac{1}{K(D)}
.
This proves Proposition
A.10
for the posterior.

Proof for interpolators.

The rest of Appendix
E.1
is now devoted to the proof of Proposition
A.10
for the interpolators, which poses significantly more difficulties.
We first state and prove the following Lemmas which we will need in the following proof.
In the following, we recall the definition of
\eta=\eta(q,\rho)
from (
A.19
).

Lemma E.1
.

Letting
s\sim\mathcal{D}
, define

v(y)=\log\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[\exp u\big(xS+y+\sqrt{\rho-q}W\big)\big]\,.

Then we have
v^{\prime}\geq 0
and

r=\alpha\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[v^{\prime}\big(\sqrt{q}Z\big)^{2}\big]\,,\,\bar{r}-r=\alpha\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[v^{\prime\prime}\big(\sqrt{q}Z\big)\big]\,.

Proof.

The fact that
v^{\prime}\geq 0
follows directly as
u^{\prime}\geq 0
. The equalities written above follow from direct calculation, using that
r=\psi_{\alpha}(q,\rho),\bar{r}=\bar{\psi}_{\alpha}(q,\rho)
.
∎

Lemma E.2
.

Letting
Y:=\frac{\kappa-xS-\sqrt{q}Z}{\sqrt{\rho-q}}
and
K_{\textup{\ref{lem:RSboundedupperboundr}}}=20
, we have

\Bigg(\frac{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[W\exp u(\eta)\big]}{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[\exp u(\eta)\big]}\Bigg)^{2}\leq K_{\textup{\ref{lem:RSboundedupperboundr}}}+Y^{2}\mathbf{1}\{Y\geq 0\}\,.

Proof.

If
Y\leq 1
, then as
u\leq 0
and
u(\eta)=0
for
\eta\geq\kappa
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[W\exp u(\eta)\big]

\displaystyle\leq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[|W|\big]=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\,,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[\exp u(\eta)\big]

\displaystyle\geq\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{W}(\eta\geq\kappa)=\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{W}(W\geq Y)\geq\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{W}(W\geq 1)\,.

As
K_{\textup{\ref{lem:RSboundedupperboundr}}}\geq 20
, we obtain an upper bound of
K_{\textup{\ref{lem:RSboundedupperboundr}}}
in this case.

Else suppose
Y\geq 1
. First as
u^{\prime}\geq 0
, observe that

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[W\exp u(\eta)\big]=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[\exp u(\eta)\cdot u^{\prime}(\eta)\big]\cdot\sqrt{\rho-q}\geq 0\,.

Thus we may upper bound

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[W\exp u(\eta)\big]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[W\mathbf{1}\{W\leq Y\}\exp u(\eta)\big]+\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[W\mathbf{1}\{W\geq Y\}\exp u(\eta)\big]

\displaystyle\leq Y\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[\mathbf{1}\{W\leq Y\}\exp u(\eta)\big]+\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[W\mathbf{1}\{W\geq Y\}\big]

\displaystyle\leq Y\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[\mathbf{1}\{W\leq Y\}\exp u(\eta)\big]+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp(-Y^{2}/2)\,.

We next lower bound, using that
u(\eta)=0
for
W\geq Y
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[\exp u(\eta)\big]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[\mathbf{1}\{W\leq Y\}\exp u(\eta)\big]+\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[\mathbf{1}\{W\geq Y\}\exp u(\eta)\big]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[\mathbf{1}\{W\leq Y\}\exp u(\eta)\big]+\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(W\geq Y)

\displaystyle\geq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[\mathbf{1}\{W\leq Y\}\exp u(\eta)\big]+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\cdot\frac{Y}{Y^{2}+1}\exp(-Y^{2}/2)\,.

Here the lower bound on
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(W\geq Y)
is standard, see e.g.
[
Ver18
]
.
We thus obtain

\displaystyle 0\leq\frac{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[W\exp u(\eta)\big]}{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[\exp u(\eta)\big]}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{Y\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[\mathbf{1}\{W\leq Y\}\exp u(\eta)\big]+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp(-Y^{2}/2)}{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[\mathbf{1}\{W\leq Y\}\exp u(\eta)\big]+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\cdot\frac{Y}{Y^{2}+1}\exp(-Y^{2}/2)}

\displaystyle\leq Y+\frac{1}{Y}\,,

where we use the following inequality stated on p. 238 of
[
Tal10
]
that for
a,b>0
,
\frac{aY+b}{a+\frac{Y}{1+Y^{2}}b}\leq Y+\frac{1}{Y}
.
Hence we have, as
Y\geq 1
in this case,

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[W\exp u(\eta)\big]}{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[\exp u(\eta)\big]}\Big)^{2}\leq Y^{2}+2+\frac{1}{Y^{2}}\leq Y^{2}+3\,.

This completes the proof of the Lemma.
∎

Lemma E.3
.

For any
q\leq\rho
, we have
\psi_{\alpha}(q,\rho)\geq\bar{\psi}_{\alpha}(q,\rho)
. In particular, we have
r\geq\bar{r}
.

Proof.

When
q=\rho
the desired inequality follows as
u^{\prime\prime}\leq 0
. Else when
q<\rho
, it suffices to show

\displaystyle\frac{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[(W^{2}-1)\exp u(\eta)\big]}{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[\exp u(\eta)\big]}\leq\Big(\frac{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[W\exp u(\eta)\big]}{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[\exp u(\eta)\big]}\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle\iff\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[W^{2}\exp u(\eta)\big]-\frac{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[W\exp u(\eta)\big]^{2}}{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[\exp u(\eta)\big]}\leq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[\exp u(\eta)\big]\,.

Letting
w(y)=u(xS+\sqrt{q}Z+\sqrt{\rho-q}\cdot y)-\frac{y^{2}}{2}
which is
2
strongly-concave as
u^{\prime\prime}\leq 0
, Lemma
F.1
yields for some real
y^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[\exp u(\eta)\big]

\displaystyle\geq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[(W-y^{\prime})^{2}\exp u(\eta)\big]\geq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[W^{2}\exp u(\eta)\big]-\frac{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[W\exp u(\eta)\big]^{2}}{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[\exp u(\eta)\big]}\,,

completing the proof of the Lemma.
∎

Now we return to the proof of Proposition
A.10
.

Bounding
q,\rho
:

First, clearly
0\leq q\leq\rho
by definition of the system (
A.30
). It remains to upper bound
q,\rho
. Now, note as
r\geq\bar{r}
by Lemma
E.3
,

\displaystyle\rho-q=\frac{1}{2\beta+r-\bar{r}}\leq\frac{1}{2\beta_{0}}\,.

(E.1)

It thus remains to upper bound
q
. If
0\leq q\leq 1
we obtain

\displaystyle 0\leq q,\rho\leq\frac{1}{2\beta_{0}}+1<C_{\textsc{sol}}\,,

yielding the required bound.
We now suppose that
q>1
.
Now by definition of (
A.30
), we have

\displaystyle r=q(2\beta+r-\bar{r})^{2}=\frac{q}{(\rho-q)^{2}}\,.

(E.2)

Next by Lemma
E.2
, letting
Y:=\frac{\kappa-xS-\sqrt{q}Z}{\sqrt{\rho-q}}
, we have

\displaystyle r

\displaystyle=\psi_{\alpha}\big(q,\rho\big)=\frac{\alpha}{\rho-q}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big(\frac{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[W\exp u(\eta)\big]}{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\big[\exp u(\eta)\big]}\Big)^{2}\leq\frac{\alpha}{\rho-q}\Big(K_{\textup{\ref{lem:RSboundedupperboundr}}}+\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[Y^{2}\mathbf{1}\{Y\geq 0\}\big]\Big)\,.

(E.3)

Now we upper bound
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[Y^{2}\mathbf{1}\{Y\geq 0\}\big]
.

Upper bound for
\kappa\geq 0
.

Using
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[S^{2}]\leq K_{\textsc{signal}}
,
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[Z^{2}]=1
,
x^{2}\leq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[Y^{2}\mathbf{1}\{Y\geq 0\}\big]\leq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[Y^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3}{\rho-q}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[\kappa^{2}+x^{2}S^{2}+qZ^{2}\big]\leq\frac{3(\kappa^{2}+K_{\textsc{signal}}+q)}{\rho-q}\,.

(E.4)

Upper bound for
\kappa<0
.

Here as we would like to establish this result for all
\alpha\leq\alpha_{0}
at most a universal constant, the proof idea is more complicated.
Define the ‘effective margin’

\displaystyle\kappa^{\prime}:=\kappa-xS\,.

(E.5)

We now analyze the sign of
\kappa^{\prime}
. Note:

•

If
|S|<|\kappa|
, as
|x|\leq 1
, we must have
\kappa^{\prime}=\kappa-xS<0
. Then for
Y<0
to occur, we must have
|Z|\geq\frac{|\kappa^{\prime}|}{\sqrt{q}}
, the probability of which over
Z
is exponentially unlikely in
|\kappa^{\prime}|/\sqrt{q}
.

•

Else, the probability over
S
that
|S|\geq|\kappa|
is exponentially unlikely in
|\kappa|^{2}
as the law of
S^{2}
is sub-Exponential.

Thus in both cases we can upper bound
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[Y^{2}\mathbf{1}\{Y\geq 0\}\big]
. We now execute this idea. Write

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[Y^{2}\mathbf{1}\{Y\geq 0\}\big]

\displaystyle=\int_{s,z}\frac{(\kappa^{\prime}-z\sqrt{q})^{2}}{\rho-q}\mathbf{1}\{\kappa^{\prime}\geq z\sqrt{q}\}\varphi_{0}(z)\varphi_{1}(s)\,\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\int_{s:|s|<|\kappa|}\int_{z}\frac{(\kappa^{\prime}-z\sqrt{q})^{2}}{\rho-q}\mathbf{1}\{\kappa^{\prime}\geq z\sqrt{q}\}\varphi_{0}(z)\varphi_{1}(s)\,\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\int_{s:|s|\geq|\kappa|}\int_{z}\frac{(\kappa^{\prime}-z\sqrt{q})^{2}}{\rho-q}\mathbf{1}\{\kappa^{\prime}\geq z\sqrt{q}\}\varphi_{0}(z)\varphi_{1}(s)\,\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}s\,.

If
|s|<|\kappa|
, then
|xs|<|\kappa|
, so
\kappa^{\prime}=\kappa-xs<0
as
\kappa<0
. Recall
q\geq 1
, otherwise the proof is already completed. Thus
\kappa^{\prime}\geq z\sqrt{q}
implies that
|z|\geq\frac{|\kappa^{\prime}|}{\sqrt{q}}
. Hence

\displaystyle\int_{s:|s|<|\kappa|}\int_{z}\frac{(\kappa^{\prime}-z\sqrt{q})^{2}}{\rho-q}\mathbf{1}\{\kappa^{\prime}\geq z\sqrt{q}\}\varphi_{0}(z)\varphi_{1}(s)\,\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{q}{\rho-q}\int_{s:|s|<|\kappa|}\int_{z}2\Big(\frac{|\kappa^{\prime}|^{2}}{q}+z^{2}\Big)\cdot\exp(-|\kappa^{\prime}|^{2}/4q)\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp(-z^{2}/4)\varphi_{1}(s)\,\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{2q}{\rho-q}\Big(\int_{s}\varphi_{1}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\Big)\Bigg(\int_{z}\Big(\frac{|\kappa^{\prime}|^{2}}{q}+z^{2}\Big)\cdot\exp(-|\kappa^{\prime}|^{2}/4q)\exp(-z^{2}/4)\,\mathrm{d}z\Bigg)

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{10q}{\rho-q}\,.

When
|s|\geq|\kappa|
, we employ a similar strategy, but now we analyze the probability in
S
rather than
Z
. Since
q\geq 1
, we may bound

\displaystyle\int_{s:|s|\geq|\kappa|}\int_{z}\frac{(\kappa^{\prime}-z\sqrt{q})^{2}}{\rho-q}\mathbf{1}\{\kappa^{\prime}\geq z\sqrt{q}\}\varphi_{0}(z)\varphi_{1}(s)\,\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{2q}{\rho-q}\int_{s:|s|\geq|\kappa|}\int_{z}\Big(z^{2}+\frac{2(\kappa^{2}+s^{2})}{q}\Big)\varphi_{0}(z)\varphi_{1}(s)\,\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{2q}{\rho-q}\Big(\int_{z}z^{2}\varphi_{0}(z)\,\mathrm{d}z+2\int_{s}s^{2}\varphi_{1}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s+2\kappa^{2}\int_{s:|s|\geq|\kappa|}\varphi_{1}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\Big)

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{2q}{\rho-q}\Big(1+2K_{\textsc{signal}}+2\kappa^{2}\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}\Big(S^{2}>\kappa^{2}\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{2q}{\rho-q}\Big(1+2K_{\textsc{signal}}+4\kappa^{2}\exp\Big(-\frac{\kappa^{2}}{K_{\textsc{signal}}}\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{2q}{\rho-q}\Big(1+3.5K_{\textsc{signal}}\Big)\,.

Here we used that
S^{2}
is
K_{\textsc{signal}}
sub-Exponential to upper bound
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(S^{2}>\kappa^{2})
, and used Assumption
1
to upper bound
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[S^{2}]\leq K_{\textsc{signal}}
.
Combining the above displays yields

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[Y^{2}\mathbf{1}\{Y\geq 0\}\big]\leq\frac{q}{\rho-q}\Big(12+7K_{\textsc{signal}}\Big)\,.

(E.6)

Having upper bounded
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[Y^{2}\mathbf{1}\{Y\geq 0\}\big]
in both cases, in the
\kappa>0
case we obtain via (
E.3
) and (
E.4
), using that
q\geq 1
by assumption here (else we have already finished the proof) and that
K_{\textsc{signal}}\geq 1
,

\displaystyle q=r(\rho-q)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(\rho-q)^{2}\cdot\frac{\alpha}{\rho-q}\Big(K_{\textup{\ref{lem:RSboundedupperboundr}}}+\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[Y^{2}\mathbf{1}\{Y\geq 0\}\big]\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\alpha K_{\textup{\ref{lem:RSboundedupperboundr}}}(\rho-q)+3\alpha q\big(\kappa^{2}+K_{\textsc{signal}}+1\big)\,.

Similarly in the
\kappa<0
case we have by (
E.3
) and (
E.6
),

\displaystyle q=r(\rho-q)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\alpha K_{\textup{\ref{lem:RSboundedupperboundr}}}(\rho-q)+\alpha q\big(12+7K_{\textsc{signal}}\big)\,.

Since
\alpha_{0}\leq\min\big\{\frac{1}{2K_{\textup{\ref{lem:RSboundedupperboundr}}}},\frac{1}{24+14K_{\textsc{signal}}}\big\}
when
\kappa<0
and
\alpha_{0}\leq\frac{1}{3(\kappa^{2}+K_{\textsc{signal}}+1)}
when
\kappa\geq 0
, combining with (
E.1
) yield that in either case, we have

q\leq\frac{q}{2}+\frac{\rho-q}{2}=\frac{\rho}{2}\leq\frac{q}{2}+\frac{1}{4\beta_{0}}\,,\quad\text{thus}\quad q\leq\frac{1}{2\beta_{0}}\,.

Consequently we obtain
\rho\leq\frac{1}{\beta_{0}}
from (
E.1
), and thus
0\leq q,\rho<C_{\textsc{sol}}
.

Upper bounding
\frac{1}{\rho-q}
:

From the definition of the system (
A.30
), we have

\frac{1}{\rho-q}=2\beta+r-\bar{r}=2\beta-\alpha\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[v^{\prime\prime}\big(\sqrt{q}Z\big)\big]\,.

Next by Gaussian Integration by Parts and as
v^{\prime}\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[v^{\prime\prime}\big(\sqrt{q}Z\big)\big]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{q}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[Zv^{\prime}\big(\sqrt{q}Z\big)\big]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{q}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[\mathbf{1}\{Z\leq 0\}\cdot Zv^{\prime}\big(\sqrt{q}Z\big)\big]

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{1}{\sqrt{q}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[Z^{2}\mathbf{1}\{Z\leq 0\}\big]^{1/2}\cdot\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[v^{\prime}\big(\sqrt{q}Z\big)^{2}\big]^{1/2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2q}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[v^{\prime}\big(\sqrt{q}Z\big)^{2}\big]^{1/2}\,.

Combining the above with Lemma
E.1
gives

\frac{1}{\rho-q}\leq 2\beta+\frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{2q}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[v^{\prime}\big(\sqrt{q}Z\big)^{2}\big]^{1/2}=2\beta+\sqrt{\frac{\alpha r}{2q}}\,.

Combining the above with (
E.2
) and using
\alpha\leq\alpha_{0}\leq 1/2
yields

\frac{1}{\rho-q}\leq 2\beta+\sqrt{\frac{\alpha}{2}}\cdot\frac{1}{\rho-q}\,,\quad\text{thus}\quad\frac{1}{\rho-q}\leq 4\beta\leq 4\beta_{1}<C_{\textsc{sol}}\,.

E.2
Proof of Proposition
A.3

Throughout Appendix
E.2
,
I
is held fixed, and so we denote
F_{I}
by
F
for simplicity of notation. As done in Chapter 3 of
[
Tal10
]
, consider the transformation

y=\frac{q}{\rho-q}\,.

Hence given
y
, we set
q=\frac{y\rho}{1+y}
, and have
\rho>q
.
Define

\displaystyle G(y,\rho)

\displaystyle:=F\Big(\frac{y\rho}{1+y},\rho\Big)\,.

Thus we have

\displaystyle G(y,\rho)

\displaystyle=\alpha\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\log\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\exp u\Big(xS+\sqrt{\frac{y\rho}{1+y}}Z+\sqrt{\frac{\rho}{1+y}}W\Big)\Big]+\frac{y}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\log\rho-\frac{1}{2}\log(1+y)

(E.7)

\displaystyle-\beta\big(\rho+r_{I}(x)\big)\,,

where
u(\cdot)
is given by
\exp u(x)=\mathbf{1}\{x\geq\kappa\}
for the interpolators, and by (
3.17
) in the GMM case or (
3.18
) in the logistic case for the posterior. Consider the system

\displaystyle\frac{\partial G}{\partial\rho}=\frac{\partial G}{\partial y}=0\,.

(E.8)

Note this system does not depend on the
-\beta r(x)
term in the definition of
G(y,\rho)
.

Lemma E.4
.

The system
(
A.11
)
has a unique solution
(q_{0},\rho_{0})\in[0,C_{\textsc{sol}}]\times[\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}]
with
q_{0}<\rho_{0}
iff
(
E.8
)
has a unique solution
(y_{0},\rho_{0})\in[0,\infty)\times[\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}]
.

Proof.

First, notice the transformation
y=\frac{q}{\rho-q}
maps any
(q,\rho)\in[0,C_{\textsc{sol}}]\times[\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}]
with
q<\rho
to some
(y,\rho)\in[0,\infty)\times[\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}]
. Also for any
(y,\rho)\in[0,\infty)\times[\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}]
, notice the transformation
q=\frac{y\rho}{1+y}
is such that
0\leq q<\rho\leq C_{\textsc{sol}}
. Thus the transformation
q=\frac{y\rho}{1+y}
maps any
(y,\rho)\in[0,\infty)\times[\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}]
to some
(q,\rho)\in[0,C_{\textsc{sol}}]\times[\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}]
with
q<\rho
.

Thus, it suffices to show that
(q_{0},\rho_{0})\in[0,C_{\textsc{sol}}]\times[\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}]
with
q_{0}<\rho_{0}
is a solution to (
A.11
) iff
(y_{0},\rho_{0})\in[0,\infty)\times[\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}]
is a solution to (
E.8
), where
y_{0}=\frac{q_{0}}{\rho_{0}-q_{0}}
. Establishing that
(q_{0},\rho_{0})
,
(y_{0},\rho_{0})
belong in the appropriate domains has already been established above. To show that this transformation preserves solutions, note

\displaystyle\frac{\partial G}{\partial\rho}(y,\rho)

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial F}{\partial\rho}\Big(\frac{y\rho}{1+y},\rho\Big)+\frac{\partial F}{\partial q}\Big(\frac{y\rho}{1+y},\rho\Big)\cdot\frac{\partial q}{\partial\rho}(y,\rho)\quad,\quad\frac{\partial G}{\partial y}(y,\rho)=\frac{\partial F}{\partial q}\Big(\frac{y\rho}{1+y},\rho\Big)\cdot\frac{\partial q}{\partial y}(y,\rho)\,.

Note
\frac{\partial q}{\partial y}(y_{0},\rho_{0})=\frac{\rho_{0}}{(1+y_{0})^{2}}>0
as
\rho_{0}>\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}}>0
. Thus if
(y_{0},\rho_{0})
solves (
E.8
), then we have
\frac{\partial F}{\partial q}(q_{0},\rho_{0})=0
, and consequently
\frac{\partial F}{\partial\rho}(q_{0},\rho_{0})=0
. Moreover if
(q_{0},\rho_{0})
solves (
A.11
), plugging into the equations above directly implies that
(y_{0},\rho_{0})
solves (
E.8
). This proves the Lemma.
∎

Thus in what follows, we now work with (
E.8
).

Preliminary calculations for interpolators.

We first explicitly write the system (
E.8
) and establish some of its useful properties.
Since we consider
\rho_{0}\geq\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}}>0
, we may divide by
\rho
in the following.
Now let

\displaystyle V:=\sqrt{\frac{1+y}{\rho}}(\kappa-xS)-\sqrt{y}Z=\sqrt{\frac{1+y}{\rho}}\kappa^{\prime}-\sqrt{y}Z\,,

(E.9)

where here and in the following, as in the proof of Proposition
A.10
, we define the ‘effective margin’
\kappa^{\prime}:=\kappa-xS
as per (
E.5
).
Hence letting
\mathcal{N}(\cdot)
denote the complementary c.d.f. of the standard normal as per (
3.2
), we have

\displaystyle\log\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}_{W}\Big(xS+\sqrt{\frac{y\rho}{1+y}}Z+\sqrt{\frac{\rho}{1+y}}W\geq\kappa\Big)\equiv\log\mathcal{N}\Big(\sqrt{\frac{1+y}{\rho}}(\kappa-xS)-\sqrt{y}Z\Big)\,.

(E.10)

Recalling the definition of the Inverse Mills’ Ratio
\mathcal{A}(x)
in (
3.12
), we record a few of its properties.

Lemma E.5

(Lemma 3.3.7 of
[
Tal10
]
and Lemma 17,
[
EAS22
]
)
.

We have:

1.

\mathcal{A}(x)\geq x
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

2.

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(x)=\mathcal{A}(x)^{2}-x\mathcal{A}(x)\geq 0
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

3.

x\mathcal{A}(x)\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(x)\leq\mathcal{A}(x)^{2}
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

4.

x\mathcal{A}(x)\leq 1+x^{2}
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

5.

\lim_{x\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\mathcal{A}(x)}{x}=1
.

6.

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(x),\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}(x)\leq K_{\textsc{MILLS}}
for a universal constant
K_{\textsc{MILLS}}\geq 1
,
0\leq\mathcal{A}(x)\leq 1
for all
x<0
, and
0\leq\mathcal{A}(x)\leq 2x+1
for all
x\geq 0
.

Proof.

All the above are proved in Lemma 3.3.7 of
[
Tal10
]
and Lemma 17,
[
EAS22
]
except the bound
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}(x)\leq K_{\textsc{MILLS}}
.
This follows from noting as in the proof of Lemma 17,
[
EAS22
]
the bound
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}(x)=\mathcal{A}(x)P(x,\mathcal{A}(x))
for a polynomial
P(\cdot,\cdot)
.
It thus suffices to control
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}(x)
for
x
sufficiently large.
The
x\rightarrow+\infty
case follows from Corollary 1.6 of
[
Pin19
]
, while the
x\rightarrow-\infty
case follows from noting that
\mathcal{A}(x)\rightarrow 0
exponentially fast as
x\rightarrow-\infty
.
∎

We now explicitly write the system (
E.8
). By explicit calculation and (
E.10
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial G}{\partial\rho}

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha}{2}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[(\kappa-xS)\frac{(1+y)^{1/2}}{\rho^{3/2}}\mathcal{A}(V)\Big]+\frac{1}{2\rho}-\beta=\frac{\alpha}{2}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\kappa^{\prime}\frac{(1+y)^{1/2}}{\rho^{3/2}}\mathcal{A}(V)\Big]+\frac{1}{2\rho}-\beta\,,

(E.11)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial G}{\partial y}

\displaystyle=\alpha\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\Big(-\frac{\kappa-xS}{2\rho^{1/2}(1+y)^{1/2}}+\frac{Z}{2y^{1/2}}\Big)\mathcal{A}(V)\Big]+\frac{y}{2(1+y)}\,.

(E.12)

Applying Gaussian Integration by Parts and Lemma
E.5
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\frac{Z}{\sqrt{y}}\mathcal{A}(V)\Big]=-\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(V)\Big]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[-\mathcal{A}(V)^{2}+V\mathcal{A}(V)\Big]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[-\mathcal{A}(V)^{2}\Big]+\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\Big((\kappa-xS)\sqrt{\frac{1+y}{\rho}}-\sqrt{y}Z\Big)\mathcal{A}(V)\Big]\,.

Rearranging yields

\displaystyle\frac{Z}{\sqrt{y}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\mathcal{A}(V)]=\frac{1}{1+y}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[-\mathcal{A}(V)^{2}+(\kappa-xS)\sqrt{\frac{1+y}{\rho}}\mathcal{A}(V)\Big]\,.

We thus obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\partial G}{\partial y}=-\frac{\alpha}{2(1+y)}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\mathcal{A}(V)^{2}\Big]+\frac{y}{2(1+y)}\,.

(E.13)

Following the argument of
[
ST03
]
,
[
Tal10
]
, we establish that (
E.11
), (
E.13
) have a unique solution by considering
\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial^{2}\rho}
and the sign of
\frac{\partial G}{\partial y}
, and arguing that the signs work out to yield a saddle point and thus a unique solution to (
E.8
).
We first compute from (
E.11
),

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial\rho^{2}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{3\alpha(1+y)^{1/2}}{4\rho^{5/2}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\kappa^{\prime}\mathcal{A}(V)\Big]+\frac{\alpha(1+y)^{1/2}}{2\rho^{3/2}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\kappa^{\prime}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(V)\cdot-\frac{1}{2}(1+y)^{1/2}\rho^{-3/2}\kappa^{\prime}\Big]-\frac{1}{2\rho^{2}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{3\alpha(1+y)^{1/2}}{4\rho^{5/2}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\kappa^{\prime}\mathcal{A}(V)\Big]-\frac{\alpha(1+y)}{4\rho^{3}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\kappa^{\prime 2}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(V)\Big]-\frac{1}{2\rho^{2}}\,.

(E.14)

Now in preparation on our work on the sign of
\frac{\partial G}{\partial y}
, we define

\displaystyle g(y,\rho):=y-\alpha\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[\mathcal{A}(V)^{2}\big]\,,\quad\text{thus}\quad\frac{\partial G}{\partial y}(y,\rho)=\frac{g(y,\rho)}{2(1+y)}\,.

(E.15)

We will need to show
\frac{\partial g}{\partial y}>0
in an appropriate domain, which we do in Lemma
E.10
. In preparation, we compute

\displaystyle\frac{\partial g}{\partial y}

\displaystyle=1-\alpha\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[2\mathcal{A}(V)\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(V)\Big(\frac{\kappa^{\prime}}{2\sqrt{\rho(1+y)}}-\frac{1}{2}Zy^{-1/2}\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle=1-\frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{\rho(1+y)}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\kappa^{\prime}\mathcal{A}(V)\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(V)\Big]+\frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{y}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[Z\mathcal{A}(V)\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(V)\Big]\,.

Notice
\frac{\partial V}{\partial Z}=-\sqrt{y}
, so Gaussian Integration by Parts gives

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[Z\mathcal{A}(V)\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(V)\Big]=-\sqrt{y}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\mathcal{A}^{\prime 2}(V)+\mathcal{A}(V)\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}(V)\Big]\,.

Thus

\displaystyle\frac{\partial g}{\partial y}=1-\frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{\rho(1+y)}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\kappa^{\prime}\mathcal{A}(V)\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(V)\Big]-\alpha\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\mathcal{A}^{\prime 2}(V)+\mathcal{A}(V)\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}(V)\Big]\,.

(E.16)

To control the signs of the above, since we do not have non-negative margin –
\kappa\geq 0
or
\kappa^{\prime}=\kappa-xS\geq 0
is not uniformly true – the signs of each component of the derivative do not work out directly in the same way as in
[
ST03
]
. Rather we show that for
\alpha\leq\alpha_{0}
, the part of the derivatives that do not have the correct sign are small in magnitude. We will carry this argument out in the proofs to follow.

Preliminary calculations for posterior.

We now define

\displaystyle\bar{V}:=xS+\sqrt{\frac{y\rho}{1+y}}Z+\sqrt{\frac{\rho}{1+y}}W\,.

(E.17)

Here in Appendix
E.2
, we let
\langle\cdot\rangle
denote a Gibbs average w.r.t.
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\exp u(\bar{V})
. In what follows, when the derivatives
u^{l}
are written, they are taken with argument
\bar{V}
unless otherwise stated. We obtain by Gaussian Integration by Parts,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial G}{\partial\rho}

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha}{2}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big\langle u^{\prime\prime}+u^{\prime 2}\big\rangle-\frac{\alpha y}{2(1+y)}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big\langle u^{\prime}\big\rangle^{2}+\frac{1}{2\rho}-\beta\,,

(E.18)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial G}{\partial y}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\alpha\rho}{2(1+y)^{2}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big\langle\exp u^{\prime}\big\rangle^{2}+\frac{y}{2(1+y)}\,.

(E.19)

We will follow the same strategy as in the interpolators of establishing the saddle point structure of the RS equations. As such we compute

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial\rho^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha}{4}\Big(\textsc{(I)}+\textsc{(II)}+\textsc{(III)}+\textsc{(IV)}\Big)-\frac{1}{2\rho^{2}}\,,

(E.20)

where

\displaystyle\textsc{(I)}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{1+y}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big\langle u^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}+4u^{\prime\prime\prime}u^{\prime}+3u^{\prime\prime 2}+6u^{\prime\prime}u^{\prime 2}+u^{\prime 4}\big\rangle-\big\langle u^{\prime\prime}+u^{\prime 2}\big\rangle^{2}\Big]\,,

(E.21)

\displaystyle\textsc{(II)}

\displaystyle:=\frac{y}{1+y}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big\langle u^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}+4u^{\prime\prime\prime}u^{\prime}+3u^{\prime\prime 2}+6u^{\prime\prime}u^{\prime 2}+u^{\prime 4}\big\rangle+2\big\langle u^{\prime\prime}+u^{\prime 2}\big\rangle\big\langle u^{\prime}\big\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle-\big\langle u^{\prime\prime\prime}+3u^{\prime\prime}u^{\prime}+u^{\prime 3}\big\rangle\big\langle u^{\prime}\big\rangle-\big\langle u^{\prime\prime\prime}+3u^{\prime\prime}u^{\prime}+u^{\prime 3}\big\rangle\big\langle u^{\prime\prime}+u^{\prime 2}\big\rangle\Big]\,,

\displaystyle\textsc{(III)}

\displaystyle:=\frac{-y}{2(1+y)^{2}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big\langle u^{\prime\prime\prime}+3u^{\prime\prime}u^{\prime}+u^{\prime 3}\big\rangle\big\langle u^{\prime}\big\rangle-\big\langle u^{\prime\prime}+u^{\prime 2}\big\rangle\big\langle u^{\prime}\big\rangle^{2}\Big]\,,

\displaystyle\textsc{(IV)}

\displaystyle:=\frac{-y^{2}}{2(1+y)^{2}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big\langle u^{\prime\prime}+u^{\prime 2}\big\rangle^{2}+\big\langle u^{\prime\prime\prime}+3u^{\prime\prime}u^{\prime}+u^{\prime 3}\big\rangle\big\langle u^{\prime}\big\rangle+3\big\langle u^{\prime}\big\rangle^{4}-5\big\langle u^{\prime\prime}+u^{\prime 2}\big\rangle\big\langle u^{\prime}\big\rangle^{2}\Big]\,.

We also define similarly to (
E.15
),

\displaystyle g(y):=y(1+y)-\alpha\rho\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big\langle\exp u^{\prime}\big\rangle^{2}\,,\quad\text{thus}\quad\frac{\partial G}{\partial y}(y,\rho)=\frac{g(y)}{2(1+y)^{2}}\,.

(E.22)

Again in Lemma
E.10
, we show
\frac{\partial g}{\partial y}>0
. In preparation, we compute

\displaystyle\frac{\partial g}{\partial y}=1+2y-\frac{\alpha\rho}{(1+y)^{2}}\Big(\textsc{(I)}+\textsc{(II)}\Big)\,,

(E.23)

where

\displaystyle\textsc{(I)}

\displaystyle:=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[-\big\langle u^{\prime\prime\prime}+3u^{\prime\prime}u^{\prime}+u^{\prime 3}\big\rangle\big\langle u^{\prime}\big\rangle+\big\langle u^{\prime\prime}+u^{\prime 2}\big\rangle\big\langle u^{\prime}\big\rangle^{2}\Big]\,,

(E.24)

\displaystyle\textsc{(II)}

\displaystyle:=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big\langle u^{\prime\prime}+u^{\prime 2}\big\rangle^{2}+\big\langle u^{\prime\prime\prime}+3u^{\prime\prime}u^{\prime}+u^{\prime 3}\big\rangle\big\langle u^{\prime}\big\rangle+3\big\langle u^{\prime}\big\rangle^{4}-5\big\langle u^{\prime\prime}+u^{\prime 2}\big\rangle\big\langle u^{\prime}\big\rangle^{2}\Big]

Bounding relevant quantities for the interpolators.

To study the system (
E.8
) for the interpolators, we will need to bound various quantities involving the Inverse Mills’ Ratio. This step is not necessary to study (
E.8
) for the posterior.
To this end, we first control
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\mathcal{A}(V)^{2}]
in terms of
y
. We then use this to upper bound
y
. Finally we use these two upper bounds to upper bound the quantities of interest, namely
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[\mathcal{A}(V)\big],\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[\mathcal{A}(V)^{2}\big],\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[|\kappa-xS|\mathcal{A}(V)\big]
.

Lemma E.6
.

Consider
(\alpha,\beta)\in[0,\alpha_{0}]\times[\beta_{0},\beta_{1}]
. Then for any
y\geq 0
and
\rho\geq\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}}
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\mathcal{A}(V)^{2}\Big]\leq K_{\textup{\ref{lem:controlexpectationofAWinit}}}y+K_{\textup{\ref{lem:controlexpectationofAWinit}}}\,,

where

\displaystyle K_{\textup{\ref{lem:controlexpectationofAWinit}}}=\begin{cases}12C_{\textsc{sol}}K_{\textsc{signal}}+K_{\textsc{signal}}+51&:\kappa<0\,,\\
24C_{\textsc{sol}}(\kappa^{2}+K_{\textsc{signal}})+12&:\kappa\geq 0\,.\end{cases}

Proof.

We perform a similar argument as in the proof of Proposition
A.10
.
We break into the two cases
\kappa\geq 0
and
\kappa<0
.

When
\kappa\geq 0
.

By Lemma
E.5
,
0\leq\mathcal{A}(x)\leq 2|x|+1
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
.
Consequently using
x^{2}\leq 1
and
\rho\geq\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(V)^{2}\leq(2|V|+1)^{2}\leq 6V^{2}+3

\displaystyle\leq 12\kappa^{\prime 2}\cdot\frac{1+y}{\rho}+12yZ^{2}+3

\displaystyle\leq 24C_{\textsc{sol}}(\kappa^{2}+S^{2})(y+1)+12yZ^{2}+3\,.

As
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[S^{2}]\leq K_{\textsc{signal}}
,
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[Z^{2}]=1
, we obtain

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\mathcal{A}(V)^{2}\Big]\leq 24C_{\textsc{sol}}(\kappa^{2}+K_{\textsc{signal}})(y+1)+12y+3\leq K_{\textup{\ref{lem:controlexpectationofAWinit}}}y+K_{\textup{\ref{lem:controlexpectationofAWinit}}}\,.

When
\kappa<0
.

Recalling that
\kappa^{\prime}=\kappa-xS
, write

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\mathcal{A}(V)^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle=\int_{s,z}\mathcal{A}(V)^{2}\varphi_{0}(z)\varphi_{1}(s)\,\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\int_{s:|s|<|\kappa|}\int_{z}\mathcal{A}(V)^{2}\varphi_{0}(z)\varphi_{1}(s)\,\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}s+\int_{s:|s|\geq|\kappa|}\int_{z}\mathcal{A}(V)^{2}\varphi_{0}(z)\varphi_{1}(s)\,\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}s\,.

We separately upper bound each of these two terms:

•

If
|s|<|\kappa|
, then as
|xs|<|\kappa|
and
\kappa<0
, we must have
\kappa^{\prime}=\kappa-xs<0
. Now, consider the set
E:=\{(s,z)\,:\,V\leq 1\}
. Hence for
(s,z)\in E
,
\mathcal{A}(V)\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\mathcal{N}(1)}
.

Otherwise consider
(s,z)\not\in E
, so here we have
V>1
. First note that in this case,
y>0
; if
y=0
, then as
\kappa^{\prime}<0
, we have
1<V=\kappa^{\prime}\sqrt{\frac{1+y}{\rho}}-z\sqrt{y}<0
which is a contradiction. Thus recalling
\rho>0
,

1<V=\kappa^{\prime}\sqrt{\frac{1+y}{\rho}}-z\sqrt{y}\implies z\leq\kappa^{\prime}\sqrt{\frac{1+y}{y\rho}}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{y}}\,.

Hence as
\kappa^{\prime}<0
and as
y,\rho>0
, we have

\displaystyle z^{2}\geq|\kappa^{\prime}|^{2}\cdot\frac{1+y}{y\rho}+\frac{1}{y}\geq\frac{|\kappa^{\prime}|^{2}}{\rho}\,.

Moreover when
V>1
, by Lemma
E.5
,

\mathcal{A}(V)^{2}\leq(2V+1)^{2}\leq 8V^{2}+2\leq 16\Big(|\kappa^{\prime}|^{2}\cdot\frac{1+y}{\rho}+z^{2}y\Big)+2\,.

Consequently we can upper bound

\displaystyle\int_{s:|s|<|\kappa|}\int_{z}\mathcal{A}(V)^{2}\varphi_{0}(z)\varphi_{1}(s)\,\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{(s,z)\not\in E,|s|<|\kappa|}\mathcal{A}(V)^{2}\varphi_{0}(z)\varphi_{1}(s)\,\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}s+\int_{(s,z)\in E,|s|<|\kappa|}\mathcal{A}(V)^{2}\varphi_{0}(z)\varphi_{1}(s)\,\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{(s,z)\not\in E,|s|<|\kappa|}\Big(\frac{16|\kappa^{\prime}|^{2}(y+1)}{\rho}+z^{2}y+2\Big)\varphi_{0}(z)\varphi_{1}(s)\,\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}s+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathcal{N}(1)^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\leq 16(y+1)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{|\kappa^{\prime}|^{2}}{\rho}\exp\Big(-\frac{|\kappa^{\prime}|^{2}}{4\rho}\Big)\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp(-z^{2}/4)\varphi_{1}(s)\,\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}s+y+2+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathcal{N}(1)^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\leq 35y+43\,.

Here we used that
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[Z^{2}]=1
, and upper bounded
\frac{\kappa^{\prime 2}}{\rho}\exp\Big(-\frac{\kappa^{\prime 2}}{4\rho}\Big)
by using that for all
x\geq 0
, we have
x\exp(-x/4)\leq K
where
K
is a universal constant.

•

Else if
|s|\geq|\kappa|
, note by Lemma
E.5
that

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(V)^{2}\leq 1+(2V+1)^{2}\leq 8V^{2}+3

\displaystyle\leq 16\Big((\kappa-xs)^{2}\cdot\frac{1+y}{\rho}+z^{2}y\Big)+3

\displaystyle\leq 32C_{\textsc{sol}}(\kappa^{2}+s^{2})(y+1)+16z^{2}y+3\,.

Thus as
S^{2}
is
K_{\textsc{signal}}
sub-Exponential and
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[S^{2}]\leq K_{\textsc{signal}}
,

\displaystyle\int_{s:|s|\geq|\kappa|}\int_{z}\mathcal{A}(V)^{2}\varphi_{0}(z)\varphi_{1}(s)\,\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad\leq 32C_{\textsc{sol}}(y+1)\kappa^{2}\int_{s:|s|\geq|\kappa|}\int_{z}\varphi_{0}(z)\varphi_{1}(s)\,\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}s+(y+1)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}s^{2}\varphi_{0}(z)\varphi_{1}(s)\,\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+16y\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}z^{2}\varphi_{0}(z)\varphi_{1}(s)\,\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}s+3

\displaystyle\quad=32C_{\textsc{sol}}(y+1)\kappa^{2}\cdot\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}\Big(S^{2}\geq\kappa^{2}\Big)+K_{\textsc{signal}}(y+1)+16y+3

\displaystyle\quad\leq 32C_{\textsc{sol}}(y+1)\kappa^{2}\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{\kappa^{2}}{K_{\textsc{signal}}}\Big)+K_{\textsc{signal}}(y+1)+16y+3

\displaystyle\quad\leq y(12C_{\textsc{sol}}K_{\textsc{signal}}+K_{\textsc{signal}}+16)+(12C_{\textsc{sol}}K_{\textsc{signal}}+K_{\textsc{signal}}+3)\,.

Summing the above bounds yields the desired upper bound on
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[\mathcal{A}(V)^{2}\big]
.
∎

Lemma E.7
.

Suppose
(\alpha,\beta)\in[0,\alpha_{0}]\times[\beta_{0},\beta_{1}]
. For any
(y,\rho)
such that
y\geq 0
,
\rho\geq\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}}
that satisfies
\frac{\partial G}{\partial y}(y,\rho)=0
– in particular, to any solution to
(
E.8
)
– we must have
y\leq 1
.

Proof.

By our expression (
E.13
) for
\frac{\partial G}{\partial y}
and by Lemma
E.6
, we obtain

y=\alpha\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[\mathcal{A}(V)^{2}\big]\leq\alpha(K_{\textup{\ref{lem:controlexpectationofAWinit}}}y+K_{\textup{\ref{lem:controlexpectationofAWinit}}})\leq\frac{1}{2}y+\frac{1}{2}\implies y\leq 1\,,

where we used that
\alpha\leq\frac{1}{2K_{\textup{\ref{lem:controlexpectationofAWinit}}}}
.
∎

The last preliminary step we need is the following Lemma, which lets us control various expectations of
\mathcal{A}(V)
.

Lemma E.8
.

Suppose
(\alpha,\beta)\in[0,\alpha_{0}]\times[\beta_{0},\beta_{1}]
. Then we have for any
(y,\rho)\in[0,1]\times[\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}]
that

0\leq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[\mathcal{A}(V)\big]\,,\,\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[\mathcal{A}(V)^{2}\big]\,,\,\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[\big|\kappa^{\prime}\mathcal{A}(V)\big|\big]\leq K_{\textup{\ref{lem:controlexpectationofAW}}}\,,

where

\displaystyle K_{\textup{\ref{lem:controlexpectationofAW}}}=\begin{cases}42C_{\textsc{sol}}K_{\textsc{signal}}+13C_{\textsc{sol}}^{3/2}+7.5&:\kappa<0\,,\\
8.3C_{\textsc{sol}}^{1/2}(\kappa^{2}+K_{\textsc{signal}})+2.3&:\kappa\geq 0\,.\end{cases}

Proof.

The desired lower bounds are obvious as
\mathcal{A}\geq 0
, and the first two upper bounds are immediate by combining Lemma
E.6
and Lemma
E.7
.
As
\mathcal{A}\geq 0
, it remains to upper bound
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[|\kappa^{\prime}|\mathcal{A}(V)\big]
, which we do similarly as in the proof of Lemma
E.6
.
We again break into the cases
\kappa\geq 0
,
\kappa<0
.

When
\kappa\geq 0
.

By Lemma
E.5
, we have
\mathcal{A}(x)\leq 2|x|+1
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
.
We thus may write

\displaystyle|\kappa^{\prime}|\mathcal{A}(V)

\displaystyle\leq 2|\kappa^{\prime}||V|+|\kappa^{\prime}|

\displaystyle\leq 2\Big(|\kappa^{\prime}|^{2}\sqrt{\frac{1+y}{\rho}}+\sqrt{y}|Z||\kappa^{\prime}|\Big)+|\kappa^{\prime}|

\displaystyle\leq 4(\kappa^{2}+x^{2}S^{2})\sqrt{2C_{\textsc{sol}}}+\frac{2|Z|+1}{2}\cdot\big(2(\kappa^{2}+x^{2}S^{2})+1\big)\,,

where we upper bounded
|\kappa^{\prime}|
by
\frac{\kappa^{\prime 2}+1}{2}
. Consequently as
S
,
Z
are independent,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[\big|\kappa^{\prime}\mathcal{A}(V)\big|\big]

\displaystyle\leq 5.7(\kappa^{2}+K_{\textsc{signal}})C_{\textsc{sol}}^{1/2}+2.6(\kappa^{2}+K_{\textsc{signal}})+2.3\leq K_{\textup{\ref{lem:controlexpectationofAW}}}\,.

When
\kappa<0
.

We write

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[|\kappa^{\prime}|\mathcal{A}(V)\big]

\displaystyle=\int_{s,z}|\kappa^{\prime}|\mathcal{A}(V)\varphi_{0}(z)\varphi_{1}(s)\,\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\int_{s:|s|<|\kappa|}\int_{z}|\kappa^{\prime}|\mathcal{A}(V)\varphi_{0}(z)\varphi_{1}(s)\,\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}s+\int_{s:|s|\geq|\kappa|}\int_{z}|\kappa^{\prime}|\mathcal{A}(V)\varphi_{0}(z)\varphi_{1}(s)\,\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}s\,.

Again, we upper bound each of these two integrals separately.

•

If
|s|<|\kappa|
, then as
|xs|<|\kappa|
and
\kappa^{\prime}<0
, we have
\kappa^{\prime}<0
.
Define the sets

\displaystyle E_{1}

\displaystyle:=\Big\{(s,z)\,:\,z\sqrt{y}\geq\frac{\kappa^{\prime}}{2}\sqrt{\frac{1+y}{\rho}}\Big\}\,,

\displaystyle E_{2}

\displaystyle:=\Big\{(s,z)\,:\,\kappa^{\prime}\sqrt{\frac{1+y}{\rho}}-1<z\sqrt{y}<\frac{\kappa^{\prime}}{2}\sqrt{\frac{1+y}{\rho}}\Big\}\,,

\displaystyle E_{3}

\displaystyle:=\Big\{(s,z)\,:\,z\sqrt{y}\leq\kappa^{\prime}\sqrt{\frac{1+y}{\rho}}-1\Big\}\,.

Note as
\kappa^{\prime}<0
and
y,\rho\geq 0
,
E_{1},E_{2},E_{3}
are all disjoint and partition
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
We thus can write

\displaystyle\int_{s:|s|<|\kappa|}\int_{z}|\kappa^{\prime}|\mathcal{A}(V)\varphi_{0}(z)\varphi_{1}(s)\,\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\textsc{(I)}+\textsc{(II)}+\textsc{(III)}\,,

where

(I)

\displaystyle:=\int_{(s,z)\in E_{1}:|s|<|\kappa|}|\kappa^{\prime}|\mathcal{A}(V)\varphi_{0}(z)\varphi_{1}(s)\,\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}s

(II)

\displaystyle:=\int_{(s,z)\in E_{2}:|s|<|\kappa|}|\kappa^{\prime}|\mathcal{A}(V)\varphi_{0}(z)\varphi_{1}(s)\,\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}s

(III)

\displaystyle:=\int_{(s,z)\in E_{3}:|s|<|\kappa|}|\kappa^{\prime}|\mathcal{A}(V)\varphi_{0}(z)\varphi_{1}(s)\,\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}s\,.

We now upper bound
\textsc{(I)},\textsc{(II)},\textsc{(III)}
:

1.

For
(I)
: by definition of
E_{1}
, for
(s,z)\in E_{1}
we have

\displaystyle V=\kappa^{\prime}\sqrt{\frac{1+y}{\rho}}-z\sqrt{y}\leq\frac{\kappa^{\prime}}{2}\sqrt{\frac{1+y}{\rho}}<0\,.

Hence
\mathcal{N}(V)\geq\frac{1}{2}
for
(s,z)\in E_{1}
, and moreover as
\kappa^{\prime}<0
, we have for
(s,z)\in E_{1}
that

V^{2}\geq\frac{|\kappa^{\prime}|^{2}}{4}\cdot\frac{1+y}{\rho}\geq\frac{|\kappa^{\prime}|^{2}}{4\rho}\geq\frac{|\kappa^{\prime}|^{2}}{4C_{\textsc{sol}}}\,.

Thus for
(s,z)\in E_{1}
,

\displaystyle 0\leq\mathcal{A}(V)\leq\frac{e^{-V^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}\mathcal{N}(V)}\leq\frac{2}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp\Big(-\frac{|\kappa^{\prime}|^{2}}{8C_{\textsc{sol}}}\Big)\,.

Thus,

\displaystyle\textsc{(I)}\leq\int_{(s,z)\in E_{1}:|s|<|\kappa|}\frac{2|\kappa^{\prime}|}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp\Big(-\frac{|\kappa^{\prime}|^{2}}{8C_{\textsc{sol}}}\Big)\varphi_{0}(z)\varphi_{1}(s)\,\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}s\leq\sqrt{C_{\textsc{sol}}}\,.

2.

For
(II)
: for
(s,z)\in E_{2}
, we have
V=\kappa^{\prime}\sqrt{\frac{1+y}{\rho}}-z\sqrt{y}\leq 1
. Therefore for
(s,z)\in E_{2}
,
\mathcal{A}(V)\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\mathcal{N}(1)}
. Furthermore, it is not possible in this case that
y=0
, as then we have
0=z\sqrt{y}<\frac{\kappa^{\prime}}{2}\sqrt{\frac{1+y}{\rho}}<0
as
\kappa^{\prime}<0
. Thus
y>0
and so

z\leq\frac{\kappa^{\prime}}{2}\sqrt{\frac{1+y}{y\rho}}<0\implies z^{2}\geq\frac{|\kappa^{\prime}|^{2}}{4}\cdot\frac{1+1/y}{\rho}\geq\frac{|\kappa^{\prime}|^{2}}{4C_{\textsc{sol}}}\,.

Therefore,

(II)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{(s,z)\in E_{2}:|s|<|\kappa|}|\kappa^{\prime}|\cdot\frac{1}{2\pi\mathcal{N}(1)}\exp\Big(-\frac{|\kappa^{\prime}|^{2}}{16C_{\textsc{sol}}}\Big)\exp(-z^{2}/4)\varphi_{1}(s)\,\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq 1.72\sqrt{C_{\textsc{sol}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp(-z^{2}/4)\varphi_{1}(s)\,\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}s\leq 2.5\sqrt{C_{\textsc{sol}}}\,.

3.

For
(III)
: by Lemma
E.5
and Lemma
E.7
,

\displaystyle 0\leq\mathcal{A}(V)

\displaystyle\leq 2|V|+1\leq 2|\kappa^{\prime}|\sqrt{\frac{1+y}{\rho}}+2|z|\sqrt{y}+1\leq 3|\kappa^{\prime}|\sqrt{C_{\textsc{sol}}}+2|z|+1\,.

Moreover, here we have as
\kappa^{\prime}\leq 0
and
\rho\leq C_{\textsc{sol}}
that

\displaystyle z\leq\kappa^{\prime}\sqrt{\frac{1+y}{y\rho}}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{y}}<0\implies z^{2}\geq|\kappa^{\prime}|^{2}\cdot\frac{1+y}{y\rho}+\frac{1}{y}\geq\frac{|\kappa^{\prime}|^{2}}{C_{\textsc{sol}}}\,.

Therefore,

(III)

\displaystyle\leq 3\sqrt{C_{\textsc{sol}}}\int_{(s,z)\in E_{3}:|s|<|\kappa|}|\kappa^{\prime}|^{2}\exp\Big(-\frac{|\kappa^{\prime}|^{2}}{4C_{\textsc{sol}}}\Big)\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp(-z^{2}/4)\varphi_{1}(s)\,\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\qquad+2\int_{(s,z)\in E_{3}:|s|<|\kappa|}|z||\kappa^{\prime}|\exp\Big(-\frac{|\kappa^{\prime}|^{2}}{4C_{\textsc{sol}}}\Big)\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp(-z^{2}/4)\varphi_{1}(s)\,\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{(s,z)\in E_{3}:|s|<|\kappa|}|\kappa^{\prime}|\exp\Big(-\frac{|\kappa^{\prime}|^{2}}{4C_{\textsc{sol}}}\Big)\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp(-z^{2}/4)\varphi_{1}(s)\,\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq 6.25C_{\textsc{sol}}^{3/2}+3.15C_{\textsc{sol}}^{1/2}\,.

Putting everything together and using that
C_{\textsc{sol}}\geq 1
, we obtain

\int_{s:|s|<|\kappa|}\int_{z}|\kappa^{\prime}|\mathcal{A}(V)\varphi_{0}(z)\varphi_{1}(s)\,\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}s=\textsc{(I)}+\textsc{(II)}+\textsc{(III)}\leq 13C_{\textsc{sol}}^{3/2}\,.

•

If
|s|\geq|\kappa|
, we obtain by Lemma
E.5
and Lemma
E.7
that

\displaystyle 0\leq\mathcal{A}(V)\leq 2V+1\leq 2|V|+1\leq 3|\kappa^{\prime}|\sqrt{C_{\textsc{sol}}}+2|z|+1\,,

thus by AM-GM,

\displaystyle|\kappa^{\prime}|\mathcal{A}(V)\leq\frac{|\kappa^{\prime}|^{2}}{2}+\frac{3}{2}\big(9|\kappa^{\prime}|^{2}C_{\textsc{sol}}+4|z|^{2}+1\big)

\displaystyle\leq|\kappa^{\prime}|^{2}(13.5C_{\textsc{sol}}+0.5)+6z^{2}+1.5

\displaystyle\leq(\kappa^{2}+s^{2})(27C_{\textsc{sol}}+1)+6z^{2}+1.5\,.

We obtain

\displaystyle\int_{s:|s|\geq|\kappa|}\int_{z}\mathcal{A}(V)^{2}\varphi_{0}(z)\varphi_{1}(s)\,\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad\leq(27C_{\textsc{sol}}+1)\kappa^{2}\int_{s:|s|\geq|\kappa|}\int_{z}\varphi_{0}(z)\varphi_{1}(s)\,\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}s+(27C_{\textsc{sol}}+1)\int_{s:|s|\geq|\kappa|}\int_{z}s^{2}\varphi_{0}(z)\varphi_{1}(s)\,\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\int_{s:|s|\geq|\kappa|}(6z^{2}+1.5)\varphi_{0}(z)\varphi_{1}(s)\,\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad\leq(27C_{\textsc{sol}}+1)\kappa^{2}\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}\Big(S^{2}>\kappa^{2}\Big)+(27C_{\textsc{sol}}+1)K_{\textsc{signal}}+7.5

\displaystyle\quad\leq(27C_{\textsc{sol}}+1)\kappa^{2}\exp\Big(-\frac{\kappa^{2}}{K_{\textsc{signal}}}\Big)+(27C_{\textsc{sol}}+1)K_{\textsc{signal}}+7.5

\displaystyle\quad\leq 1.5(27C_{\textsc{sol}}+1)K_{\textsc{signal}}+7.5\,,

where we used that
S^{2}
is
K_{\textsc{signal}}
sub-Exponential and
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[S^{2}]\leq K_{\textsc{signal}}
.

Summing the upper bounds from both cases and using that
C_{\textsc{sol}}\geq 1
, we obtain

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[|\kappa^{\prime}|\mathcal{A}(V)\big]\leq 42C_{\textsc{sol}}K_{\textsc{signal}}+13C_{\textsc{sol}}^{3/2}+7.5\,.

This proves the Lemma in both cases
\kappa<0
,
\kappa\geq 0
.
∎

Finishing the argument.

By Lemma
E.7
, to show (
E.8
) has a unique solution in the desired domain, it suffices to show (
E.8
) has a unique solution in
(y,\rho)\in[0,1]\times[\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}]
.
Now, we consider the signs of the derivatives of
G
in
y
and
\rho
. This is done for the interpolators and posterior together in a unified manner.

Lemma E.9
.

Suppose
(\alpha,\beta)\in[0,\alpha_{0}]\times[\beta_{0},\beta_{1}]
. For
(y,\rho)\in[0,1]\times[\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}]
, we have
\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial^{2}\rho}(y,\rho)<0
.

Proof.

We first establish the desired result for the interpolators. Consider (
E.14
), which states

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial^{2}\rho}(y,\rho)=-\frac{3\alpha(1+y)^{1/2}}{4\rho^{5/2}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\kappa^{\prime}\mathcal{A}(V)\Big]-\frac{\alpha(1+y)}{4\rho^{3}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\kappa^{\prime 2}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(V)]-\frac{1}{2\rho^{2}}\,.

The second and third terms in this expression are non-positive as
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\geq 0
by Lemma
E.5
, and the third term is strictly negative. Finally, note by Lemma
E.7
and Lemma
E.8
and since
\alpha\leq\frac{2}{3\sqrt{2C_{\textsc{sol}}}K_{\textup{\ref{lem:controlexpectationofAW}}}}
, we have

\displaystyle\Big|-\frac{3\alpha(1+y)^{1/2}}{4\rho^{5/2}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\kappa^{\prime}\mathcal{A}(V)\Big]\Big|\leq\frac{3\sqrt{2C_{\textsc{sol}}}\alpha}{4\rho^{2}}\cdot K_{\textup{\ref{lem:controlexpectationofAW}}}\leq\frac{1}{4\rho^{2}}\,.

It follows that

\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial\rho^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{4\rho^{2}}-\frac{1}{2\rho^{2}}=-\frac{1}{2\rho^{2}}<0\,.

For the posterior, we follow the same strategy. Recall (
E.20
) states that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial\rho^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha}{4}\Big(\textsc{(I)}+\textsc{(II)}+\textsc{(III)}+\textsc{(IV)}\Big)-\frac{1}{2\rho^{2}}\,,

where
\textsc{(I)},\textsc{(II)},\textsc{(III)},\textsc{(IV)}
are as defined in (
E.21
). As
1/(1+y),y/(1+y)\in[0,1]
for
y\in[0,1]
, we have
|\textsc{(I)}|,|\textsc{(II)}|,|\textsc{(III)}|,|\textsc{(IV)}|\leq K(D)
. Since
\rho\leq C_{\textsc{sol}}
and
\alpha
is small enough in terms of
D,C_{\textsc{sol}}
, it follows that
\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial\rho^{2}}<0
for the posterior as well.
∎

Lemma E.10
.

Suppose
(\alpha,\beta)\in[0,\alpha_{0}]\times[\beta_{0},\beta_{1}]
. Then for any
\rho^{\prime}\in[\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}]
, we have the following:

1.

There exists a unique
y^{\prime}\in[0,1]
such that
\frac{\partial G}{\partial y}(y^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})=0
.

2.

The one variable function
y\rightarrow G(y,\rho^{\prime})
attains its minimum on
[0,\infty)
at
y=y^{\prime}
.

3.

We have
\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial y^{2}}(y^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})>0
.

4.

Defining
y^{\prime}:=y^{\prime}(\rho^{\prime})
from 1),
y^{\prime}(\rho^{\prime})
is continuous in
\rho^{\prime}
for
\rho^{\prime}\in[\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}]
.

Proof.

We first prove 1), 2) of the Lemma.
Recall
g(y,\rho)
defined in (
E.15
) for the interpolators or (
E.22
) for the posterior.
In either case,
g(y,\rho)
is clearly jointly continuous in both arguments.
We claim that to prove 1), 2) of the Lemma, it suffices to show that
g(y,\rho^{\prime})=0
at a unique
y=y^{\prime}
, and that
g(y,\rho^{\prime})<0
for
y<y^{\prime}
,
g(y,\rho^{\prime})>0
for
y
such that
y^{\prime}<y\leq 1
.

To justify why, note by (
E.15
) or (
E.22
) that this implies
\frac{\partial G}{\partial y}(y,\rho^{\prime})=0
at
y=y^{\prime}
, and that
\frac{\partial G}{\partial y}(y,\rho^{\prime})<0
for
y<y^{\prime}
,
\frac{\partial G}{\partial y}(y,\rho^{\prime})>0
for
y^{\prime}<y\leq 1
. From here, we note that another
y^{\prime\prime}\in(1,\infty)
with
\frac{\partial G}{\partial y}(y^{\prime\prime},\rho^{\prime})=0
would contradict Lemma
E.7
. This would imply 1).
Moreover by continuity of
\frac{\partial G}{\partial y}(\cdot,\rho^{\prime})
and as
\frac{\partial G}{\partial y}(y,\rho^{\prime})>0
for
y^{\prime}<y\leq 1
, since there is no
y^{\prime\prime}\in(1,\infty)
with
\frac{\partial G}{\partial y}(y^{\prime\prime},\rho^{\prime})=0
, this would mean that
\frac{\partial G}{\partial y}(y^{\prime\prime},\rho^{\prime})>0
for all
y^{\prime\prime}\in(1,\infty)
. Together with the above, this would imply 2).

Now, we claim that
\frac{\partial g}{\partial y}(y,\rho^{\prime})>0
for all
y\in[0,1]
, for both the interpolators and the posterior:

•

For the interpolators: By (
E.16
),

\displaystyle\frac{\partial g}{\partial y}

\displaystyle=1-\frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{\rho(1+y)}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\kappa^{\prime}\mathcal{A}(V)\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(V)\Big]-\alpha\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\mathcal{A}^{\prime 2}(V)+\mathcal{A}(V)\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}(V)\Big]\,.

By Lemma
E.5
and Lemma
E.8
we have

\displaystyle\Big|\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\mathcal{A}^{\prime 2}(V)+\mathcal{A}(V)\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}(V)\Big]\Big|

\displaystyle\leq K_{\textsc{MILLS}}^{2}+K_{\textsc{MILLS}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\mathcal{A}(V)]\leq K_{\textsc{MILLS}}^{2}+K_{\textsc{MILLS}}K_{\textup{\ref{lem:controlexpectationofAW}}}\,,

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{1}{\sqrt{\rho(1+y)}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\kappa^{\prime}\mathcal{A}(V)\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(V)\Big]\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\textsc{sol}}^{1/2}K_{\textsc{MILLS}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|\kappa^{\prime}\mathcal{A}(V)\big|\Big]\leq C_{\textsc{sol}}^{1/2}K_{\textsc{MILLS}}K_{\textup{\ref{lem:controlexpectationofAW}}}\,.

Thus since
\alpha\leq\frac{1}{4}\min\big\{\frac{1}{K_{\textsc{MILLS}}^{2}+K_{\textsc{MILLS}}K_{\textup{\ref{lem:controlexpectationofAW}}}},\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}^{1/2}K_{\textsc{MILLS}}K_{\textup{\ref{lem:controlexpectationofAW}}}}\big\}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial g}{\partial y}

\displaystyle=1-\frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{\rho(1+y)}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\kappa^{\prime}\mathcal{A}(V)\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(V)\Big]-\alpha\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\mathcal{A}^{\prime 2}(V)+\mathcal{A}(V)\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}(V)\Big]\geq\frac{1}{2}>0\,.

(E.25)

•

For the posterior: Recall (
E.23
), that
y\geq 0
and
|u^{l}|\leq D
for
l=1,2,3,4
, and that
\rho^{\prime}\leq C_{\textsc{sol}}
,
\frac{1}{1+y}\leq 0
. Thus since
\alpha\leq\alpha_{0}
and
\alpha_{0}
is small enough in terms of
D,C_{\textsc{sol}}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial g}{\partial y}=1+2y-\frac{\alpha\rho}{(1+y)^{2}}\Big(\textsc{(I)}+\textsc{(II)}\Big)\geq\frac{1}{2}\,,

(E.26)

where
\textsc{(I)},\textsc{(II)}
are as in (
E.24
). We have
|\textsc{(I)}|,|\textsc{(II)}|\leq K(D)
as
|u^{l}|\leq D
for
l=1,2,3,4
.

Note that the bounds (
E.25
), (
E.26
) hold uniformly in
(y,\rho)\in[0,1]\times[\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}]
. This implies there is at most one
y^{\prime}\in[0,1]
such that
g(y^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})=0
. Moreover, if this
y^{\prime}
exists, our above work clearly implies
g(y)<0
for
y<y^{\prime}
,
g(y)>0
for
y>y^{\prime}
.

Now we show there exists such an
y^{\prime}\in[0,1]
. When
y=0
, evidently
g(y)\leq 0
. Now consider when
y=1
. For the interpolators, note as
\alpha<\frac{1}{K_{\textup{\ref{lem:controlexpectationofAW}}}}
, we have by Proposition
E.8
that

g(1)=1-\alpha\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\mathcal{A}(V)^{2}]>0.

For the posterior, since
|u^{\prime}|\leq D,\rho^{\prime}\leq C_{\textsc{sol}}
, and
\alpha
is small enough in terms of
D,C_{\textsc{sol}}
, we have

\displaystyle g(1)=2-\alpha\rho\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big\langle\exp u^{\prime}\big\rangle^{2}>0\,.

Thus for both the interpolators and the posterior, there exists
y^{\prime}\in[0,1]
with
g(y^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})=0
by the Intermediate Value Theorem. The above steps now prove that
g(y,\rho^{\prime})=0
at a unique
y=y^{\prime}
, and that
g(y,\rho^{\prime})<0
for
y<y^{\prime}
,
g(y,\rho^{\prime})>0
for
y
such that
y^{\prime}<y\leq 1
, which as discussed earlier implies 1), 2) of the Lemma.

Next, we prove 3) of the Lemma for both the interpolators and the posterior:

•

For the interpolators: Differentiating (
E.15
),

\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial y^{2}}(y,\rho^{\prime})=\frac{\frac{\partial g}{\partial y}(y,\rho^{\prime})(1+y)-g(y,\rho^{\prime})}{2(1+y)^{2}}\,.

Observe
g(y,\rho^{\prime})\leq y
. Also from the above steps, we have

\frac{\partial g}{\partial y}(y,\rho^{\prime})\geq 1-\alpha\max\big\{K_{\textsc{MILLS}}^{2}+K_{\textsc{MILLS}}K_{\textup{\ref{lem:controlexpectationofAW}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}^{1/2}K_{\textsc{MILLS}}K_{\textup{\ref{lem:controlexpectationofAW}}}\big\}\,.

It therefore remains to show

1-\alpha\max\big\{K_{\textsc{MILLS}}^{2}+K_{\textsc{MILLS}}K_{\textup{\ref{lem:controlexpectationofAW}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}^{1/2}K_{\textsc{MILLS}}K_{\textup{\ref{lem:controlexpectationofAW}}}\big\}>\frac{y^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}+1}.

Since
\frac{y}{y+1}
is increasing and as
y^{\prime}\leq 1
by 1), it suffices to show

1-\alpha\max\big\{K_{\textsc{MILLS}}^{2}+K_{\textsc{MILLS}}K_{\textup{\ref{lem:controlexpectationofAW}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}^{1/2}K_{\textsc{MILLS}}K_{\textup{\ref{lem:controlexpectationofAW}}}\big\}>\frac{2}{3}\,,

which holds true by our condition on
\alpha
.

•

For the posterior: Differentiating (
E.22
) and using (
E.23
),

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial y^{2}}(y,\rho^{\prime})=\frac{\frac{\partial g}{\partial y}(y,\rho^{\prime})(1+y)-2g(y)}{2(1+y)^{3}}=\frac{1+y+2\alpha\rho\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big\langle\exp u^{\prime}\big\rangle^{2}-\frac{\alpha\rho}{(1+y)^{2}}\big(\textsc{(I)}+\textsc{(II)}\big)}{2(1+y)^{3}}\,,

where
\textsc{(I)},\textsc{(II)}
are as in (
E.24
). Note
|\textsc{(I)}|,|\textsc{(II)}|\leq K(D)
as
|u^{l}|\leq D
for
l=1,2,3,4
. Thus as
y\geq 0,\rho\leq C_{\textsc{sol}}
, and
\alpha\leq\alpha_{0}
is small enough in terms of
D,C_{\textsc{sol}}
, we have
\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial y^{2}}(y^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})>0
.

This proves 3) of the Lemma in both cases.

Finally, we prove 4) of the Lemma. Note that
y(\rho^{\prime})
is a solution to the fixed point equation
g(y(\rho^{\prime}),\rho^{\prime})=0
. As
\frac{\partial g}{\partial y}>0
holds uniformly on
[0,1]\times[\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}]
as argued above, and as
y(\rho^{\prime})\in[0,1]
as shown in 1), we conclude 4) by the Implicit Function Theorem.
∎

The above Lemmas establish the desired convex-concave structure of the RS equations, and we are now ready to complete the proof of Proposition
A.3
.

Lemma E.11
.

Consider
(\alpha,\beta)\in[0,\alpha_{0}]\times[\beta_{0},\beta_{1}]
. Then there is at most one solution
(y,\rho)\in[0,1]\times[\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}]
to
(
E.8
)
.

Proof.

Suppose that there are two distinct solutions
(y_{1},\rho_{1})
and
(y_{2},\rho_{2})
both in the domain
[0,1]\times[\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}]
.
Assume without loss of generality that
y_{1}<y_{2}
.
Directly from Lemma
E.9
and Lemma
E.10
, we have
y_{1}\neq y_{2}
and
\rho_{1}\neq\rho_{2}
.
Applying Taylor Expansion to degree 2 about
(y_{1},\rho_{1})
, since
\frac{\partial G}{\partial\rho}(y_{1},\rho_{1})=0
by definition of the system of equations (
E.8
) and since
\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial\rho^{2}}(y_{1},\cdot)<0
from Lemma
E.9
, we see that
G(y_{1},\rho_{1})>G(y_{1},\rho_{2})
(since we Taylor expand about a
\rho^{\prime}\in[\min\{\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\},\max\{\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\}]\subseteq[\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}]
, we may apply the above work).
By Lemma
E.10
, since
\frac{\partial G}{\partial y}(y_{2},\rho_{2})=0
, we have
G(y_{1},\rho_{2})>G(y_{2},\rho_{2})
.
By analogous reasoning again using Lemma
E.9
, we have
G(y_{2},\rho_{2})>G(y_{2},\rho_{1})
.
Again by Lemma
E.10
, we have
G(y_{2},\rho_{1})>G(y_{1},\rho_{1})
.
Combining these inequalities, we obtain

G(y_{1},\rho_{1})>G(y_{1},\rho_{2})>G(y_{2},\rho_{2})>G(y_{2},\rho_{1})>G(y_{1},\rho_{1})\,,

a contradiction.
∎

Lemma E.12
.

Consider
(\alpha,\beta)\in[0,\alpha_{0}]\times[\beta_{0},\beta_{1}]
. Then there exists a solution
(y,\rho)\in[0,1]\times[\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}]
to
(
E.8
)
.

Proof.

By Lemma
E.10
and Lemma
E.7
, for any
\rho\in[\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}]
, there exists a unique
y(\rho)\in[0,1]
where
\frac{\partial G}{\partial y}(y(\rho),\rho)=0
. Thus it is enough to show there exists a
\rho^{\prime}\in[\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}]
such that
\frac{\partial G}{\partial\rho}(y(\rho^{\prime}),\rho^{\prime})=0
.

Let
h(\rho):=2\frac{\partial G}{\partial\rho}(y(\rho),\rho)
. By 4) of Lemma
E.10
and using the explicit form (
E.11
), (
E.18
) for
\frac{\partial G}{\partial\rho}
for the interpolators and posterior respectively,
h(\rho)
varies continuously in
\rho\in[\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}]
.

We now prove that
h(1.5/\beta)<0,h(1/3.5\beta)>0
for both the interpolators and the posterior.

•

For the interpolators: Observe that because
y(\rho)\leq 1
by Lemma
E.7
, Lemma
E.8
yields

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{(1+y(\rho))^{1/2}}{\rho^{3/2}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\kappa^{\prime}\mathcal{A}(V)\Big]\Big|\leq 2^{1/2}C_{\textsc{sol}}^{3/2}K_{\textup{\ref{lem:controlexpectationofAW}}}\,.

Now note by (
E.11
) and as
\alpha<\frac{0.9\beta_{0}}{C_{\textsc{sol}}^{3/2}K_{\textup{\ref{lem:controlexpectationofAW}}}}
, we have

h\Big(\frac{1.5}{\beta}\Big)=\frac{\alpha(1+y(\rho))^{1/2}}{\rho^{3/2}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\kappa^{\prime}\mathcal{A}(V)\Big]+\frac{2}{3}\beta-2\beta<\alpha 2^{1/2}C_{\textsc{sol}}^{3/2}K_{\textup{\ref{lem:controlexpectationofAW}}}+\frac{2}{3}\beta-2\beta<0\,.

Similarly we obtain

h\Big(\frac{1}{3.5\beta}\Big)\geq-\frac{4}{3}\beta+3.5\beta-2\beta>0\,.

•

For the posterior: Again
y(\rho)\leq 1
by Lemma
E.7
. Thus as
|u^{\prime}|,|u^{\prime\prime}|\leq D
,

\displaystyle\Big|\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big\langle u^{\prime\prime}+u^{\prime}\big\rangle-\frac{y}{1+y}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big\langle u^{\prime}\big\rangle^{2}\Big|\leq K(D)\,.

Thus as
\alpha
is small enough in terms of
\beta_{0},\beta_{1},D,C_{\textsc{sol}}
, by (
E.18
),

\displaystyle h\Big(\frac{1.5}{\beta}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\alpha K(D)+\frac{2}{3}\beta-2\beta<0\quad,\quad h\Big(\frac{1}{3.5\beta}\Big)\geq-\alpha K(D)+3.5\beta-2\beta>0\,.

Thus by Intermediate Value Theorem and continuity of
h(\rho)
, there is some
\rho^{\prime}\in[\frac{1}{3.5\beta},1.5\beta]
such that
h(\rho^{\prime})=0
, that is,
\frac{\partial G}{\partial\rho}(y(\rho^{\prime}),\rho^{\prime})=0
. By our definition of
C_{\textsc{sol}}
, we have
[\frac{1}{3.5\beta},\frac{1.5}{\beta}]\subset[\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}]
for all
\beta\in[\beta_{0},\beta_{1}]
. Hence
(y(\rho^{\prime}),\rho)
is a solution in
[0,1]\times[\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}]
to (
E.8
), and this proves the Lemma.
∎

Finally, recall that for
(\alpha,\beta)\in[0,\alpha_{0}]\times[\beta_{0},\beta_{1}]
, if there is a solution
(y,\rho)\in[0,\infty)\times[\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}]
, then this solution must be in
[0,1]\times[\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}]
by Lemma
E.7
. We conclude that (
E.8
) has a unique solution
(y,\rho)\in[0,\infty)\times[\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}]
(in particular, this solution must be in
[0,1]\times[\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}]
). By our initial remarks in this proof, it follows that (
A.11
) have a unique solution
(q,\rho)\in[0,C_{\textsc{sol}}]\times[\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}]
, as desired.
Finally, as this unique solution
(y,\rho)\in[0,1]\times[\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}]
, we have
\rho-q=\frac{\rho}{y+1}\geq\frac{1}{2C_{\textsc{sol}}}
.
This proves the first part of Proposition
A.3
.

We now prove the second part of Proposition
A.3
.
First, recall
\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial^{2}y}\big(y_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x),\rho_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)\big)
and
\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial^{2}\rho}\big(y_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x),\rho_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)\big)
are of opposite signs and nonzero by Lemma
E.9
and Lemma
E.10
. Thus the determinant of the following Jacobian is strictly negative:

\det\begin{bmatrix}\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial^{2}y}\big(y_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x),\rho_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)\big)&\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial y\partial\rho}\big(y_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x),\rho_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)\big)\\
\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial y\partial\rho}\big(y_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x),\rho_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)\big)&\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial^{2}\rho}\big(y_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x),\rho_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)\big)\end{bmatrix}<0~.

Since
G
is infinitely differentiable in
x,y,\rho
, and as
\big(y_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x),\rho_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)\big)
denotes the unique solution
(y,\rho)\in[0,\infty)\times[\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}]
to the system
\frac{\partial G}{\partial y}=\frac{\partial G}{\partial\rho}=0
, it follows by the Implicit Function Theorem that
y_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x),\rho_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)
are infinitely differentiable in
\alpha
,
\beta
and
x
. Hence as
y_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)\geq 0
, it follows that

q_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)=\frac{y_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)\rho_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)}{1+y_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)}

is also infinitely differentiable in
\alpha
,
\beta
and
x
.

E.3
Proof of Lemma
C.3

For this proof, we make the same transformation to
G(y,\rho)
we considered in Appendix
E.2
, where
G(y,\rho)
is defined as in (
E.7
) (again, here
I
is held fixed so
F\equiv F_{I}
).
We let
q_{0}=q_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)
,
\rho_{0}=\rho_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)
, and
y_{0}=y_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)=\frac{q_{0}}{\rho_{0}-q_{0}}
.

We first prove 1) of the Lemma.
Differentiating the relations
\frac{\partial G}{\partial\rho}(y_{0},\rho_{0})\equiv 0
,
\frac{\partial G}{\partial y}(y_{0},\rho_{0})\equiv 0
with respect to
\beta
(recall
y_{0},\rho_{0}
depend on
\beta
), the Chain Rule gives

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial\rho\partial y}(y_{0},\rho_{0})\cdot\frac{\partial y_{0}}{\partial\beta}+\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial\rho^{2}}(y_{0},\rho_{0})\cdot\frac{\partial\rho_{0}}{\partial\beta}+\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial\beta\partial\rho}(y_{0},\rho_{0})

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial y^{2}}(y_{0},\rho_{0})\cdot\frac{\partial y_{0}}{\partial\beta}+\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial\rho\partial y}(y_{0},\rho_{0})\cdot\frac{\partial\rho_{0}}{\partial\beta}+\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial\beta\partial y}(y_{0},\rho_{0})

\displaystyle=0\,,

where differentiability is justified by Proposition
A.3
.
Now identically as functions, we have
\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial\beta\partial\rho}=-1
,
\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial\beta\partial y}=0
by the definition of
G
in (
E.7
).
Multiplying the first relation above by
\frac{\partial\rho_{0}}{\partial\beta}
and the second relation above by
\frac{\partial y_{0}}{\partial\beta}
and subtracting, the
\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial\rho\partial y}
terms cancel and we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\rho_{0}}{\partial\beta}=\Big(\frac{\partial\rho_{0}}{\partial\beta}\Big)^{2}\cdot\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial\rho^{2}}(y_{0},\rho_{0})-\Big(\frac{\partial y_{0}}{\partial\beta}\Big)^{2}\cdot\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial y^{2}}(y_{0},\rho_{0})<0\,,

where Lemma
E.9
and Lemma
E.10
justify that
\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial\rho^{2}}(y_{0},\rho_{0})<0
,
\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial y^{2}}(y_{0},\rho_{0})>0
. This proves 1) of the Lemma.

Remark 3
.

Note that we only have
\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial\rho^{2}}(y_{0},\rho_{0})<0
,
\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial y^{2}}(y_{0},\rho_{0})>0

at the solution

y_{0},\rho_{0}
.

We next prove 2) of the Lemma.
To this end, we will show that
\rho_{0}(\alpha,1/4,x)>1-x^{2}
,
\rho_{0}(\alpha,9,x)<1-x^{2}
. The existence and uniqueness of such a
\beta=\beta(\alpha,x)\in[1/4,9]\subseteq[\beta_{0},\beta_{1}]
then follows by part 1) of this Lemma, and moreover this establishes that
\beta(\alpha,x)\in(1/4,9)
.

We first show that
\rho_{0}(\alpha,1/4,x)>1\geq 1-x^{2}
. Define

(I)

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\kappa^{\prime}\frac{(1+y_{0})^{1/2}}{\rho_{0}^{3/2}}\mathcal{A}(V)\Big]&:\text{ for interpolators}\,,\\
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big\langle u^{\prime\prime}(\bar{V})+u^{\prime}(\bar{V})^{2}\big\rangle-\frac{y}{1+y}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big\langle u^{\prime}(\bar{V})\big\rangle^{2}&:\text{ for posterior}\,,\end{cases}

(E.27)

where
V
is as in (
E.9
) and
\bar{V}
is as in (
E.17
), taken with arguments
\rho_{0},y_{0}
.
By (
E.11
) for the interpolators and (
E.18
) for the posterior, we have

\displaystyle 0=\frac{\alpha}{2}\textsc{(I)}+\frac{1}{2\rho_{0}}-\beta\,.

(E.28)

Now note

\displaystyle|\textsc{(I)}|\leq\begin{cases}\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\rho_{0}}K_{\textup{\ref{lem:controlexpectationofAW}}}C_{\textsc{sol}}^{1/2}&:\text{ for interpolators}\,,\\
2D^{2}+D&:\text{ for posterior}\,.\end{cases}

(E.29)

For the posterior, this simply uses that
|u^{\prime}|,|u^{\prime\prime}|\leq D
. For the interpolators, we use the proof of Proposition
A.3
, that
\rho_{0}\geq\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}}
,
y_{0}\geq 0
, and that
y_{0}\leq 1
by Lemma
E.7
. These facts combined with Lemma
E.8
imply the above bound on
\big|\textsc{(I)}\big|
.

Now as
\alpha\leq\alpha_{0}
and by our conditions on
\alpha_{0}
, we have by (
E.29
) that
\alpha|\textsc{(I)}|/2<\frac{1}{4\rho_{0}}
.
Combining with (
E.28
) now establishes
