\displaystyle\beta\in\Big(\frac{1}{4\rho_{0}},\frac{3}{4\rho_{0}}\Big)\implies\frac{1}{4\beta}<\rho_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)<\frac{3}{4\beta}\implies\rho_{0}(\alpha,1/4,x)>1\geq 1-x^{2}\,.

(E.30)

Next, we claim that
\rho_{0}(\alpha,9,x)<1-x^{2}
for
x\in[-1+\delta,1-\delta]
. To this end, (
E.28
), (
E.29
) imply that

\displaystyle 9\leq\frac{1}{2\rho_{0}(\alpha,9,x)}+\frac{\alpha}{2}|\textsc{(I)}|\implies\rho_{0}(\alpha,9,x)<1-x^{2}\,.

(E.31)

Here, the last inequality holds for
x\in[-1+\delta,1-\delta]
, using the definition of
\delta
in (
C.1
) for the interpolators or (
C.6
) for the posterior.
As mentioned earlier, (
E.30
), (
E.31
) yields 2) of the Lemma.

Now to prove 3) of the Lemma, for fixed
(\alpha,x)
, it follows from Proposition
A.3
that
f(\beta)
is infinitely differentiable in
\beta
.
Let
F(x,q,\rho)=\bar{\Phi}(x,q,\rho)-\beta(\rho+x^{2})
.
Note
\frac{\partial F}{\partial\rho}=\frac{\partial F_{I}}{\partial\rho}
,
\frac{\partial F}{\partial q}=\frac{\partial F_{I}}{\partial q}
, thus
\frac{\partial F}{\partial\rho}(x,q_{0},\rho_{0})=\frac{\partial F}{\partial q}(x,q_{0},\rho_{0})=0
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\beta}f(\beta)

\displaystyle=1-\big(\rho_{0}+x^{2}\big)+\frac{\partial F}{\partial\rho}(x,q_{0},\rho_{0})\cdot\frac{\partial\rho_{0}}{\partial\beta}+\frac{\partial F}{\partial q}(x,q_{0},\rho_{0})\cdot\frac{\partial q_{0}}{\partial\beta}=1-\big(\rho_{0}+x^{2}\big)\,.

Thus

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}\beta^{2}}f(\beta)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\partial\rho_{0}(\alpha,\beta,x)}{\partial\beta}\,.

By 1) of this Lemma,
\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}\beta^{2}}f(\beta)>0
on
[\beta_{0},\beta_{1}]
, and by 2) of this Lemma,
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\beta}f\big(\beta(\alpha,x)\big)=0
. Hence 3) of the Lemma follows.

Next, we prove 4) of the Lemma.
Since
\rho_{0}\big(\alpha,\beta(\alpha,x),x)=1-x^{2}
, we have

\displaystyle f\big(\beta(\alpha,x)\big)=\bar{\Phi}\big(x,q_{0}\big(\alpha,\beta(\alpha,x),x\big),1-x^{2}\big)\,.

Note as
x\in[-1+\delta,1-\delta]\subseteq[-17/18,17/18]
,
1\geq 1-x^{2}\geq\frac{1}{10}
. Thus
1-x^{2}\in\big[\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}\big]
as
C_{\textsc{sol}}\geq 10
.
Recalling the definition of
\bar{\Phi}
in (
5.4
), define the following function of
y
:

\displaystyle\bar{\Phi}^{\prime}(x,y,\rho):=\bar{\Phi}\Big(x,\frac{y\rho}{y+1},\rho\Big)\,.

Note
\frac{\partial\bar{\Phi}^{\prime}}{\partial y}\big(y_{0}\big(\alpha,\beta(\alpha,x),x\big),1-x^{2}\big)=\frac{\partial G_{I}}{\partial y}\big(y_{0}\big(\alpha,\beta(\alpha,x),x\big),\rho_{0}\big(\alpha,\beta(\alpha,x),x\big)\big)=0
.
Now as a direct consequence of the proof of Lemma
E.10
and by 2) of this Lemma,
\bar{\Phi}^{\prime}(x,y,1-x^{2})
attains its unique minimum on
[0,\infty)
at
y=y_{0}\big(\alpha,\beta(\alpha,x),x\big)
.
By monotonicity of the transformation
q=\frac{y\rho}{y+1}
, it follows that
\bar{\Phi}(x,q,1-x^{2})
attains a unique minimum on
[0,1-x^{2})
at
q=q_{0}\big(\alpha,\beta(\alpha,x),x\big)
.
Thus

\displaystyle f\big(\beta(\alpha,x)\big)=\inf_{q\in[0,1-x^{2})}\bar{\Phi}(x,q,1-x^{2})=\inf_{q\in[0,1)}\Phi(x,q)\,,

where the last step follows from the transformation
q\leftarrow\frac{q}{1-x^{2}}
. This proves 4) of the Lemma.

Finally, to prove 5) of the Lemma, we claim that
\beta(\alpha,x)
is a continuous function of
(\alpha,x)
.
To this end, note by (
E.11
) and 2), we know that
\beta(\alpha,x)
is the unique solution in
[\beta_{0},\beta_{1}]
to

\displaystyle\beta=\frac{\alpha}{2}\textsc{(I)}+\frac{1}{2(1-x^{2})}\,,

(E.32)

where
(I)
is taken with
\rho_{0}=1-x^{2}
.
Let
\bar{f}(\alpha,\beta,x)
denote the right hand side of (
E.32
); this is continuous in
(\alpha,\beta,x)
for
(\alpha,\beta,x)\in[0,\alpha_{0}]\times[\beta_{0},\beta_{1}]\times[-1+\delta,1-\delta]
by Proposition
A.3
.
Consider any sequence
(\alpha_{n},x_{n})\rightarrow(\alpha^{\star},x^{\star})
where
\alpha_{n}\in[0,\alpha_{0}]
,
x_{n}\in[-1+\delta,1-\delta]
.
Consider the corresponding
\beta_{n}:=\beta(\alpha_{n},x_{n})
.
Since
\beta_{n}\in[\beta_{0},\beta_{1}]
, it follows that we can extract a convergent subsequence.
Consider any convergent subsequence
\beta_{n_{k}}
, with limit
\beta^{\star}
.
By the above it follows by compactness that
\beta^{\star}\in[\beta_{0},\beta_{1}]
.
As
\bar{f}(\alpha,\beta,x)
is continuous, we have

\displaystyle\beta^{\star}=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\beta_{n_{k}}=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\bar{f}(\alpha_{n_{k}},\beta_{n_{k}},x_{n_{k}})=\bar{f}(\alpha^{\star},\beta^{\star},x^{\star})\,.

However as
(\alpha^{\star},x^{\star})\in[0,\alpha_{0}]\times[-1+\delta,1-\delta]
, the uniqueness of
\beta(\alpha,x)
in
[\beta_{0},\beta_{1}]
supplied by 2) implies that
\beta^{\star}=\beta(\alpha^{\star},x^{\star})
.
Therefore,
\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\beta(\alpha_{n_{k}},x_{n_{k}})=\beta^{\star}=\beta\big(\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}(\alpha_{n_{k}},x_{n_{k}})\big)
.
Since the convergent subsequence
\beta_{n_{k}}
is arbitrary, it follows that
\beta_{n}
converges to
\beta(\alpha^{\star},x^{\star})
.
Continuity of
\beta(\alpha,x)
follows.
Combining this claim with 4) and Proposition
A.3
now proves 5).
This concludes the proof of Lemma
C.3
.

E.4
Proof of Propositions
3.2
,
3.3
,
3.6
,
3.7
, and Lemma
3.8

Here we first prove these four Propositions and then prove Lemma
3.8
. We will prove these four Propositions together in a unified manner as follows. We show the following:

1.

There is at most one pair
(x,q)
satisfying
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\Phi(x,q)=\frac{\partial}{\partial q}\Phi(x,q)=0
.

2.

This implies that the saddle point problem is uniquely achieved.

Proof of Part
1
.

First, we show that any
(x,q)
satisfying
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\Phi(x,q)=0
,
\frac{\partial}{\partial q}\Phi(x,q)=0
must satisfy the relevant bivariate system of equations given in Propositions
3.2
,
3.3
,
3.6
,
3.7
respectively.
The proof follows from explicitly computing
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\Phi(x,q)
and
\frac{\partial}{\partial q}\Phi(x,q)
and applying Gaussian Integration by Parts to simplify the resulting expression. We discuss the proof for Propositions
3.2
,
3.3
for the interpolators; the proof for Propositions
3.6
,
3.7
for the posterior is identical.
Recalling the definition of
S
in (
3.5
) in the GMM case and (
3.6
) in the logistic case, we let

\displaystyle V:=\frac{\kappa-xS-\sqrt{(1-x^{2})q}Z}{\sqrt{(1-x^{2})(1-q)}}\,.

Explicitly differentiating
\Phi(x,q)
w.r.t.
q
and simplifying with Guassian Integration by Parts and the identity
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(V)=\mathcal{A}(V)^{2}-V\mathcal{A}(V)
gives

\displaystyle\frac{q}{1-q}

\displaystyle=\alpha\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\mathcal{A}(V)\Big\{\frac{\kappa-xS}{\sqrt{(1-q)(1-x^{2})}}-\frac{Z}{\sqrt{q(1-q)}}\Big\}\Big]

\displaystyle=\alpha\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\mathcal{A}(V)\Big\{V-\sqrt{\frac{1-q}{q}}Z\Big\}\Big]

\displaystyle=\alpha\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[\mathcal{A}(V)V+\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(V)\big]=\alpha\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[\mathcal{A}(V)^{2}\big].

Therefore, such
(x,q)
satisfying the stationary point equations must satisfy (
3.14
) in the GMM case or (
3.16
) in the logistic case.

We will now show the proof that such
(x,q)
satisfying the stationary point equations must satisfy (
3.15
) in the logistic case. The proof for (
3.13
) in the GMM case is similar (in fact simpler). We differentiate
\Phi(x,q)
w.r.t.
x
to obtain

\displaystyle x\sqrt{(1-q)(1-x^{2})}=\alpha\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\mathcal{A}(V)\big(S-\kappa x\big)\Big]=\alpha\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\mathcal{A}(V)\big(YG-\kappa x\big)\Big]\,,

(E.33)

where we used the explicit form
S=YG
from (
3.6
) (for the GMM case, we use
S=G+\sqrt{\lambda}
as per (
3.5
)).
We now simplify
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[\mathcal{A}(V)YG\big]
via Gaussian Integration by Parts to obtain

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\mathcal{A}(V)YG\Big]=-\frac{x}{\sqrt{(1-x^{2})(1-q)}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(V)\big]+\sqrt{\lambda}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\varphi\big(-\sqrt{\lambda}YG\big)\mathcal{A}(V)\Big]\,.

(E.34)

Similar calculations, using the identity
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(V)=\mathcal{A}(V)^{2}-V\mathcal{A}(V)
, yield

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(V)\big]

\displaystyle=(1-x^{2})(1-q)\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[\mathcal{A}(V)^{2}\big]-\kappa\sqrt{(1-x^{2})(1-q)}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[\mathcal{A}(V)\big]

(E.35)

\displaystyle+x\sqrt{\lambda(1-x^{2})(1-q)}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\varphi\big(-\sqrt{\lambda}YG\big)\mathcal{A}(V)\Big]\,.

Combining (
E.33
), (
E.34
), (
E.35
) and using the identity
\alpha\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[\mathcal{A}(V)^{2}\big]=\frac{q}{1-q}
and rearranging yields (
3.15
).
For Proposition
3.6
, this establishes (
3.24
), which directly implies (
3.25
) and proves 1). For Proposition
3.7
, 1) now follows from Proposition
3.8
. We now prove 1) for Propositions
3.2
,
3.3
for the interpolators.

To this end, our next step is to bound the value of
x,q
for solutions to
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\Phi(x,q)=0
,
\frac{\partial}{\partial q}\Phi(x,q)=0
. Specifically, we will prove the following Lemma.

Lemma E.13
.

For any solution
(x,q)
to
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\Phi(x,q)=0
,
\frac{\partial}{\partial q}\Phi(x,q)=0
, we have

\displaystyle 0\leq x,q

\displaystyle\leq\alpha K(\lambda)\,.

(E.36)

To prove Lemma
E.13
, we will need the following useful Lemma. Its proof is analogous to that of Lemma
E.6
and we omit it for brevity.

Lemma E.14
.

We have for all
0\leq x<1,0\leq q<1
that

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[\mathcal{A}(V)^{p}\big]\leq K\Big(\frac{\kappa_{+}+1}{\sqrt{(1-x^{2})(1-q)}}+\sqrt{\frac{q}{1-q}}+1\Big)^{p}

for
p\in\{1,2\}
, where
K>0
depends on
\lambda
.

Proof of Lemma
E.13
.

We now complete the proof of Lemma
E.13
. We start with the bound on
x
.
We apply Lemma
E.14
with
p=1
. First, note
x\geq 0
as when
x<0
, the LHS of (
3.15
), (
3.13
) is strictly negative while the RHS is non-negative. Noting that
\varphi\in[0,1]
and
\mathcal{A}\geq 0
, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{x}{\sqrt{1-x^{2}}}

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\sqrt{\lambda(1-q)}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[\mathcal{A}(V)\big]\leq\alpha\sqrt{\lambda(1-q)}\cdot K(\lambda)\Big(\frac{\kappa_{+}+1}{\sqrt{(1-x^{2})(1-q)}}+\sqrt{\frac{q}{1-q}}+1\Big)\,.

As
\alpha\leq\alpha_{0}(\lambda,\kappa_{+})
, we now use
0\leq q\leq 1
and multiply both sides by
\sqrt{1-x^{2}}
to obtain the bound in (
E.36
) for
x
.
Note as
\alpha\leq\alpha_{0}(\lambda,\kappa_{+})
, we have
x\leq\frac{1}{2}
.
We now prove the desired bound in (
E.36
) for
q
.
By Lemma
E.14
and (
3.14
) for the GMM case or (
3.16
) for the logistic case, we have

\displaystyle\frac{q}{1-q}\leq\alpha K(\lambda)\Big(\frac{\kappa_{+}^{2}+1}{(1-x^{2})(1-q)}+\frac{q}{1-q}+1\Big)\implies\frac{q}{1-q}\leq\frac{q}{2(1-q)}+\alpha K(\lambda)\Big(\frac{\kappa_{+}^{2}+1}{1-q}+1\Big)\,,

where we used
\alpha\leq\alpha_{0}(\lambda,\kappa_{+})
and
x\leq\frac{1}{2}
.
The above display now implies (
E.36
) for
q
.
∎

We now are in a position to finish the proof of 1). Let
f_{1}(x,q)
denote the
LHS
minus the
RHS
of (
3.13
) or (
3.15
) in the GMM case and the logistic case respectively. We similarly let
f_{2}(x,q)
denote the
LHS
minus the
RHS
of (
3.14
) or (
3.16
) in these two respective cases. We now establish the following Lemma
E.15
, which shows that all principal minors of the Jacobian of
\big(f_{1}(x,q),f_{2}(x,q)\big)
are strictly positive in the domain
(x,q)\in[0,\alpha K(\lambda)]^{2}
. Since any solution
(x,q)
must lie in
[0,\alpha K(\lambda)]^{2}
by Lemma
E.13
, injectivity of
\big(f_{1}(x,q),f_{2}(x,q)\big)
on
(x,q)\in[0,\alpha K(\lambda)]^{2}
and therefore 1) now follows from the Gale-Nikaido Theorem
[
GN65
]
.

Lemma E.15
.

We have for all
(x,q)\in[0,\alpha K(\lambda)]^{2}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x}f_{1}(x,q)\,,\,\frac{\partial}{\partial q}f_{2}(x,q)\,,\,\frac{\partial}{\partial x}f_{1}(x,q)\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial q}f_{2}(x,q)-\frac{\partial}{\partial q}f_{1}(x,q)\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial x}f_{2}(x,q)>0\,.

Proof of Lemma
E.15
.

Lemma
E.13
implies
x,q\leq 1/2
, thus we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\frac{x}{\sqrt{1-x^{2}}}\,,\,\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\frac{x}{1-x^{2}}\,,\,\frac{\partial}{\partial q}\frac{q}{1-q}\,,\,\frac{\partial}{\partial q}\frac{q}{(1-q)^{2}}\geq c>0\,,

where
c>0
is a universal constant.
Since
x,q\leq 1/2
,
\alpha\leq\alpha_{0}(\lambda,\kappa_{+})
, and
\varphi\in[0,1]
, it suffices to prove

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\Big|\mathcal{A}(V)\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(V)\frac{\partial V}{\partial x}\Big|\Big],\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\Big|\mathcal{A}(V)\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(V)\frac{\partial V}{\partial q}\Big|\Big],\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\Big|\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(V)\frac{\partial V}{\partial x}\Big|\Big],\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|\mathcal{A}(V)\big|+\Big|\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(V)\frac{\partial V}{\partial q}\Big|\Big]\leq K(\lambda,\kappa_{+})\,.

We note that the corresponding
\rho=1-x^{2}\in\big[\frac{1}{C_{\textsc{sol}}},C_{\textsc{sol}}\big]
and
y=\frac{q}{\rho-q}=\frac{q}{1-x^{2}-q}\leq 1
as
(x,q)\in[0,\alpha K(\lambda)]^{2}
. Therefore the conditions of Lemma
E.8
apply, giving
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[\big|(\kappa-Sx)\mathcal{A}(V)\big|\big]\leq K(\lambda,\kappa_{+})
.
Upon using Cauchy-Schwarz together with Lemma
E.14
to upper bound
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[\big|S\mathcal{A}(V)\big|\big]
, this implies that
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[\big|\kappa\mathcal{A}(V)\big|\big]\leq K(\lambda,\kappa_{+})
.
The upper bounds on the first two quantities above now follow as
|\mathcal{A}^{\prime}|\leq 1
,
0\leq x,q\leq\frac{1}{2}
, using the explicit forms of
\partial V/\partial x,\partial V/\partial q
, and applying the Cauchy-Schwarz Inequality together with Lemma
E.14
.

To prove bound the third and fourth quantities above, as
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(V)=\mathcal{A}(V)^{2}-V\mathcal{A}(V)
, we have
|\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(V)|\leq 3|V||\mathcal{A}(V)|+|\mathcal{A}(V)|
as
\mathcal{A}(V)\leq 2|V|+1
. Now, following the exact same proof as that of Lemma
E.8
and since
0\leq x,q\leq\frac{1}{2}
, we have
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[\big|\kappa^{\prime 2}\mathcal{A}(V)\big|\big]\leq K(\lambda,\kappa_{+})
. Using Cauchy-Schwarz and Lemma
E.14
to upper bound
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[S^{2}|\mathcal{A}(V)|\big]
, it follows that
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[|\kappa|^{2}|\mathcal{A}(V)|\big]\leq K(\lambda,\kappa_{+})
. Again applying Cauchy-Schwarz, this implies
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[\big|\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(V)\partial V/\partial x\big|\big]
,
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[\big|\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(V)\partial V/\partial q\big|\big]\leq K(\lambda,\kappa_{+})
. Lemma
E.15
now follows.
∎

Proof of Part
2
.

We now complete the proof of Propositions
3.2
,
3.3
,
3.6
,
3.7
.
Define the function
f(x):=\inf_{q\in[0,1)}\Phi(x,q)
.
Note
f(x)
is the infimum of an arbitrary family of continuous functions, and therefore is upper semicontinuous.
First notice for the posterior, as
u(x)\geq-D(\lambda)(|x|+1)
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\log\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\exp u\Big(xS+\sqrt{(1-x^{2})q}Z+\sqrt{(1-x^{2})(1-q)}W\Big)

(E.37)

\displaystyle\geq-D(\lambda)\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[1+\big|xS+\sqrt{(1-x^{2})q}Z+\sqrt{(1-x^{2})(1-q)}W\big|\Big]\geq-K(\lambda)\,.

For the interpolators, using that
\mathcal{A}(x)\leq 2x+1
for all
x\geq 0
by Lemma
E.5
and that
-\log\mathcal{N}(V)\leq\log 2
when
V\leq 0
, we obtain
-\log\mathcal{N}(V)\leq K(V^{2}_{+}+1)
for all
V\geq 0
where
V_{+}=\max\{V,0\}
.
Since
(a+b)_{+}\leq b_{+}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\log\mathcal{N}(V)\geq-K\cdot\frac{\kappa_{+}^{2}+x^{2}S^{2}+(1-x^{2})qZ^{2}}{(1-x^{2})(1-q)}\,.

(E.38)

Thus for
\alpha\leq\alpha_{0}(\lambda,\kappa_{+})
, we have for both the posterior and the interpolators,

\displaystyle\Phi(x,q)\geq\frac{q}{4(1-q)}-\frac{\alpha K(\lambda)(\kappa_{+}^{2}+1)}{(1-x^{2})(1-q)}+\frac{1}{2}\log(1-x^{2})+\frac{1}{2}\log(1-q)-\alpha K(\lambda)

(E.39)

Taking
x=0
in the above display, bounding
\alpha K(\lambda)(\kappa_{+}^{2}+1)\leq 1/8
, and noting the infimum of the resulting lower bound is attained at
q=1/2
now proves that
f(0)\geq-K(\lambda)
.

Next, notice
f(x)\leq\Phi(x,0)
. Note for the interpolators or the posterior in the logistic case, we have
u\leq 0
and therefore
\Phi(x,0)\leq\frac{1}{2}\log(1-x^{2})
. For the posterior in the GMM case, we have
u(x)=\sqrt{\lambda}x
and therefore

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\log\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\exp u\Big(xS+\sqrt{(1-x^{2})q}Z+\sqrt{(1-x^{2})(1-q)}W\Big)

(E.40)

\displaystyle\leq\log\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\exp\sqrt{\lambda}\Big(xS+\sqrt{(1-x^{2})q}Z+\sqrt{(1-x^{2})(1-q)}W\Big)\leq K(\lambda)\,.

Thus in all cases, we have
\lim_{x\rightarrow\pm 1}\Phi(x,0)=-\infty
, and justifying the existence of the following limit and the equality

\displaystyle\lim_{x\rightarrow\pm 1}f(x)=-\infty\,.

The above derivation also proves that
f(0)\leq\Phi(0,0)\leq K(\lambda)
. Now as
f(0)\geq-K(\lambda)
and
\lim_{x\rightarrow\pm 1}f(x)=-\infty
, by the upper semicontinuity of
f
and as
f(0)\leq K(\lambda)
, the Weierstrass Extreme Value theorem implies that
\sup_{x\in(-1,1)}f(x)
is attained by at least one
x^{\star}\in[-1,1]
and that all such
x^{\star}
must lie in
[-1+\delta(\lambda),1-\delta(\lambda)]
.

Consider any such
x^{\star}
. As
x^{\star}\in[-1+\delta(\lambda),1-\delta(\lambda)]
and
\Phi(x^{\star},q)
is continuous in
q
, (
E.39
) implies that
\inf_{q\in[0,1)}\Phi(x^{\star},q)
is attained at
q\in[0,1-\delta^{\prime}(\lambda)]
, since the
RHS
of (
E.39
) goes to
+\infty
as
q\rightarrow 1
and as
\alpha\leq\alpha_{0}(\lambda,\kappa_{+})
.
Thus
\sup_{x\in(-1,1)}\inf_{q\in[0,1)}\Phi(x,q)
is attained by at least one
(x^{\star},q^{\star})
.

Now consider any
x\in(-1,1)
. For the interpolators, we explicitly compute using Gaussian Integration by Parts,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial q}\Phi(x,0)=-\frac{\alpha}{2}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[\mathcal{A}(V)^{2}\big]<0\,.

For the posterior, a similar calculation gives

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial q}\Phi(x,0)=-\frac{\alpha(1-x^{2})}{2}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\Big(\frac{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}[u^{\prime}\cdot\exp u]}{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}[\exp u]}\Big)^{2}\Big]<0\,.

It follows that all such
q^{\star}>0
, and therefore any such
(x^{\star},q^{\star})\in(-1,1)\times(0,1)
. Hence any such
(x^{\star},q^{\star})
solves the system
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\Phi(x,q)=0
,
\frac{\partial}{\partial q}\Phi(x,q)=0
. By 1), it follows that
(x^{\star},q^{\star})
is unique and satisfies
(x_{\star},q_{\star})\in[0,K(\lambda)\alpha]^{2}
– for the posterior these bounds follow directly (GMM) or by Lemma
3.8
(logistic).
This completes the proof.

Proof of Lemma
3.8
.

Let

G\sim N(0,1)\,,\qquad Y|G\sim\text{Rad}\big(\varphi(\sqrt{\lambda}G)\big)\,,\qquad\eta:=\varphi(-\sqrt{\lambda}YG)\,.

(E.41)

Recall the system of equations

\displaystyle\alpha\lambda(1-q)\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[\varphi\big(-\sqrt{\lambda}YG\big)R(x,q)\big]=\frac{x}{1-x^{2}}\,,~~\mbox{and}~~\alpha\lambda(1-x^{2})\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\big[R(x,q)^{2}\big]=\frac{q}{(1-q)^{2}}\,,

(E.42)

where

\displaystyle R(x,q):=\frac{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\varphi^{\prime}(\sqrt{\lambda}V)}{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\varphi(\sqrt{\lambda}V)}\,,~~V:=xYG+\sqrt{(1-x^{2})q}Z+\sqrt{(1-x^{2})(1-q)}W\,,

(E.43)

and
G,Z,W\sim N(0,1)
are mutually independent.
We wish to show that for
\alpha
small enough this system has a unique solution which takes the form
(x,q)=(s/(1+s),s/(1+2s))
.
Consider the parametrized curve

x_{s}:=\frac{s}{1+s}\,,\qquad q_{s}:=\frac{s}{1+2s}\,,\qquad s\geq 0\,,

which observes the Nishimori identity

\frac{q_{s}}{1-q_{s}}=x_{s}\,,

and

1-x_{s}^{2}=\frac{1+2s}{(1+s)^{2}}\,,\qquad\sqrt{(1-x_{s}^{2})q_{s}}=\frac{\sqrt{s}}{1+s}\,,\qquad\sqrt{(1-x_{s}^{2})(1-q_{s})}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+s}}\,.

(E.44)

We consider the auxiliary observation model

\bar{G}=\sqrt{s}\,G+W\,,\qquad W\sim N(0,1)\,,

with
W
independent of
(G,Y)
. Conditionally on
\bar{G}
,
the posterior law of
G
before observing
Y
is Gaussian with mean

\frac{\sqrt{s}}{1+s}\bar{G}
and variance
\frac{1}{1+s}
.
Since

\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}(Y=y\,|\,G)=\varphi(\sqrt{\lambda}yG)\,,\qquad y\in\{-1,+1\}\,,

Bayes’ formula gives

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\eta\,|\,\bar{G},Y]=\frac{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\varphi^{\prime}\!\left(\sqrt{\lambda}Y\left(\frac{\sqrt{s}}{1+s}\bar{G}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+s}}W\right)\right)}{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\varphi\!\left(\sqrt{\lambda}Y\left(\frac{\sqrt{s}}{1+s}\bar{G}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+s}}W\right)\right)}=R(x_{s},q_{s})\,.

(E.45)

Here we used the identity
\varphi(t)\varphi(-t)=\varphi^{\prime}(t)

and symmetry of the Gaussian distribution, together with the equalities displayed in (
E.44
).
Consequently by the tower property,

\mathbb{E}[\eta R(x_{s},q_{s})]=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\eta\mid\bar{G},Y]^{2}\right]=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[R(x_{s},q_{s})^{2}].

(E.46)

Now define

C(s):=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[R(x_{s},q_{s})^{2}]\,.

Using (
E.46
), the system of equations (
E.42
) restricted to the curve

s\mapsto(x_{s},q_{s})
become

\alpha\lambda(1-q_{s})C(s)=\frac{x_{s}}{1-x_{s}^{2}}\,,\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\alpha\lambda(1-x_{s}^{2})C(s)=\frac{q_{s}}{(1-q_{s})^{2}}\,.

Both are equivalent to

s=\alpha\lambda C(s)\,.

(E.47)

We show that for
\alpha
small enough, there is a unique solution which must lie on this curve.
Let

A(x,q):=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\eta R(x,q)]\,,\qquad B(x,q):=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[R(x,q)^{2}]\,.

Since
0<\varphi<1
and
0<\varphi^{\prime}<\varphi
,
A,B,C\in(0,1)
.
Therefore any solution of (
E.42
) satisfies

\frac{x}{1-x^{2}}\leq\alpha\lambda\,,\qquad\frac{q}{(1-q)^{2}}\leq\alpha\lambda\,.

In particular,

x\leq\alpha\lambda\,,\qquad q\leq\alpha\lambda\,.

(E.48)

Thus every solution lies in an arbitrarily small neighborhood of
(0,0)
once
\alpha
is sufficiently small.
We now use local uniqueness. Let

H(x):=\frac{2x}{1+\sqrt{1+4x^{2}}}\,,\qquad K(q):=\frac{\sqrt{1+4q}-1}{\sqrt{1+4q}+1}\,,

the inverses of
x\mapsto x/(1-x^{2})
on
[0,1)
and
q\mapsto q/(1-q)^{2}
on
[0,1)
, respectively. Hence the system is equivalent to
the fixed-point equation

(x,q)=T_{\alpha}(x,q)\,,

where

T_{\alpha}(x,q):=\left(H\!\left(\alpha\lambda(1-q)A(x,q)\right),K\!\left(\alpha\lambda(1-x^{2})B(x,q)\right)\right).

(E.49)

The functions
A
and
B
are locally Lipschitz in a neighborhood of
(0,0)
.
Indeed, after conditioning on
(G,Y)
, the remaining average (with respect to
Z
, Eq. (
E.43
)) is a Gaussian
heat semigroup applied to the smooth function

u\mapsto\frac{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\varphi^{\prime}(\sqrt{\lambda}(u+\sigma W))}{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{W}\varphi(\sqrt{\lambda}(u+\sigma W))}\,,

(and its square) with
\sigma
bounded away from
0
near
(x,q)=(0,0)
. Differentiation under the Gaussian integral is justified by the boundedness and exponential decay properties of the derivatives of the logistic function. Hence there exist

\delta>0
and
L<\infty
, depending only on
\lambda
, such that
A
and
B

are
L
-Lipschitz on
[0,\delta]^{2}
.

Since
H
and
K
have bounded derivatives on bounded intervals, it follows
that, for
(x,q),(x^{\prime},q^{\prime})\in[0,\delta]^{2}
,

\|T_{\alpha}(x,q)-T_{\alpha}(x^{\prime},q^{\prime})\|_{\infty}\leq C_{\lambda}\alpha\|(x,q)-(x^{\prime},q^{\prime})\|_{\infty}

for some finite constant
C_{\lambda}
. Choosing
\alpha_{0}>0
sufficiently small,
we may ensure that
C_{\lambda}\alpha_{0}<1
and
\alpha_{0}\lambda<\delta
.
Then
T_{\alpha}
is a contraction on
[0,\delta]^{2}
for every

\alpha\in(0,\alpha_{0})
.
By (
E.48
), every solution of the system (
E.42
) lies in
[0,\delta]^{2}
for

\alpha<\alpha_{0}
. Hence the system has at most one solution.

On the other hand, the scalar equation (
E.47
) has a solution for small
\alpha
. Indeed,
0<C(s)<1
and
C
is continuous near
0
, while

s-\alpha\lambda C(s)

is negative at
s=0
and positive at
s=\alpha\lambda
for
\alpha
small
enough. Moreover, by the same Lipschitz argument, the map

s\mapsto\alpha\lambda C(s)
is a contraction on a small interval, so this
solution is unique.

Thus the unique small-
\alpha
solution of the two-dimensional system lies on
the curve

(x,q)=\left(\frac{s}{1+s},\frac{s}{1+2s}\right)\,,

and satisfies
s=\alpha\lambda C(s)
. This proves the claim.
∎

Appendix F
Additional technical results

Lemma F.1

(Lemma 3.2.2,
[
Tal10
]
)
.

Consider a real
c>0
and a univariate concave function
w(y)
such that
w^{\prime\prime}\leq-c<0
. Let
y^{\prime}
be the solution to
w^{\prime}(y^{\prime})=0
. Then we have

\displaystyle c\int(y-y^{\prime})^{2}\exp w(y)\,\mathrm{d}y\leq\int\exp w(y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,.

F.1
Truncated Logarithm

Lemma F.2

(Lemma 8.3.7,
[
Tal11
]
)
.

For
0\leq x,z\leq 1
, we have

\Big|\log_{A}x-\log_{A}z\Big|\leq\Big|\log_{A}\Big(\frac{x}{z}\Big)\Big|\,.

Proof.

Suppose
z\leq x
. The result is clear if
x\leq\exp(-A)
, when the left hand side is 0, or if
z\geq\exp(-A)
, where the desired inequality follows from definition of logarithms. If
z\leq\exp(-A)\leq x
, as here
\frac{x}{z}\geq 1
and
x,z\leq 1
, we obtain

|\log_{A}x-\log_{A}z|\leq|\log x-\log z|=\Big|\log\frac{x}{z}\Big|=\Big|\log_{A}\frac{x}{z}\Big|\,.

Else, suppose
x\leq z
. By similar arguments as above, the result is clear if
z\leq\exp(-A)
or if
x\geq\exp(-A)
, where we now note
x\geq\exp(-A)\geq z\exp(-A)
in the latter case. If
x\leq\exp(-A)\leq z
, then noting
\log z\in[-A,0]
,
\log x\leq-A
, and
x,z\leq 1
, we obtain

|\log_{A}x-\log_{A}z|=|-A-\log z|\leq A\,,\,|\log_{A}x-\log_{A}z|\leq|\log x-\log z|=\Big|\log\frac{x}{z}\Big|\,.

Therefore, we obtain

|\log_{A}x-\log_{A}z|\leq\min\Big\{A,\Big|\log\frac{x}{z}\Big|\Big\}=\Big|\log_{A}\frac{x}{z}\Big|\,

in this case as well.
∎

Lemma F.3

(Lemma 8.3.10,
[
Tal11
]
)
.

If
0\leq x,y,z\leq 1
, we have

|\log_{A}xz-\log_{A}yz|\leq|\log_{A}x-\log_{A}y|\mathbf{1}\{z\geq\exp(-A)\}\,.

Lemma F.4

(Lemma 8.3.11,
[
Tal11
]
)
.

If
0<y\leq x\leq 1
, then for any
c>0
,

|\log_{A}x-\log_{A}y|\leq|\log_{A}y|\mathbf{1}\{y\leq c\}+\frac{|x-y|}{c}\,.
